\title[An Obstruction Ledger and a Pick Reformulation of RH]{The Riemann Hypothesis:
An Obstruction Ledger and an Arithmetic Pick Reformulation\\[0.3em]
\normalsize\textmd{Obstructions, a common boundary quantifier, and a chain ending at the Li--Keiper
criterion}}

\author{David Alejandro Trejo Pizzo}
\thanks{Independent Researcher. \texttt{dtrejopizzo@gmail.com}}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
This paper develops an obstruction ledger for several spectral, arithmetic, adelic, and
positivity-based approaches to the Riemann Hypothesis; identifies a recurring uniform-boundary quantifier
(the passage from average/almost-all/interior to specific/uniform/boundary) as their common failure mode;
and constructs an arithmetic Pick/Nevanlinna route whose analytic part is proved (two of its Steps
conditionally on the open input) and whose sole
arithmetic input is shown to terminate exactly at Li--Keiper positivity.

The first part is the ledger: the concrete no-gos that closed each route, distilled into a small number
of structural walls --- arithmetic propagation, wrong-sign positivity, infinite-dimensional
Hasse--Minkowski failure, per-place indefiniteness, the cohomological-Frobenius wall,
sign-versus-magnitude, and the uniform-norm (rank-one escape) wall --- each carrying either an explicit logical
classification (exact equivalence with RH, framework closure obstruction, or insufficiency theorem for a
stated input class) or an explicit label as heuristic or observation. The standard is strict throughout: no assertion is treated as proved because it has
been named, defined, or made plausible by computation, and statements that hold only in a weaker or
conditional form are stated in that form.

The second part is the architecture those walls shaped: the \emph{arithmetic Pick positivity} (ARP-P)
chain, fifteen Steps establishing the equivalence $\textnormal{ARP-P}\Longleftrightarrow\textnormal{RH}$.
Fourteen of the fifteen Steps are proved in the precise sense recorded in the dependency map (two of them
conditionally on the open input). The remaining Step~5 is equivalent to Li's criterion $\lambda_n\ge0$
for all $n$, hence to RH; the chain is therefore a reformulation of RH, not progress toward it. The
internal reduction of Step~5 terminates at one terminal-defect inequality,
\[
   \delta_N\ge0\ \text{for all }N\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \textnormal{RH}\qquad(\Omega_7),
\]
equivalently $\lambda_n\ge0$ for all $n$. $\Omega_7$ is not our discovery --- it is the classical Li
criterion; the contribution is that an independently constructed architecture, after the analysis of
its difficulty, terminates exactly there, and the ledger explains why the major routes considered here
repeatedly return to RH-equivalent boundary or uniformity conditions. The paper does not prove
RH and does not present the open input as a small gap: being equivalent to RH, it carries the full
difficulty of the Hypothesis.

Inside $\Omega_7$, the direct arithmetic route is carried one step farther.  An unconditional
prime-number-theorem tail estimate (A0) and exact pole--prime--Gamma bookkeeping
reduce the whole forward implication to one compact signed inequality (A1): a single Chebyshev-discrepancy
integral against $e^{-u}L_{n-1}^{(2)}(u)$ with an explicit archimedean reserve of order
$\frac38n\log n$.  Every implication from A1 to Li positivity and hence to RH is proved; A1 itself
remains open.  A finite range of A1 is checked by interval arithmetic, partly conditionally on an
unproved coefficient-tail bound, and these numbers are not certified.  A1 implies $\lambda_n>0$ for all $n$, so it is at least as strong as RH, and the strengthened sufficient forms are at least as strong as the classical Li criterion.  This refinement reformulates the terminal inequality; it does not prove it.
\end{abstract}

\tableofcontents
\newpage

\section{Prologue}\label{sec:prologue}

\paragraph{What this paper claims.}  Four things: an obstruction ledger for the major spectral,
arithmetic, adelic, and positivity-based routes considered here, each obstruction carrying an
explicit logical classification (\S\ref{sec:walls}); a finite-probe signature architecture (the sourced
determinant and its Pick kernel); the ARP-P equivalence framework, whose analytic bridge (Steps 6--15) is proved and which restates RH in Nevanlinna form
(\S\ref{sec:status-map}); and the localization of that framework's single unresolved arithmetic input at
classical Li--Keiper positivity.

\paragraph{What this paper does not claim.}  It does not prove the Riemann Hypothesis.  It does not
present $\Omega_7$ as a new criterion for RH --- $\Omega_7$ is the classical Li criterion, reached here as
the terminal statement of an independently constructed architecture.  And it does not describe the
unresolved input as a ``small gap'': since $\Omega_7$ is equivalent to RH, its being unproved carries
the full difficulty of the Hypothesis.

\paragraph{How the paper is organized.}  The fifteen-step implication chain, the H0--H8 internal analysis of
Step~5, and the $\Omega_1$--$\Omega_7$ difficulty-localization chain are three distinct logical levels,
kept typed and separate throughout; their dependency maps appear in \S\ref{sec:status-map},
and the provenance of every claimed contribution is listed at the end of this Prologue.  Two statements that
might be expected are recorded as not used, with their correct forms (\S\ref{sec:withdrawn}).

Three further statements are not used, and their correct forms are given: the compactness extension
of H2, an unconditional tail domination for the explicit H6 witness, and the
stationary-phase separation of the divergent series $\sum_\rho(1-1/\rho)^n$.  It also records the Castelnuovo sign mismatch and
states the sampling and de Bruijn--Newman cascade laws only under their missing uniform estimates.  The
correct scoped statements appear at the original locations, so none of these assertions is used as a
lemma in the terminal chain.

\medskip

The path of Part~II is short to state and reformulates the Hypothesis as a single inequality; but that
one inequality, $\Omega_7$, remains open, and it is itself equivalent to RH. The paper is therefore
presented in two parts: the obstructions met by the other routes considered (Part~I), and the
reduction those obstructions shaped, with its one open input marked (Part~II).

Several of the routes of Part~I start from the arithmetic statistics of $\omega(n)$, the number of
distinct prime factors, from the fluctuations of the von Mangoldt function in short intervals, and from
localized Weil quadratic forms. Throughout, reformulations of positivity are tested against the
Davenport--Heilbronn function --- a function that satisfies a functional equation of the same type, $\xi_{\mathrm{DH}}(s)=\xi_{\mathrm{DH}}(1-s)$ with $\xi_{\mathrm{DH}}(s)=(\pi/5)^{-s/2}\Gamma(\tfrac{s+1}2)L_{\mathrm{DH}}(s)$, and has no Euler product; it has zeros off the critical line and therefore serves as a
falsifier: any argument that ``proves'' the Riemann Hypothesis but does not distinguish $\zeta$ from
Davenport--Heilbronn is, by that very fact, wrong.

The routes of Part~I meet one recurring obstruction, which we call the
\emph{symmetry/positivity wall}: the location of the zeros is a constraint that is global, one-sided (an
upper bound, not a two-sided equality), and orthogonal to every symmetry the problem hands you for free.
Positivity --- the fact that $\Lambda(n)\ge0$, that the von Mangoldt measure is a genuine measure ---
was always available, but positivity controls \emph{sign}, and the Riemann Hypothesis is about
\emph{magnitude}; at several levels (operator norm, Carleson measure, diagonal of a kernel, derivative of
a spectral shift) we observe that the positivity-based arguments considered do not cross the gap.  This
is a heuristic description, not a theorem.

The change of viewpoint behind Part~II is not a new estimate but a new \emph{object}. A scalar
determinant cannot see the difference between a self-adjoint operator and one with complex eigenvalues:
its zeros are its zeros, wherever they sit. But a determinant equipped with its \emph{response to
finite-rank probes} --- a \emph{sourced} determinant --- can. Its second variation in the probe directions
reconstructs a two-variable Pick kernel. By the theory of generalized Nevanlinna functions
(M.~G.~Kre\u{\i}n and H.~Langer, Math.\ Nachr.\ 77 (1977)), if the Pick kernel of a function meromorphic in
$\C\setminus\R$ has finitely many, say $\kappa$, negative squares, the function has at most $\kappa$ poles
in the open upper half-plane, and $\kappa=0$ means it is a Nevanlinna function, with no non-real poles.
The idea is to work not with the scalar determinant but with this signature kernel, which is not blind
to the signature.

The second idea: the one genuinely divergent direction in the
problem --- the pole of $\zeta$ at $s=1$, which contributes a trace growing like $\tfrac12(\log P)^2$ ---
must be removed by \emph{shorting}, the Schur-complement operation, and never by subtraction. The
distinction sounds pedantic until one writes down the $2\times2$ example
$\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}R&\sqrt R\\\sqrt R&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\succeq0$ and watches subtraction of
the divergent corner manufacture a spurious negative eigenvalue while the Schur complement leaves the
matrix positive. The divergent pole is harmless if shorted and catastrophic if subtracted.

The third idea supplies a conditional realization route.  If, after shorting the pole, the surviving
objects $G_P^\circ(z)$ are identified with compressed resolvents of genuine self-adjoint operators, then
a compressed self-adjoint resolvent is bounded by $1/|\Im z|$ ---
uniformly, with the divergence safely quarantined in the discarded corner. A uniformly bounded family of
holomorphic functions is a normal family, and a normal family cannot converge to a function with a pole.
If these resolvents also converge to the right object \emph{anywhere on an open set}, Vitali's theorem
carries the convergence across the strip and forbids the off-axis poles --- which are exactly the
off-critical zeros. Thus, if $G_P^\circ$ were compressed resolvents of self-adjoint operators converging
on an open subset of $\Re s>1$, Vitali's theorem would exclude off-line zeros; both the operator
identification and the convergence are open, they form part of Step~5, and neither is a consequence of
$\Lambda(n)\ge0$ alone.

\paragraph{Scope.}
We present this paper as a ledger of obstructions together with a \emph{reformulation}, not as a proof. The path (Part~II, \S\ref{sec:live-proof-steps}) restates the Hypothesis as a single open input,
Step~5, whose internal reduction terminates at the terminal-defect positivity $\Omega_7$: $\delta_N\ge0$
for all $N$, equivalently $\lambda_n\ge0$
for all $n$. Fourteen of the fifteen Steps are proved in the precise sense recorded in the dependency map
(\S\ref{sec:status-map}): their asserted construction, implication, equivalence, or conditional
consequence is proved. The unresolved mathematical input is Step~5, whose internal reduction terminates
at $\Omega_7$; and $\Omega_7$ is itself equivalent to
RH, being Li's criterion; the chain is therefore a reformulation of RH, not progress toward it. We do
not claim to have settled the Hypothesis. We therefore direct attention precisely there --- at $\Omega_7$ and at the
$\Omega$-chain reduction that isolates it (\S\ref{sec:live-proof-steps}) --- and we record, in the same
place, why each attempted closure of $\Omega_7$ returns to the structural walls of Part~I.

\subsection*{The setting: $\zeta$, the explicit formula, and the Weil criterion}

We orient the reader with the objects that recur throughout. The Riemann zeta function is
\[
   \zeta(s)\ =\ \sum_{n=1}^\infty n^{-s}\ =\ \prod_p (1-p^{-s})^{-1},\qquad\Re s>1,
\]
the Euler product being the avatar of unique factorization. The completed function $\xi(s)=
\tfrac12 s(s-1)\pi^{-s/2}\Gamma(s/2)\zeta(s)$ is entire of order 1, satisfies the functional equation
$\xi(s)=\xi(1-s)$, and has precisely the nontrivial zeros $\rho=\beta+i\gamma$ of $\zeta$ among its zeros.
In the coordinates $s=\tfrac12+iz$ the function $\Xi(z)=\xi(\tfrac12+iz)$ is even and real for real $z$,
with zeros at $z_\rho=i(\rho-\tfrac12)$; on the critical line $\beta=\tfrac12$ these zeros are real.
The Riemann Hypothesis asserts: \emph{all nontrivial zeros $\rho$ of $\zeta$ have $\Re\rho=\tfrac12$.}

The prime-number theory of these zeros is encoded in the explicit formula. For $h\in C_c^\infty(\R)$ put
$\widehat h(r)=\int_\R h(x)e^{irx}\,dx$ and write $\gamma_\rho=-i(\rho-\tfrac12)$, so that
$\gamma_\rho$ is real exactly when $\Re\rho=\tfrac12$. Weil's form of the explicit formula
\cite{Weil1952} states that $\sum_\rho\widehat h(\gamma_\rho)$ (summed symmetrically over all nontrivial
zeros, with multiplicity) equals the functional $\cW(h)$ of \eqref{eq:weilform} below, built from the
pole terms $\widehat h(\pm\tfrac i2)$, the prime-power weights $\Lambda(p^k)=\log p$, and an archimedean
term. Weil's criterion states that the Hypothesis is equivalent to
\[
   \cW(h\star\tilde h)\ \ge\ 0\qquad\text{for all }h\in C_c^\infty(\R),
\]
where $\tilde h(x)=\overline{h(-x)}$ and $\star$ is convolution. For a finite family $h_1,\dots,h_m$ the
Gram matrix $Q_{ij}=\cW(h_i\star\tilde h_j)$ is the finite-dimensional truncation of this criterion, and
the sign of $Q$ --- or
equivalently of the Pick kernel of $-\Xi'/\Xi$ --- is the object around which every route in this paper
revolves.

The Davenport--Heilbronn function $L_{\mathrm{DH}}$ \cite{DavenportHeilbronn,Titchmarsh} is a linear
combination of Dirichlet $L$-functions to an imaginary character modulo $5$; it satisfies a functional equation of the same type, $\xi_{\mathrm{DH}}(s)=\xi_{\mathrm{DH}}(1-s)$ with $\xi_{\mathrm{DH}}(s)=(\pi/5)^{-s/2}\Gamma(\tfrac{s+1}2)L_{\mathrm{DH}}(s)$, and has no Euler product; it has zeros off the critical line. It serves as the falsifier throughout: any inequality proved for $\zeta$ that also holds
for $L_{\mathrm{DH}}$ does not separate on-line from off-line zeros and therefore cannot imply RH. We apply this test throughout.

\section*{Original constructions and results of this paper}\label{sec:provenance}

To the author's knowledge, the following constructions, reductions, and assembled arguments are original
to this paper.  The claim is one of provenance of the present manuscript, not a substitute for an
exhaustive priority determination.

\emph{New constructions introduced here.}  The finite sourced channels and the sourced-determinant
(signature-kernel) architecture (Definition~\ref{def:target-channel} and \S\ref{sec:prologue}); the
ARP-P formulation itself (Definition~\ref{def:ARP-P}); the reference-whitened terminal defect
(Definition~\ref{def:terminal-defect-fixed}); the H0--H8 hierarchy of Step~5; and the $\Omega$-reduction
architecture (\S\ref{subsec:omega7-attack}).  The ARP-P architecture as a whole is introduced here and
assembled from stated classical analytic interfaces.

\emph{Results proved or derived here.}  The determinant serialization of RH
(Theorem~\ref{thm:det-serialization}) --- proved here; the one-point jet serialization
(Theorem~\ref{thm:one-point-jets}) --- proved here; the fixed-$N$ band closure
(Theorem~\ref{thm:band-closure}, with a threshold that is not effective) --- proved here, with classical Cauchy--Pick machinery; the global
reference-whitened theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:reference-whitened-terminal}) and the archimedean
whitening theorem in its positive range (Theorem~\ref{thm:whitened-terminal}) --- proved here; the Li--Cayley dictionary
(Theorem~\ref{thm:li-dictionary}) --- derived here, with the classical Li criterion used as terminal
input; the qualitative H6 witness and phase-adjusted quantitative reduction
(Corollary~\ref{cor:live-H6} and Theorem~\ref{thm:H6-closed}) --- proved here, with tail domination
left explicit; the de Bruijn--Newman cascade, of which only the bound $t_N^*\le\Lambda_\Xi=\Lambda_{\mathrm{dBN}}/4$ is proved and
the full formula is stated as Conjecture~\ref{thm:alpha5};
an isolated-zero envelope reduction is not used; Remark~\ref{prop:envelope-reduction} explains
that its separated zero series diverges;
and the stated insufficiency results (Proposition~\ref{prop:W5},
Proposition~\ref{prop:unboundable}, the last via Bombieri--Lagarias, cited) --- proved here; Remark~\ref{thm:W4} is a heuristic.

\emph{Classical inputs and external results}, used with citation and never reproved: the Weil explicit
formula and criterion \cite{Weil1952}; Nevanlinna--Pick interpolation and commutant lifting
\cite{FoiasFrazho}; Montel/Vitali normality; the Li criterion \cite{Li}, Keiper
\cite{Keiper1992}, and Bombieri--Lagarias \cite{BombieriLagarias1999}; de Bruijn \cite{deBruijn} and
Rodgers--Tao \cite{RodgersTao}; Connes--Consani \cite{connesconsani2021,ConnesConsani2023}; and Guth--Maynard
\cite{GuthMaynard}.

$\Omega_7$ is not our discovery; the independently constructed ARP-P architecture is shown, after the
analysis of its difficulty, to terminate at Li positivity.  That termination, together with the
obstruction ledger explaining why the major routes considered here repeatedly return to
RH-equivalent boundary or uniformity conditions, is the claimed contribution.  A construction can be
original even when the terminal statement it reaches is classical; that is exactly the situation here,
and we state it rather than let the reader discover it.

\part{The walls, and the path they shaped}

\section{Earlier approaches and their obstructions}\label{sec:history}

This section describes, in mathematical terms, the principal routes considered before the construction
given here, the obstructions met by each of them, and the way those obstructions point toward that
construction. Some of the obstructions below are proved statements; others are observations or
heuristics, and each is labelled accordingly.

\subsection{Overview}\label{sec:journey}

The first route starts from the statistics of arithmetic functions --- the distribution of the
number of prime factors $\omega(n)$, the fluctuations of $\Lambda(n)$ in short intervals, the numerics of
the explicit formula. It produced no theorem; the object retained from it is a \emph{localized Weil
quadratic form}, the Gram matrix $Q_{ij}=\cW(h_i\star\tilde h_j)$ of a finite family of test functions,
whose sign is related to the Riemann Hypothesis by Weil's criterion. From it grew a sequence of theoretical attacks: a
reduction of the Hypothesis to a Lindel\"of-type bound on a cross sum; an inverse-spectral, Hilbert--P\'olya
search for a self-adjoint operator with the zeros as spectrum; an adelic, trace-formula framing in the
spirit of the spectral approach discussed in \S\ref{sec:relwork}; and finally a sequence of
Hodge-theoretic and Frobenius-realization attempts modelled on the function-field proof. Each reached a
precise obstruction. Heuristically these obstructions are versions of one obstruction, and this
suggested working from the region of absolute convergence rather than on the critical line itself.
That is the move this paper makes.

\subsection{Localized Weil forms and their limitations}\label{sec:empirical}

The first route studied the arithmetic of $\omega(n)=\#\{p:p\mid n\}$ and of the von Mangoldt weights
$\Lambda(n)$ against the explicit formula. The goal was to find a \emph{reproducible discriminator}: a quantity, computable from finite data, positive for
$\zeta$ and negative for a function with a functional equation of the same type but zeros off the line --- the
Davenport--Heilbronn function $L_{\mathrm{DH}}$ \cite{DavenportHeilbronn,Titchmarsh}, which serves
throughout as the falsifier. The object retained is a \emph{localized Weil quadratic form}. For
$h\in C_c^\infty(\R)$, with $\widehat h(r)=\int_\R h(x)e^{irx}\,dx$, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weilform}
   \mathcal W(h)\ :=\ \widehat h(\tfrac i2)+\widehat h(-\tfrac i2)
   -\sum_{p,k}\frac{\log p}{p^{k/2}}\bigl(h(k\log p)+h(-k\log p)\bigr)+W_\infty(h),
\end{equation}
\[
   W_\infty(h)\ :=\ -h(0)\log\pi+\frac1{2\pi}\int_{\R}\widehat h(r)\,
   \Re\frac{\Gamma'}{\Gamma}\Bigl(\frac14+\frac{ir}2\Bigr)\,dr .
\]
By the explicit formula \cite{Weil1952}, $\mathcal W(h)=\sum_\rho\widehat h(\gamma_\rho)$, the sum over
the nontrivial zeros with $\gamma_\rho=-i(\rho-\tfrac12)$, taken symmetrically. (Normalization check: for the Gaussian $h(x)=e^{-x^2/2}$, to which the formula extends, the right side is $5.68076-2.68197-2.99880=0$ to the precision computed, and the zero side is $<10^{-42}$; this floating-point check is not certified.) Weil's criterion \cite{Weil1952} is that the Hypothesis is equivalent to
$\mathcal W(h\star\tilde h)\ge0$ for all $h\in C_c^\infty(\R)$. Localizing the test functions to a band of
scales, $h_1,\dots,h_m$, produces the finite Hermitian Gram matrix $Q_{ij}=\mathcal W(h_i\star\tilde h_j)$,
whose least eigenvalue is the object studied in this section. This is, in spirit, the band-limited and
archimedean-refined positivity of Connes--Consani \cite{connesconsani2021}; the study of $Q$ found
$\omega$-stratified structure in its fluctuations, but did not beat the convexity bound. What it leaves is
the form $Q$ and an analysis of \emph{why} it is hard to read, which we now record as a sequence of no-gos.

The form $\mathcal W$ can be read in two ways. On the \emph{zero side} $\sum_\rho\widehat h(\gamma_\rho)$ it
is a linear functional of the zero multiset $\{\rho\}$. On the \emph{arithmetic side} \eqref{eq:weilform}
it is a sum over prime powers, carrying the von Mangoldt weights $\Lambda(p^k)=\log p$, plus the pole
terms and the archimedean term from the $\Gamma$-factor. The two readings are equal by the explicit
formula; the Hypothesis is the statement that the common value is non-negative on every $h\star\tilde h$.
The numerical study of this balance across many test functions found structure stratified by
$\omega(n)$; it did not beat the convexity bound or give an unconditional proof.

The numerical work was conducted with the contemporary picture of $\zeta$ on the critical line in view:
the random-matrix statistics of the zeros and of the value distribution \cite{KeatingSnaith,FHK}, the
multiplicative-chaos description of the large values \cite{Soundararajan,BondarenkoSeip,SaksmanWebb,RhodesVargas}, the universality phenomenon \cite{Voronin,Bagchi}, and the hyperbolicity/interlacing
circle of ideas \cite{GORZ,MSS,BorceaBranden}. Each is a source of \emph{generic} information about the
zeros; none, by the principle isolated below, detects the measure-zero event of an off-line zero, and that
negative recognition is what this route contributed. None of the known results on the statistics of the zeros, their pair correlation or universality excludes an off-line zero at a small displacement. The Hypothesis is a statement about the \emph{individual}
configuration, not the generic one.

Each of the routes sketched in \S\ref{sec:journey} met a precise obstruction. We give them two full
sections of their own. Section~\ref{sec:nogos} records the \emph{no-go results}: concrete attempts,
each with the mathematics that closed it. Section~\ref{sec:walls} distils these into the small number of
\emph{structural walls} --- the obstructions that reappear, in disguise, on every front --- and attaches
to each a precise logical classification (defined at the head of \S\ref{sec:walls}): exact equivalence
with RH, framework closure obstruction, or insufficiency theorem for a stated input class. Informally we
shall sometimes say a statement is ``RH-strength''; this phrase carries no logical content beyond the
explicit classification attached to the statement. The reader interested only in the
reformulation may skip to \S\ref{sec:live-proof-steps}; the chain there was designed with the walls of
\S\ref{sec:walls} in view, and its open Step~5 meets them again.

\section{The no-go results}\label{sec:nogos}

This section is a ledger of negative results, grouped by route. Each obstruction is stated as a numbered
\textbf{No-go}, followed by the computation or theorem behind it. A no-go is a proved statement only when
its proof is given or cited; entries resting on numerical experiments or on heuristic arguments are
labelled \emph{observation} or \emph{heuristic} in their titles. Two earlier statements whose correct
forms are weaker are recorded in \S\ref{sec:withdrawn}.

\subsection{The detector and the limits of generic information}\label{sec:nogo-detector}

The object retained from the first route is the localized Weil form, the Gram matrix
$Q_{ij}=\cW(h_i\star\tilde h_j)$ built from \eqref{eq:weilform}. Before turning to the routes built on it, we record four no-gos
that concern the \emph{reading} of $Q$ itself: ways in which a quantity extracted from it looks like
progress but is, on inspection, either positive by construction or blind to the only event that matters.

\begin{nogo}[one-sided positivity is automatic]\label{ng:psd}
The least eigenvalue of the \emph{zero-side} Gram matrix is non-negative for trivial reasons and carries no
arithmetic content.
\end{nogo}
\noindent Write the zero-side block, assuming for this display that all $\gamma_\rho$ are real, as $G_{\mathrm{zeros}}[i,j]=\sum_\rho\widehat h_i(\gamma_\rho)\,
\overline{\widehat h_j(\gamma_\rho)}=\langle v_i,v_j\rangle$ with $v_i=(\widehat h_i(\gamma_\rho))_\rho$.
This is a Gram matrix, hence
\[
   G_{\mathrm{zeros}}\succeq0,\qquad\text{so}\qquad \lambda_{\min}(G_{\mathrm{zeros}})\ge0
\]
\emph{identically}, for every sampling of test functions. A positive measured value of
$\lambda_{\min}(G_{\mathrm{zeros}})$ for $\zeta$ is therefore not evidence of anything: it is the
positivity of an inner-product matrix, which holds whenever the ordinates are real. Positivity of a matrix
of this form is just a restatement of the location of the zeros; it carries no arithmetic content.

\begin{nogo}[the scalar minimum is a tautology under the Hypothesis]\label{ng:tauto}
Using $\lambda_{\min}(Q)$ as the discriminator between $\zeta$ and the falsifier separates them by the very
quantity one is trying to control.
\end{nogo}
\noindent Under the Hypothesis, $\lambda_{\min}(Q)\ge0$ for every finite family, by Weil's criterion.
Conversely, an off-line zero makes $\lambda_{\min}(Q)<0$ for some finite family (Weil's criterion gives an
$h$ with $\cW(h\star\tilde h)<0$; take the one-element family $\{h\}$). Thus
\[
   \inf_{\text{finite families}}\lambda_{\min}(Q)\ \ge\ 0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\RH,
\]
so ``separate $\zeta$ from $L_{\mathrm{DH}}$ by $\lambda_{\min}$'' is identical to ``decide whether there is
an off-line zero.'' The scalar is a faithful \emph{detector} but a circular \emph{criterion}; the genuine
non-tautological content lives one level down, in the prime-power terms of \eqref{eq:weilform},
not in any single eigenvalue.

\begin{nogo}[the global Cauchy--Schwarz bound is vacuous; heuristic]\label{ng:cs}
Controlling the localized form by a global second-moment bound destroys the localization that carries the
information.
\end{nogo}
\noindent For the energy $E(F)=\sum_\gamma|F(\gamma)|^2-\bar\rho\!\int|F|^2$ (with $\bar\rho$ the mean
density of the zero ordinates) the natural global estimate bounds the cross term by Cauchy--Schwarz with a second moment of order $T\log T$. The resulting inequality
is true but \emph{scale-blind}: it bounds a band-limited quantity by its total mass, and an off-line zero
perturbs the band, not the mass. Heuristically, the information sits in a Carleson-type measure
on the localized scales \cite{Carleson}, which the global bound integrates away.

\begin{nogo}[topology of the zero set does not see the shift; heuristic and observation]\label{ng:tda}
Persistent homology of the zero configuration cannot detect a displacement off the critical line.
\end{nogo}
\noindent A zero moved off the line by $\delta$ changes pairwise distances by $O(\delta^2)$, to which the
$0$-dimensional persistence barcode is insensitive at any realistic sample size; against a length-matched
control $L_{\mathrm{DH}}$ was not an outlier in numerical experiments not reproduced here. The instrument
measures a \emph{generic} feature of the configuration, and an off-line zero is a measure-zero
perturbation of it --- the recurring heuristic reason, discussed in Remark~\ref{thm:marginality} below,
that generic statistics do not decide the Hypothesis.

\subsection{First arc: the $\omega$-class route and the parity barrier}\label{sec:arcA}

The first theoretical attack tried to extract the Hypothesis, or at least the Lindel\"of bound, from the
measured statistics of the classes of $n$ by the number of prime factors $\omega(n)$. Six attempts closed,
each for a structural reason; we group them because the lesson is uniform.

\begin{nogo}[the cross-sum reduction is self-strengthening; heuristic]\label{ng:circular}
A bound for an $\omega$-class cross sum that yields Lindel\"of appears to require Lindel\"of-strength cancellation.
\end{nogo}
\noindent Let $B(N)$ denote a cross sum between the classes $\{n\le N:\omega(n)=k\}$ in the mean square of the Dirichlet polynomial $\sum_{n\le N}n^{-1/2-it}$ (its precise form is not needed). A bound $B(N)\ll N^\varepsilon$ was the hypothesis of a reduction to the Lindel\"of hypothesis; we know of no way to prove such a bound that does not already use cancellation of Lindel\"of strength, and we do not prove that it is strictly stronger. This is the first appearance of a pattern --- a true
implication whose antecedent is RH-strength --- that will recur as the master quantifier of
\S\ref{sec:walls}.

\begin{nogo}[exponent extrapolation does not cross the threshold; observation]\label{ng:extrap}
The measured large-values exponent does not fall below the level a large-values/Lindel\"of argument requires,
and the trend is not monotone.
\end{nogo}
\noindent In the large-values approach a Dirichlet polynomial of length $N$ must have its set of
$\sigma$-large points controlled by an exponent below the Bourgain--Demeter--Guth / Guth--Maynard barrier
\cite{BourgainDemeterGuth,GuthMaynard}. In numerical experiments not reproduced here, the exponent fitted
to $B(N)$ over a finite range stayed above the required level and its trend was not monotone in the number
of prime factors. In any case a numerical extrapolation of an exponent is not a proof.

\begin{nogo}[unconditional anti-correlation vanishes; heuristic]\label{ng:mv}
The cross-class anti-correlation that is visible at peaks is, unconditionally, zero on average.
\end{nogo}
\noindent By the Montgomery--Vaughan mean-value theorem \cite{IwaniecKowalski}, the mean square of the
relevant Dirichlet polynomial is the diagonal term plus a genuinely smaller off-diagonal, so the
unconditional cross-covariance between $\omega$-classes is of smaller order than the diagonal term. The anti-correlation that the
numerical study saw is a \emph{conditional}, peak-localized phenomenon; averaged unconditionally it is not
there, so it cannot be promoted to a statement about the zeros.

\begin{nogo}[the parity barrier blocks the separation]\label{ng:parity}
Classical sieve weights alone cannot distinguish $\omega(n)$ even from $\omega(n)$ odd, which is what the
route needs.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The separation the route requires is exactly a control on $\omega(n)\bmod2$, i.e.\ on the
Liouville-type sign $(-1)^{\omega(n)}$. Classical sieve weights alone cannot distinguish integers with
$\omega(n)$ even from odd (Selberg's parity phenomenon, exhibited by explicit examples
\cite{IwaniecKowalski}); breaking parity requires additional bilinear information, as in the work of
Friedlander--Iwaniec and Heath-Brown, and the route did not provide such information.

\begin{nogo}[hyperbolicity reformulations are equivalences]\label{ng:reform}
The Li, Jensen/hyperbolicity, de Bruijn--Newman and Nyman--Beurling criteria are restatements of the
Hypothesis, not approximations to it.
\end{nogo}
\noindent Each of
\[
   \lambda_n\ge0\ (\forall n),\qquad J^{d,n}\ \text{hyperbolic}\ (\forall d,n),\qquad
   \Lambda_{\mathrm{dBN}}\le0,\qquad d_N\to0,
\]
where $J^{d,n}$ are the Jensen polynomials of the Taylor coefficients of $\Xi$ (P\'olya; see
\cite{GORZ}), is \emph{equivalent} to the Hypothesis \cite{Li,GORZ,BaezDuarte,deBruijn,Newman,RodgersTao}
(RH $\iff\Lambda_{\mathrm{dBN}}\le0$ is de Bruijn--Newman \cite{deBruijn,Newman}; with Rodgers--Tao \cite{RodgersTao} this becomes $\Lambda_{\mathrm{dBN}}=0$). Verifying any of them for
finitely many $n,d,N$ is consistent with an off-line zero of arbitrarily small displacement; the finite
verification is not asymptotic evidence, and a full proof of any one of them is a full proof of RH. We
return to the precise sense of ``equivalent'' in \S\ref{sec:circular}.

\begin{nogo}[an unsupported transition; observation]\label{ng:phantom}
A geometric transition suggested by the $\omega$-class data is not supported.
\end{nogo}
\noindent An apparent transition in the $\omega$-class data at a finite threshold disappeared when the
statistic was recomputed from its definition, in numerical experiments not reproduced here; what remains is
the weaker observation that $\zeta$ appears somewhat more ``constructive'' at peaks. No statement of this
paper depends on it.

The arc closes on one lesson. Every quantity in it is either a restatement of the Hypothesis
(No-go~\ref{ng:circular}, \ref{ng:reform}) or a \emph{generic} statistic that the parity and convexity
barriers render, heuristically, blind to the measure-zero event of an off-line zero (No-go~\ref{ng:extrap}, \ref{ng:mv},
\ref{ng:parity}, \ref{ng:phantom}).

\subsection{Second arc: bounds on the off-line displacement}\label{sec:arcB}

The second route sought to control the real-part displacement
\[
   b_j\ :=\ \beta_j-\tfrac12,\qquad \rho_j=\beta_j+i\gamma_j\ \ \text{a nontrivial zero}.
\]
Since the zeros are symmetric about the critical line, RH is equivalent to $\sup_\rho(\beta_\rho-\tfrac12)=0$.
The classical mechanisms bound $\beta_\rho-\tfrac12$ from above, but only by quantities tending to
$\tfrac12$ as $|\gamma|\to\infty$ (zero-free regions), or they control aggregates (density theorems,
proportions), or they act in the wrong region, or they restate RH. We record this heuristic classification at
the end of the subsection, and give the mathematics for the most instructive cases.

\begin{nogo}[zero-free regions give a bound that does not tend to zero]\label{ng:kv}
The Korobov--Vinogradov zero-free region bounds $b_j$ from above by a quantity tending to $\tfrac12$.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The sharpest known zero-free region \cite{Korobov,MTY} states
\[
   \zeta(s)\ne0\qquad\text{for}\qquad \Re s\ >\ 1-\frac{c}{(\log|t|)^{2/3}(\log\log|t|)^{1/3}} .
\]
A zero $\rho_j=\beta_j+i\gamma_j$ therefore obeys
$\beta_j\le1-c/\bigl((\log\gamma_j)^{2/3}(\log\log\gamma_j)^{1/3}\bigr)$, which gives
\[
   b_j\ =\ \beta_j-\tfrac12\ \le\ \tfrac12-\frac{c}{(\log\gamma_j)^{2/3}(\log\log\gamma_j)^{1/3}} .
\]
This is an upper bound on $b_j$, which is the direction RH requires ($b_j\le0$); the failure is
quantitative: the bound tends to $\tfrac12$, not to $0$. Even a zero-free strip $\Re s>1-\delta$ (open,
and strictly weaker than RH) would give only $b_j\le\tfrac12-\delta$.

Heuristically, the zero-free region is proved by bounding $|\zeta(s)|$ away from zero for $\Re s$ close
to $1$, using the Euler product near the region of absolute convergence, and the bound weakens as
$\Re s\downarrow\tfrac12$; so the technique controls zeros near the boundary of the absolute-convergence
region, and its strength decays before the critical line is reached.

\begin{nogo}[the de Bruijn--Newman route is an equivalence]\label{ng:dbn}
The de Bruijn--Newman constant gives a reformulation of RH, and the known bounds do not reach it.
\end{nogo}
\noindent Let $H_t(z)=\int_0^\infty e^{tu^2}\Phi(u)\cos(zu)\,du$, so that $H_0(z)=\tfrac18\Xi(z/2)$; the
zeros of $H_0$ are $z=2i(\rho-\tfrac12)=2(-\gamma+ib)$ for $\rho=\tfrac12+b+i\gamma$, so they lie in $|\Im z|\le\Delta$ with
$\Delta=2\sup_\rho|b_\rho|$. De Bruijn \cite{deBruijn} showed $\Lambda_{\mathrm{dBN}}\le\frac12\Delta^2$
when all zeros of $H_0$ lie in $|\Im z|\le\Delta$; with Newman's conjecture $\Lambda_{\mathrm{dBN}}\ge0$
\cite{Newman} proved by Rodgers--Tao \cite{RodgersTao}, RH is equivalent to $\Lambda_{\mathrm{dBN}}=0$.
The known upper bounds $\Lambda_{\mathrm{dBN}}\le0.22$ \cite{PolymathDBN} and $\Lambda_{\mathrm{dBN}}\le0.2$
(Platt--Trudgian \cite{PlattTrudgian}) do not reach $0$; closing the gap is RH.

\begin{nogo}[the Nyman--Beurling criterion is an equivalence]\label{ng:nb}
The Nyman--Beurling criterion restates RH.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The Nyman--Beurling criterion \cite{Beurling,BaezDuarte} states that RH holds if and only if
$d_N\to0$, where $d_N$ is the $L^2(0,1)$ distance from the indicator of $(0,1)$ to the span of $N$
dilations of the fractional-part function. Proving $d_N\to0$ is therefore proving RH; the criterion
relocates the problem into a Hilbert space, and no unconditional rate for $d_N$ is known that would imply it.

\begin{nogo}[the Tur\'an inequalities hold unconditionally]\label{ng:turan}
The Tur\'an inequalities for the Taylor coefficients of $\Xi$ are a theorem, so they cannot exclude off-line
zeros.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The Tur\'an inequalities for the Taylor coefficients of $\Xi$ (hyperbolicity of the degree-$2$
Jensen polynomials) are necessary consequences of RH, and they hold unconditionally (G.~Csordas,
T.~S.~Norfolk and R.~S.~Varga, \emph{The Riemann hypothesis and the Tur\'an inequalities}, Trans.\ Amer.\
Math.\ Soc.\ 296 (1986)). Being implied by an unconditional theorem, they cannot exclude off-line zeros;
P\'olya's equivalence requires the hyperbolicity of the Jensen polynomials of all degrees and shifts.

\begin{nogo}[linear forms in logarithms address the wrong objects]\label{ng:baker}
Baker's theorem constrains algebraic numbers; the ordinates $\gamma_j$ carry no algebraic structure.
\end{nogo}
\noindent Baker's bounds for linear forms in logarithms \cite{IwaniecKowalski} apply to logarithms of
\emph{algebraic} numbers. The ordinates $\gamma_j$ are (conjecturally) transcendental and are not known to
satisfy any algebraic relation; the hypotheses of Baker's theorem simply do not engage them. This is a
category error, not a quantitative shortfall.

\begin{nogo}[pair correlation carries no off-line signal; heuristic]\label{ng:paircorr}
Montgomery's pair correlation describes spacings between on-line zeros and is silent about off-line ones.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The pair-correlation statistic \cite{Montgomery,Odlyzko,RudnickSarnak} measures the distribution
of differences $\gamma-\gamma'$ of zeros \emph{on} the line. An off-line zero is not a point of this
statistic; it contributes nothing to the correlation of the on-line ordinates and so no known form of the
statistic detects it.

The remaining mechanisms --- Selberg's density theorem and the modern zero-density and large-values
machinery \cite{Ingham,Huxley,Wooley,TTY}, the Levinson--Conrey positive-proportion results
\cite{Levinson,Conrey1989}, Littlewood's oscillation theorems, the large sieve, de Branges chain
conditions, the Selberg trace formula \cite{IwaniecSpectral}, and Weil positivity itself --- each fall, in their known form, under
one of the four headings of Remark~\ref{thm:fourway}. We expand on the density case since it is the source of the most persistent
optimism.

\begin{nogo}[density theorems count zeros, they do not locate them]\label{ng:density}
Selberg-type zero-density estimates bound the number of zeros with $\Re\rho\ge\sigma$ in aggregate; they
do not exclude an individual zero with small displacement $b_j=\beta_j-\tfrac12>0$.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The standard density results take the form
\[
   N(\sigma,T)\ :=\ \#\bigl\{\rho=\beta+i\gamma:\beta\ge\sigma,\ |\gamma|\le T\bigr\}
   \ \le\ C\,T^{A(1-\sigma)}\,(\log T)^B,
\]
for absolute constants $A,B,C$ \cite{Ingham,Huxley}. This bounds how many zeros can lie to the right of
$\Re s=\sigma$, simultaneously, in a height window $[0,T]$. But a single zero with $\beta=\tfrac12+
e^{-10^{100}}$ contributes $+1$ to $N(\sigma,T)$ for every $\sigma\le\tfrac12+e^{-10^{100}}$ and $T$
large enough --- that is, it contributes almost nothing to any density count. No density estimate of this
form can prevent one zero from having a very small displacement. The aggregate bound and the individual
bound are independent questions; all density theorems address the former and leave the latter wide open.

\begin{nogo}[positive proportions do not constrain off-line shifts]\label{ng:prop}
Knowing that a positive proportion of zeros lie on the critical line is orthogonal to knowing whether any
zero lies off it.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The Levinson--Conrey results \cite{Levinson,Conrey1989} show that a positive proportion of the
nontrivial zeros of $\zeta$ satisfy $\Re\rho=\tfrac12$: more than $40\%$ \cite{Conrey1989}, now more than
$41.7\%$ (K.~Pratt, N.~Robles, A.~Zaharescu and D.~Zeindler, Res.\ Math.\ Sci.\ 7 (2020)), as a lower
density of the zeros counted up to height $T$. This is a theorem about the on-line zeros; it says nothing
about the displacement of any zero that is not on the line. A hypothetical single off-line zero with
$b_j=10^{-1000}$ is perfectly compatible with $99.99\%$ of zeros being exactly on the line. The Hypothesis
(all zeros on the line) and the proportion results (some zeros on the line) are of entirely different
logical types. Counting is not locating.

\noindent We summarize the arc heuristically.

\begin{remark}[a heuristic classification of classical mechanisms]\label{thm:fourway}
Each of the mechanisms listed above that takes arithmetic data convergent in $\Re s>1$ yields, in its known
form, one of: \emph{(i)} an upper bound on $b_j$ that does not tend to $0$ (zero-free regions);
\emph{(ii)} a statement about the region $\Re s\approx1$ rather than the strip interior (Deuring--Heilbronn
repulsion; see the heuristic Paley--Wiener discussion below); \emph{(iii)} an aggregate count rather than
an individual displacement (density theorems, positive proportions, pair correlation); \emph{(iv)} a
reformulation equivalent to RH (Nyman--Beurling, Li/Jensen, de Bruijn--Newman); or a consequence of an
unconditional theorem (Tur\'an). We do not claim this list is exhaustive or that no combination can
succeed.
\end{remark}

\noindent We add a heuristic sketch for mode (ii), the Paley--Wiener barrier.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{The Paley--Wiener barrier (heuristic).} The Deuring--Heilbronn phenomenon: if a Dirichlet $L$-function of a real character mod $q$ has a real zero $\beta_0$ very close to $1$, then the other zeros of the $L$-functions mod $q$ are repelled from the line $\Re s=1$ (the zero-free region widens by a factor $\log\bigl(1/((1-\beta_0)\log qT)\bigr)$). This is information about the region near $\Re s=1$. To transfer it into the strip $\tfrac12<\Re s<1$, one would need a function $F(s)$
analytic on the strip that (a) is controlled by arithmetic at $\Re s>1$ and (b) recovers the desired
information at $\Re s=\tfrac12$. Such a function would have its values on the line $\Re s=\tfrac12$
determined by its values on the line $\Re s=1+\delta$ via Cauchy's integral formula, which requires
pointwise control in a wide strip. Heuristically, no known mechanism transfers such information from $\Re s\approx1$ to $\Re s=\tfrac12$; we do not prove that none exists.

The Deuring--Heilbronn repulsion is therefore an excellent theorem about the region near $\Re s=1$; it is
simply the wrong region for a proof of RH; we do not prove that no sharpening could change this.

\medskip
\noindent Heuristically, the single cause beneath all four modes is that \emph{arithmetic information available for
$\Re s>1$ does not propagate into the critical strip $\tfrac12<\Re s<1$}: the Euler product, the only
source of arithmetic data, converges only to the right of the strip. This is so even though numerical
verification of the Hypothesis now reaches astronomical height \cite{PlattTrudgian} --- precisely because
verification at a height is not propagation into the strip. Remark~\ref{thm:fourway} describes the first face of the arithmetic-propagation wall (Wall~\ref{ob:propagation}).

\subsection{Third arc: local--global factorization and the adelic eigenvectors}\label{sec:arcC}

A natural response to the propagation barrier is to localize: factor the Weil form over places,
$Q=Q_\infty+\sum_pQ_p$, prove each local piece $Q_p\succeq0$, and conclude global positivity by a
Hasse--Minkowski principle. This is the strategy by which the function-field analogue is proved. Over
$\mathbb Q$ it fails at two independent points, and the companion spectral construction --- realizing the
zeros as eigenvalues on the id\`ele class space --- fails at a third.

\begin{nogo}[the zero side does not factor over primes]\label{ng:nofactor}
The spectral part of the Weil form is a global functional of the zero multiset and admits no per-prime
decomposition.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The prime part $\sum_{p,k}\frac{\log p}{p^{k/2}}\bigl(h(k\log p)+h(-k\log p)\bigr)$ of
\eqref{eq:weilform} is a sum over primes by construction. The zero side does not decompose:
\[
   \cW(h)\ =\ \sum_\rho\widehat h(\gamma_\rho)
\]
depends on the zeros, which are \emph{global} objects --- no Euler factor $(1-p^{-s})^{-1}$ ever vanishes,
so no prime ``owns'' a zero and there is no place $p$ at which to attach a local piece $Q_p$ of the spectral
form. The factorization $Q=Q_\infty+\sum_pQ_p$ that the strategy requires does not exist on the side that
carries the information; there is no analogue of ``Frobenius at $p$'' for $\zeta/\mathbb Q$.

\begin{nogo}[no Hasse--Minkowski in infinite dimension; heuristic]\label{ng:hm}
Even granting local positivity at every place, the local-to-global passage is invalid for Hermitian forms
in infinite dimension.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The classical Hasse--Minkowski theorem --- a quadratic form over $\mathbb Q$ is isotropic iff it
is isotropic over every completion --- is a theorem about \emph{finite}-dimensional forms. The Weil form is
infinite-dimensional, and we know of no Hasse principle for the signature of an infinite-dimensional
Hermitian form. So even if No-go~\ref{ng:nofactor} were
somehow circumvented, the inference ``$Q_v\succeq0\ \forall v\Rightarrow Q\succeq0$'' would require a
separate argument. (If $Q=\sum_vQ_v$ held as an identity, positivity would pass to the sum; the point is
that no such decomposition with local positive pieces is available, and this sentence is a heuristic, not
a theorem.)

\begin{nogo}[the adelic eigenvectors are not in the space]\label{ng:l2}
The explicit characters that would realize zeros as eigenvalues on the id\`ele class space are not
square-integrable, whether or not the zero is on the line.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The companion construction seeks an operator $H_C$ on $L^2(C_{\mathbb Q})$, $C_{\mathbb Q}$ the
id\`ele class space, with the off-line zeros among its eigenvalues, witnessed by the explicit vectors
$f_j(x)=x^{\,b_j+i\gamma_j}$. But, with the multiplicative Haar measure $d^\times x=dx/x$ on $\R_+^*$,
\[
   \int_0^\infty x^{2b}\,\frac{dx}{x}\ =\ \infty\qquad\text{for every real }b,
\]
so neither $x^{i\gamma}$ nor $x^{b+i\gamma}$ lies in $L^2(\R_+^*,d^\times x)$; on-line zeros correspond to
tempered (unitary) generalized eigenvectors and off-line zeros to non-tempered ones. The obstruction is to
construct a Hilbert space on which the non-tempered characters are excluded, as in Connes' trace formula
framework; the $L^2$ norm computation alone does not do this.

\begin{nogo}[off-line is non-unitary; observation]\label{ng:twolang}
A zero is off the line exactly when its character is non-unitary, so excluding non-unitary characters is a
restatement of the problem.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The quasi-character $|x|^{b_j+i\gamma_j}$ is unitary exactly when $b_j=0$. An adelic
Gr\"ossencharakter argument that tries to exclude the non-unitary part of the spectrum without first
resolving the displacement therefore re-encounters the same question in dual language.

\subsection{Fourth arc: the sign of the curvature, and a failed anticommutation}\label{sec:arcD}

A further family of attempts tried to make the Hypothesis a \emph{variational} or \emph{symmetry} statement:
to exhibit the critical line as the minimizer of an energy, or to force a spectral invariant to vanish by an
anticommuting involution. They closed because the geometry of $\xi$ on the critical line has, under the
Hypothesis, the \emph{opposite sign} to the one these arguments need.

\begin{nogo}[the critical line as a maximum; heuristic]\label{ng:extremum}
Phrasing $b_j=0$ as the global minimum of an arithmetic energy appears to be backwards: a formal second
variation is non-positive.
\end{nogo}
\noindent For the localized form of a family of off-line test configurations, a formal second variation in
the displacement $b$ is non-positive under the vanishing condition $\widehat h(\rho_j)=0$ at the displaced
zeros; we do not give a proof here. Heuristically, an argument that hopes to pin $b_j=0$ by exhibiting it
as an energy minimum works against this curvature.

\begin{nogo}[the curvature sign is positive, so concavity criteria are false]\label{ng:curvature}
Under RH, $\sigma\mapsto\log|\xi(\sigma+i\gamma)|$ is strictly convex at the line for $\gamma$ not a zero
ordinate; a sufficiency criterion phrased as concavity cannot hold.
\end{nogo}
\noindent Under the Hypothesis, for $\gamma$ not a zero ordinate, the curvature of $\log|\xi|$ across the critical line is computed from the
Hadamard product: writing $\log\xi(s)=B_0+\sum_\rho[\log(1-s/\rho)+s/\rho]$ and differentiating twice in
$\sigma$ at $s=\tfrac12+i\gamma$,
\[
   \frac{\partial^2}{\partial\sigma^2}\log|\xi\!\left(\tfrac12+\sigma+i\gamma\right)\bigg|_{\sigma=0}
   \ =\ C(\gamma)\ :=\ \sum_\rho\frac{1}{(\gamma-\gamma_\rho)^2}\ >\ 0 .
\]
(Since $\log|\xi|$ is harmonic off the zeros, this equals $-\partial_t^2\log|\xi(\tfrac12+it)|$ at
$t=\gamma$; the sum converges absolutely.) Every term is positive, so $C(\gamma)>0$ under the Hypothesis.
Without RH an off-line pair contributes terms of either sign; we do not prove the converse. (Compare
J.~Sondow and C.~Dumitrescu, \emph{A monotonicity property of Riemann's xi function and a reformulation of
the Riemann hypothesis}, Period.\ Math.\ Hungar.\ 60 (2010), where RH is shown equivalent to $|\xi|$ being
increasing in $\sigma>\tfrac12$ on every horizontal line.) A criterion of the form
``$\sigma\mapsto\log|\xi(\sigma+i\gamma)|$ is $\sigma$-concave $\Rightarrow$ RH'' therefore cannot be
established: under RH the function is strictly $\sigma$-convex at the line off the zero ordinates, so the
hypothesis of the criterion is never satisfied. (The heuristic of No-go~\ref{ng:extremum} concerns a
different object, the localized form, not $\log|\xi|$.)

\begin{nogo}[the arithmetic sector cannot be controlled without the zeros]\label{ng:sector}
The inequality relevant to the variational route is a consequence of the Hypothesis, and controlling it
requires the location of the zeros.
\end{nogo}
\noindent Write the second derivative $\partial_t^2\log|\xi(\tfrac12+it)|$, for $t$ not a zero ordinate,
as the sum of three sectors:
\begin{align}
   G_{\mathrm{poly}}(t)&\ =\ \frac{2\bigl(\tfrac14-t^2\bigr)}{\bigl(t^2+\tfrac14\bigr)^2},\label{eq:gpoly}\\
   G_\Gamma(t)&\ =\ -\tfrac14\,\Re\,\psi^{(1)}\!\Bigl(\tfrac14+\tfrac{it}{2}\Bigr),\label{eq:ggamma}\\
   G_\zeta(t)&\ =\ \partial_t^2\log|\zeta\!\left(\tfrac12+it\right)|,\label{eq:gzeta}
\end{align}
where $\psi^{(1)}$ is the trigamma function and $G_{\mathrm{ana}}=G_{\mathrm{poly}}+G_\Gamma$ is the
analytic sector (known). The sector $G_{\mathrm{poly}}$ arises from the polynomial prefactor of $\xi$ and
is readily computed; $G_\Gamma$ arises from the $\Gamma$ factor and is a smooth, controlled function.
What remains, $G_\zeta$, is the arithmetic sector.

(The factor $\pi^{-s/2}$ contributes nothing to the second $t$-derivative.) By the Hadamard product, the
sectors sum to
\[
   G_{\mathrm{ana}}(t)+G_\zeta(t)\ =\ \partial_t^2\log|\xi(\tfrac12+it)|
   \ =\ \sum_\rho\Re\frac{1}{\bigl(\tfrac12-\beta_\rho+i(t-\gamma_\rho)\bigr)^2},
\]
which under RH equals $-\sum_\rho(t-\gamma_\rho)^{-2}<0$. (In floating point, not certified, the left side is $-0.053$,
$-0.242$, $-1.077$, $-0.583$ at $t=5,17,20,23$.) The relevant inequality is therefore
$G_\zeta<-G_{\mathrm{ana}}$ off the zero ordinates; it is a consequence of RH, and controlling $G_\zeta$
needs the location, real parts included, of the zeros. We do not prove that it implies RH.

\begin{nogo}[symmetry is not antisymmetry; heuristic]\label{ng:antisym}
The functional-equation involution preserves the relevant operator instead of negating it, so the
$\eta$-invariant argument does not run.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The attempt forces a spectral $\eta$-invariant to vanish by exhibiting an involution $J$ with
$JT_0J^{-1}=-T_0$, which would pair eigenvalues $\lambda\leftrightarrow-\lambda$ and annihilate $\eta$. But
the only RH-free involution available, the functional-equation symmetry $s\mapsto1-s$, satisfies
\[
   J\,T_0\,J^{-1}\ =\ T_0 \qquad(\text{symmetry}),\qquad\text{not}\qquad J\,T_0\,J^{-1}=-T_0\ (\text{antisymmetry}).
\]
It preserves the spectrum rather than reflecting it, so it gives no constraint on $\eta$. The required
Fourier anticommutation $Q(f,g)+Q(\widehat f,\widehat g)=0$ remains open.

\subsection{Fifth arc: reflection positivity, false discriminants, and the sign
principle}\label{sec:arcE}

The next obstructions concern \emph{positivity per se} --- the attempt to prove $Q\succeq0$ directly,
rather than to bound a displacement. Each indicates where the positivity does \emph{not} come from, and
together they lead to the heuristic sign principle of Remark~\ref{thm:marginality}.

\begin{nogo}[per-place reflection positivity fails; observation]\label{ng:perplace}
The per-prime kernel of the Osterwalder--Schrader attack considered is indefinite, so there is no local
positive structure to reconstruct from.
\end{nogo}
\noindent A reflection-positivity (Osterwalder--Schrader) strategy would factor the form into per-prime
kernels and reconstruct a positive Hamiltonian. For the kernel at a prime $p$ used in that attempt (whose
derivation we do not reproduce), the $r$-dependence enters, up to a positive factor, through the bracket
$\sqrt p\,\cos(r\log p)-1$. At $r=0$ the bracket is $\sqrt p-1>0$, but at $r=\pi/\log p$ it is
$-\sqrt p-1<0$; so this kernel is indefinite. There is no per-place positive structure in this form, so reflection positivity cannot
be reconstructed place by place; positivity, if it holds, is a genuinely \emph{cross-place} phenomenon.

\begin{nogo}[positivity does not follow from reconstruction]\label{ng:os}
A positive Hamiltonian does not follow automatically from an OS reconstruction, because the two-point
function is oscillatory.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The assertion that $H\ge0$ follows ``for free'' from
Osterwalder--Schrader reconstruction is invalid: the reconstruction theorem requires
reflection positivity, which forces the two-point function to be the Laplace transform of a positive
measure (hence positive and decreasing); the two-point function here oscillates, so the hypothesis is not met, and the conclusion does not
follow. (See also \S\ref{sec:withdrawn}.)

\begin{nogo}[multiplicativity does not control the sign; observation]\label{ng:mult}
The Euler product is not what makes the localized form positive: a reproducible discriminant fails to
separate $\zeta$ from smooth non-multiplicative controls.
\end{nogo}
\noindent A natural thesis is that \emph{multiplicativity} (the Euler product), rather than the location of
the zeros, controls the sign of the localized form. It was tested with a Carleson-type constant
$C=\lambda_{\max}(A^{-1/2}PA^{-1/2})$, for a positive matrix $A$ and a matrix $P$ built from the
coefficients, comparing $\zeta$ against smooth, deliberately \emph{non}-multiplicative controls. In
numerical experiments not reproduced here, the statistic did not separate $\zeta$ from the controls. An
apparent separation in a cruder test is attributed, heuristically, to \emph{smoothness} in $\log n$ rather
than to multiplicativity, because the smooth envelope of the localized form integrates out the
fine, individual-$\log p$ correlation in which multiplicativity actually lives. What the experiment leaves
behind is useful: a relative discriminant (fixed $A$, varying the multiplicative structure) that cancels the
normalization and is reusable. What it removes is the hope that ``positivity $=$ multiplicativity.''

All of this points in one direction, which we now state as a heuristic organizing principle.

\begin{remark}[heuristic sign principle]\label{thm:marginality}
Let $\chi$ be the character modulo $5$ with $\chi(2)=i$, let
$\kappa=\bigl(\sqrt{10-2\sqrt5}-2\bigr)/(\sqrt5-1)\approx0.284$, and let
$L_{\mathrm{DH}}=\frac{1-i\kappa}2L(s,\chi)+\frac{1+i\kappa}2L(s,\bar\chi)$. Then
$L_{\mathrm{DH}}=\sum_na(n)n^{-s}$ with $a(n)=\Re\chi(n)+\kappa\Im\chi(n)$, so $a(3)=-\kappa$, and
$-L_{\mathrm{DH}}'/L_{\mathrm{DH}}=\sum_n\Lambda_{\mathrm{DH}}(n)n^{-s}$ with
$\Lambda_{\mathrm{DH}}(3)=a(3)\log3=-\kappa\log3<0$, while $\Lambda(3)=\log3>0$ and $\Lambda(n)\ge0$ for
all $n$. \emph{Heuristic:} the feature that separates $\zeta$ from this off-line-zero falsifier is the
\emph{sign} of its von Mangoldt data, not its size; in the conditional realization layer (\S\ref{sec:shorting}; hypothetical, the paper does not construct its operators) the weights $(\log p)p^{-k/2}$ enter through square roots, which requires them to be nonnegative, and a functional that reads only the magnitudes is not expected to certify this.
\end{remark}

We use this as a heuristic organizing principle for a class of arguments: the property the conditional realization route needs --- the positivity of the Hermitian form built from the weights $\Lambda(p^k)\ge0$ in the realization layer (\S\ref{sec:shorting}) --- is a statement about the \emph{signs} of the weights $\Lambda(n)$, and we do
not expect a functional of the sizes $\{|\Lambda(n)|\}$ alone to certify it. A sign-changed sequence
$\varepsilon(n)\Lambda(n)$ has the same magnitudes and loses this positivity; $L_{\mathrm{DH}}$ is an
example of sign-indefinite coefficients together with off-line zeros. So an $\ell^p$ norm, a spectral radius, a Carleson
constant, or a second moment of $\{|\Lambda(n)|\}$ does not see the sign.
(We do not claim the magnitudes of $\zeta$ and $L_{\mathrm{DH}}$ coincide --- they do not, since
$\Lambda_{\mathrm{DH}}$ is a genuinely different sequence; the point is the sharper one that positivity is
a sign property no size functional accesses.)

The remark suggests what the distinguishing feature should be: the genuine
\emph{positivity} $\Lambda(n)\ge0$ (equivalently, that $\Lambda_{\mathrm{DH}}$ changes sign). This is not a
quantitative difference; it is a structural one. It is the input used in the realization layer (\S\ref{sec:shorting}): the positivity $\Lambda(n)\ge0$ permits a positive
Hamiltonian candidate.  A canonical self-adjoint realization $A_P$ with the required compression and
limit still has to be constructed.  If it is, its resolvent bound is the seed of the Vitali
normal-family argument. For $L_{\mathrm{DH}}$ the corresponding weights $\Lambda_{\mathrm{DH}}(n)$ change sign, so the analogous form is indefinite.

\subsection{Sixth arc: the cohomological frontier}\label{sec:arcF}

The last family of attempts modelled the desired proof on the function-field case, where the analogue of
the Hypothesis is a theorem: for curves, Weil's proof \cite{Weil1948} obtains $|\alpha|=\sqrt q$ from the
positivity of an intersection pairing (Deligne's general proof \cite{Deligne} proceeds differently).
Transporting the curve mechanism to $\zeta$ requires a Frobenius-type operator preserving a
positive-definite pairing with a lower bound uniform in the truncation. Two properties were tested
numerically on finite windows in one model (a complex structure $J$ and a polarization form); the
observations are recorded below and are not statements about all models. Before reaching them we record
the averaging obstruction that motivated the test.

\begin{nogo}[off-line secular growth survives every average; heuristic]\label{ng:cesaro}
Averaging the band statistic over scale does not remove the off-line contribution; it relocates the
Hypothesis rather than resolving it.
\end{nogo}
\noindent Consider a band statistic $b_{\mathrm{bulk}}(\lambda)$ in the scale $\lambda>1$ which, by the
explicit formula, is a sum over the zeros in which a zero $\rho=\beta+i\gamma$ contributes a term
proportional to
\[
   \lambda^{\,2\beta-1}\,\lambda^{\,2i\gamma}.
\]
On the critical line $\beta=\tfrac12$ this is the pure oscillation $\lambda^{2i\gamma}$, which a Ces\`aro
average in $\lambda$ sends to $0$. \emph{Off} the line it is the secular factor $\lambda^{2\beta-1}$ with
$2\beta-1>0$, a positive power that no Ces\`aro average removes. So Ces\`aro boundedness of such a
statistic is (granting convergence of the zero sum) a consequence of RH; we do not prove the converse
here. In numerical experiments not reproduced here, the statistic for $\zeta$ looked flat in $\lambda$
while that for $L_{\mathrm{DH}}$ grew, as the off-line contribution predicts. The average is a detector,
not a proof step.

\begin{nogo}[the polarization appears gapless; observation]\label{ng:gapless}
On finite windows, a quaternionic Hodge--Riemann positivity was observed for $\zeta$, but its spectrum
appeared to decay to zero with no gap, so it did not force an eigenvalue bound.
\end{nogo}
\noindent In numerical experiments on finite windows, not reproduced here, the finite-window Weil matrix
of $\zeta$ appeared to carry a \emph{quaternionic Hodge--Riemann polarization} (a Deninger-type structure with $J^2=-1$,
modelled on Deninger's conjectured cohomology \cite{Deninger}): the form is positive semidefinite on the
$+i$-eigenspace $V_+$ of $J$ for $\zeta$, while the falsifier $L_{\mathrm{DH}}$ and a symmetry-matched
random control appeared \emph{indefinite} there. (The structure $J$, the space $V_+$ and the windows are
those of the experiment and are not defined here; this entry is a report of observations, not a
statement.)

The observed spectrum of the polarization form on $V_+$ decayed geometrically towards $0$ as the window
grew, with no uniform positive lower bound visible, and the corresponding largest eigenvalue ratio
appeared marginal for $\zeta$ and larger for $L_{\mathrm{DH}}$. These observations are not claimed to be
equivalent to RH, and they are not consequences of a gap.

The function-field mechanism gives eigenvalue bounds because the polarization there is positive
definite on a finite-dimensional cohomology, and the isometry property forces all eigenvalues of
Frobenius onto $|\alpha|=\sqrt q$. Over $\Spec\mathbb Z$, the finite-window analogues observed above
showed no gap. Heuristically, each reformulation considered --- the Ces\`aro band statistic, the
Hodge--Riemann positivity on $V_+$ --- behaves as a marginal, gapless detector; a gap cannot be supplied by
numerics, and the construction that would supply it geometrically is the unbuilt $\Spec\mathbb
Z$-cohomology of the next no-go.

\begin{nogo}[the natural Frobenius is of the wrong type; observation]\label{ng:antilinear}
In the finite-window model, the scaling generator anticommutes with the quaternionic complex structure, so
a Frobenius built from it is complex-antilinear --- not a Hodge morphism, which must be complex-linear.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The operator whose eigenvalues are (formally) the imaginary parts of the zeros of $\zeta$ is the
log-scaling generator $D_{\log}$. With $J$ the quaternionic complex structure of No-go~\ref{ng:gapless}
(satisfying $J^2=-1$, $J$ anti-Hermitian), the finite-window computation, not reproduced here, gave
\[
   \{D_{\log},J\}\ =\ D_{\log}J+JD_{\log}\ =\ 0
\]
(the symbol of $D_{\log}$ being odd in the scale index); note that $\{D_{\log},J\}=0$ implies
$[D_{\log},J]=2D_{\log}J$, so $\|[D_{\log},J]\|=2\|D_{\log}\|$ when $J$ is unitary. Since $\{D_{\log},J\}=0$, the operator $D_{\log}$ anticommutes with $J$, i.e.\ $D_{\log}J=-JD_{\log}$. This
means that for any $J$-eigenstate $v$ with $Jv=iv$ we have $J(D_{\log}v)=-D_{\log}(Jv)=-iD_{\log}v$: the
image $D_{\log}v$ has $J$-eigenvalue $-i$, not $+i$. Therefore $D_{\log}$ maps $V_+$ to $V_-$, not to
itself; it is complex-\emph{anti}linear with respect to $J$. A Hodge morphism (a ``Frobenius'' for the
Hodge--Riemann structure) must be complex-\emph{linear}, i.e.\ must satisfy $[F,J]=0$. The operator
$D_{\log}$ is of the wrong type.

For contrast, for an elliptic curve over a finite field $\mathbb F_q$ the Frobenius is a morphism of the
relevant structure, the Hodge--Riemann form is positive definite, and every eigenvalue of the Frobenius
satisfies $|\alpha|=\sqrt q$ (Hasse). In the finite-window model examined, neither property was
observed (observation).

The object that would close this route is a Frobenius-type operator preserving a
positive-definite pairing with a uniform lower bound on a cohomology of $\Spec\mathbb Z$ --- the unbuilt object of the adelic
approach \cite{connesconsani-site}. Its absence is not an estimate one is missing; it is a
\emph{construction} that does not yet exist. Recognizing that the obstruction is one of construction
rather than of estimation is what motivates turning away from realizing the zeros as a Frobenius
\emph{spectrum} and toward realizing them as \emph{poles} forbidden by a normal-family continuation ---
the route of this paper.

\subsection{Circular reformulations}\label{sec:circular}

Several attractive criteria are not approximations to the Hypothesis but restatements of it, or are too
weak to constrain it. We list them together: for an equivalence, a finite verification proves nothing
asymptotic and a full proof is a full proof of RH; the first, second and fourth items are instead weaker
than RH. In each case the parenthetical states why.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.6em]
\item Selberg-type density bounds on the \emph{count} of off-line zeros (a single zero with $b_j=e^{-10^{100}}$
satisfies them all; counting is not locating).
\item Positive-proportion results, ``more than $40\%$ of zeros on the line'' \cite{Levinson,Conrey1989} (a statement
about on-line zeros that is silent about the shift of any off-line one).
\item The Nyman--Beurling distance $d_N\downarrow0$ \cite{Beurling,BaezDuarte} (equivalent to RH, No-go~\ref{ng:nb}).
\item The Tur\'an inequalities for the Taylor coefficients of $\Xi$ (an unconditional theorem, hence not a
constraint, No-go~\ref{ng:turan}).
\item Bounds $\psi(x)-x=O_\varepsilon(x^{\theta+\varepsilon})$ (such a bound holds for every $\varepsilon>0$ if and only if $\sup_\rho\beta_\rho\le\theta$; with $\theta=\tfrac12$ it is RH, and no $\theta<1$ is known).
\item Li's criterion $\lambda_n\ge0$ for all $n$ and the hyperbolicity of all Jensen polynomials $J^{d,n}$
\cite{Li,GORZ} (each equivalent to RH; finite verification is not asymptotic evidence).
\end{itemize}
\noindent The common signature is the umbrella wall of \S\ref{sec:walls}: the Weil-positivity criterion
$\cW(f\star\tilde f)\ge0$ is \emph{itself} equivalent to the Hypothesis \cite{Weil1952}, so any quantity that
faithfully captures it inherits the equivalence.

\subsection{Two statements not used}\label{sec:withdrawn}

Two statements that might be expected here are not used. First, for the operator model of
\S\ref{sec:analogies}, uniqueness of a spectral minimizer does not follow from convexity, since
$A\succ0$, $B\succeq0$ does not make $A-B$ convex; the correct statement is the reduction of uniqueness to
$\|A^{-1/2}BA^{-1/2}\|<1$, which is not proved. Second, no numerical value of the Davenport--Heilbronn data
is used; $L_{\mathrm{DH}}$ is used only qualitatively.

\subsection{The terminal-defect continuation: the ARP-P path and attempts on its terminal inequality}\label{sec:p52}

The companion development --- the arithmetic Pick positivity (ARP-P) path --- restates the Hypothesis as a
single terminal-defect positivity, $\delta_N\ge0$ for all $N$, equivalently $\lambda_n\ge0$ for all $n$
(Li--Keiper \cite{Li}). Attacking that residue produced a further layer of no-gos, each of which lands on
one of the structural walls of \S\ref{sec:walls}. We add them to the ledger; together they sharpen, rather
than escape, the master quantifier.

\begin{nogo}[scope of the terminal absolute-envelope route; heuristic]\label{ng:envelope}
Let $\lambda_n^{\mathrm{prime}}$ denote the prime-power (von Mangoldt) contribution to the Li coefficient
$\lambda_n$ in its explicit-formula decomposition (see \S\ref{sec:live-proof-steps}). A scale-uniform bound
$|\lambda_n^{\mathrm{prime}}|\le c\sqrt n\log n$ for $n\ge2$ is a sufficient route of RH strength once a
finite initial range is checked.  A lossless stationary-phase reduction is not available, because it would
separate a divergent zero sum (Remark~\ref{prop:envelope-reduction}).
\end{nogo}
\noindent Formally write the Li oscillation on the zero side as
$O(n)=\sum_\rho(1-1/\rho)^n$; this isolated series does not converge and is used here only to display
the expected obstruction. The functional equation
gives the exact identity $1-1/(1-\rho)=(1-1/\rho)^{-1}$ and $|1-1/\rho|^2-1=(1-2\beta)/|\rho|^2$; the
Cayley map $\rho\mapsto1-1/\rho$ carries the critical line to the unit circle. Three independent routes to
the envelope bound each terminate at a distinct canonical RH-equivalent,
\[
   \underbrace{|1-1/\rho|=1\ \forall\rho}_{\text{Riemann: zeros on the line}};\qquad
   \underbrace{N(\sigma,T)=0\ \text{on }\tfrac12<\sigma<1}_{\text{RH restated}};\qquad
   \underbrace{\psi(x)-x=O(\sqrt x\log^2 x)}_{\text{von Koch: the RH-strength PNT error}},
\]
and in each the familiar argument invokes an RH-strength tail estimate.  These are diagnostics, not
a proof of equivalence for the separated series. A single off-line zero $\rho=\beta+i\gamma$ (through
the member of its four-element cluster with $\beta<\tfrac12$) injects a term $r^n$ with
$r=|1-1/\rho|>1$ that only \emph{eventually} overtakes the ceiling, so no finite numerical
verification, at any $n$ or precision, closes it. This is the umbrella wall (W\ref{ob:weil}) read on the
Li coordinates.

\begin{nogo}[the archimedean partition cannot be a density]\label{ng:partition}
Splitting the archimedean term into per-prime densities $\sigma_p\ge0$ that dominate each prime comb
cannot work with $\sigma_p$ a genuine density: it fails at large resonances, for every prime and every
budget.
\end{nogo}
\noindent By Bochner the single-prime domination is $\hat\sigma_p(\xi)\ge2\sum_k(\log p)p^{-k/2}
\cos(k\xi\log p)$; the right side is almost-periodic with peaks $2\log p/(\sqrt p-1)$ at every resonance
$\xi\in(2\pi/\log p)\Z$, whereas a density obeys $\hat\sigma_p(\xi)\to0$ (Riemann--Lebesgue). Domination
therefore fails at all large resonances. Per prime and at any fixed support cutoff the problem \emph{is}
feasible --- compare the archimedean positivity of Connes--Consani \cite{connesconsani2021} --- and the obstruction is the simultaneous limit over all primes: the
uniform-norm wall (W\ref{ob:uniform}) in frequency form.

\begin{nogo}[the archimedean perturbative route; heuristic]\label{ng:perturbative}
The archimedean positivity margin is small compared with the prime perturbation for large support, so a
gap-versus-norm comparison cannot hold uniformly.
\end{nogo}
\noindent For the archimedean Weil operator on an interval of small length $a$, the lowest eigenvalue is
expected to behave like $\lambda_a=\log(1/a)+O(1)$ as $a\to0$ (an asymptotic we do not prove here); the
prime perturbation has $\ell^1$-mass $\asymp e^{a/2}$, so a norm comparison cannot hold for large $a$. We
do not prove that no non-perturbative bound exists; heuristically, an archimedean route to the residue
must be \emph{non-perturbative}. This is the wrong-sign
wall (W\ref{ob:wrongsign}) sharpened to a rate.

\begin{nogo}[specific de Branges positivity conditions fail (Conrey--Li)]\label{ng:debranges}
The Hermite--Biehler / reproducing-kernel positivity that would force the zeros onto the line by
Hilbert-space structure is not available in the form required.
\end{nogo}
\noindent The Cayley map recasts RH as ``the structure function $E_\xi$ of $\Xi$ is Hermite--Biehler.''
The archimedean factor $\pi^{-(1/4+iz/2)}\Gamma(1/4+iz/2)$ is \emph{not} an entire Hermite--Biehler
function (its $\Gamma$-poles lie at $z=i(2n+\tfrac12)$ in the upper half-plane), and a product
decomposition $\Xi=E_\xi E_\xi^\#$ would double the real zeros; the positivity must be handled at the
kernel level, not by a scalar factorization. Conrey and Li \cite{ConreyLi} exhibited counterexamples to
the specific positivity conditions that de Branges required for RH, so the positive-kernel route does not
deliver the Hypothesis for free: it is the Weil-positivity wall (W\ref{ob:weil}) in de Branges
coordinates. The surviving reduction is index-theoretic --- one needs the shorted primitive kernels to
converge to the genuine Kre\u{\i}n--Langer kernel of $\Xi$ (W\ref{ob:uniform}); what is known about that
convergence is recorded in the classification at W\ref{ob:uniform}.

\section{The structural walls}\label{sec:walls}

The no-gos of \S\ref{sec:nogos} are many, but they are not independent. Beneath them lies a small number of
\emph{structural walls}: obstructions that reappear, in disguise, on every front. We state them as numbered
\textbf{Wall}s, and we attach to each an explicit logical classification, so
that no wall claims more than what is proved about it.

\paragraph{Classification of obstructions.} Each wall (and each no-go it distils) is tagged with one or
more of the following classes, where $C$ denotes the condition the wall isolates:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2.2em]
\item[$E(C)$] \emph{exact equivalence}: $C\Longleftrightarrow\mathrm{RH}$ is proved.
\item[$S(C)$] \emph{sufficient}: $C\Longrightarrow\mathrm{RH}$ is proved.
\item[$N(C)$] \emph{necessary}: $\mathrm{RH}\Longrightarrow C$ is proved.
\item[$F(X;C)$] \emph{framework closure obstruction}: within an explicitly stated framework $X$, closure
of the route is proved equivalent to the condition $C$.  The class $F$ by itself asserts nothing about the
difficulty of $C$; it asserts \emph{here is exactly where this framework terminates}.  The relation of $C$
to RH is then classified separately as $E$, $S$, or $N$ whenever such a relation is proved, and composite
tags such as $F(X;C)+E(C)$ are used.
\item[$I(\mathcal I;C)$] \emph{insufficiency theorem}: an explicitly delimited class of inputs
$\mathcal I$ is proved not to suffice for the target $C$ ($\mathcal I\not\Rightarrow C$).
\end{itemize}
Informally we shall sometimes say a statement is ``RH-strength''; as declared in
\S\ref{sec:history}, this phrase carries no logical content beyond the explicit classification attached to
the statement.  The umbrella under which all the walls sit is the oldest one.

\begin{obstruction}[the Weil-positivity wall]\label{ob:weil}
Weil's criterion \cite{Weil1952} states that the Riemann Hypothesis is equivalent to
\[
   \cW(f\star\tilde f)\ \ge\ 0\qquad\text{for all test functions }f,
\]
where $\cW$ is the explicit-formula Weil form \eqref{eq:weilform} and $\tilde f(x)=\overline{f(-x)}$. This
is a clean equivalence, not an implication: \emph{proving the positivity is proving the Hypothesis}. Every
route that reduces to a positivity statement for $\cW$ inherits this equivalence in full. The walls below
are the specific disguises this equivalence adopts as one approaches the problem from different directions.

To see why the equivalence is so sharp, expand the form in terms of the zeros directly:
\[
   \cW(f\star\tilde f)\ =\ \sum_\rho \widehat f(\gamma_\rho)\,\overline{\widehat f(\gamma_{1-\bar\rho})},
\]
where $\widehat f$ is the Fourier transform of \eqref{eq:weilform}, $\gamma_\rho=-i(\rho-\tfrac12)$, and
we write $\widehat f(\rho)$ for $\widehat f(\gamma_\rho)$ below. When $\rho$ lies on the critical line, $1-\bar\rho=\rho$ and
the term is $|\widehat f(\rho)|^2\ge0$: the zero sum is manifestly non-negative under RH. An off-line zero
at $\rho_0$ with $\Re\rho_0=\tfrac12+\delta$, $\delta>0$, contributes together with its mirror $\bar\rho_0$
and the two conjugates $1-\rho_0$, $1-\bar\rho_0$, and this four-element cluster introduces an indefinite
contribution to the form:
\[
   \cW\big|_{\text{cluster}}\ =\ 2\,\Re\bigl[\widehat f(\rho_0)\,\overline{\widehat f(1-\bar\rho_0)}\bigr]
   \ +\ 2\,\Re\bigl[\widehat f(1-\rho_0)\,\overline{\widehat f(\bar\rho_0)}\bigr],
\]
which can be negative for appropriate $f$. (Here, as in item~(i) of \S\ref{sec:positive}, the expected
quantitative form is $\lambda_{\min}(Q)\le -c(\delta,\sigma,J)<0$ for a localized form; no proof of this
quantitative bound is given in this paper.) That the form is positive exactly when there are no off-line
zeros is Weil's theorem.

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{Classification:} $E(C)$ with $C=$ positivity of $\cW$ on autocorrelations ---
the equivalence is classical (Weil \cite{Weil1952}), not a contribution of this paper.
\end{obstruction}

\noindent This umbrella wall is also the starting point of Part~II. The route of Part~II reduces RH to the
statement that $\KXi$ has no negative squares (ARP-P, Step~5); this statement is open and equivalent
to RH (Corollary~\ref{cor:live-equivalence}), hence to Li's criterion \cite{Li}.

\begin{obstruction}[arithmetic propagation]\label{ob:propagation}
The Euler product $\prod_p(1-p^{-s})^{-1}$ converges absolutely only for $\Re s>1$; in the strip
$\tfrac12<\Re s<1$ it converges at best conditionally, and near the zeros of $\zeta$ not at all. The zeros
are \emph{global}: not one Euler factor vanishes, so no prime ``owns'' a zero and no local arithmetic datum
at a single prime constrains the location of any zero. No estimate that uses only finitely many Euler
factors, or only bounds valid in $\Re s>1$, is known to distinguish a zero on the line from one at
distance $10^{-100}$ from it.

To see this in quantitative form, write the logarithmic derivative in $\Re s>1$:
\[
   -\frac{\zeta'}{\zeta}(s)\ =\ \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{\Lambda(n)}{n^s},\qquad \Re s>1,
\]
where $\Lambda(n)\ge0$ is the von Mangoldt function. Since $\Lambda(n)\ge0$, $|\zeta'/\zeta(\sigma+it)|\le-\zeta'/\zeta(\sigma)$ for $\sigma>1$; this bound
carries no information on the ordinates of zeros. For $\Re s<1$ the Dirichlet series diverges, and the
continuation to the strip is not given by its partial sums.

A classical consequence is the Korobov--Vinogradov zero-free region. The best unconditional result of
this type gives, for any zero $\rho=\beta_j+i\gamma_j$ of $\zeta$ with displacement $b_j=\beta_j-\tfrac12$,
\[
   b_j\ \le\ \frac{1}{2}\ -\ \frac{c}{(\log|\gamma_j|)^{2/3}(\log\log|\gamma_j|)^{1/3}}
\]
for a positive constant $c>0$. This is an upper bound on $b_j$, which is the direction RH requires
($b_j\le0$), but it tends to $\tfrac12$ and not to $0$. Remark~\ref{thm:fourway} gives a heuristic
classification: the classical mechanisms examined there that take data from $\Re s>1$ yield, in their
known form, an upper bound that does not tend to $0$, a statement about the region $\Re s\approx1$, an
aggregate count, or a reformulation equivalent to RH.

The conditional ARP-P route of Part~II stays in $\Re s>1$ for its estimates and uses a Vitali
normal-family continuation (Steps~9--11) to pass across the strip; that continuation is conditional on
the open Step~5.
