$\sum_{j=2}^{n}\binom nj(-1)^j(1-2^{-j})\zeta(j)$.  The
summand is $O_n(r^{-2})$, so the series converges absolutely for each fixed $n$.  Its discrete second
difference is
\[
  (\lambda_{n+2}^{\rm arch}-\lambda_{n+1}^{\rm arch})
  -(\lambda_{n+1}^{\rm arch}-\lambda_n^{\rm arch})
  =\sum_{\substack{r\ge1\\r\ \mathrm{odd}}}
     \frac{(1-1/r)^n}{r^2}>0.
\]
Thus the first differences are strictly increasing.  Evaluating the finite expression in
$\zeta(2),\ldots,\zeta(8)$ gives
\[
  0.0062<\lambda_4^{\rm arch}-\lambda_3^{\rm arch}<0.0063,
  \qquad -0.356<\lambda_7^{\rm arch}<-0.355,
  \qquad 0.020<\lambda_8^{\rm arch}<0.022.
\]
(the exact values are $0.006260\ldots$, $-0.355731\ldots$, $0.020900\ldots$).  Hence the sequence is strictly increasing from $n=3$ onward, is negative
through $n=7$, and is positive from $n=8$ onward.  Direct substitution gives
$\lambda_1^{\rm arch}<0$ and $\lambda_2^{\rm arch}<0$, completing the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[the detection mechanism, exhibited]\label{rmk:li-detection}
The obstruction is a single geometric term.  A putative off-line zero $\rho_0$ ($\Re\rho_0\ne\tfrac12$)
has a functional-equation partner with $|w|>1$, and its quartet contributes
$-\,2\Re w^n+\cdots$ with $|w|^n$ growing geometrically; for large $n$ this overturns the
$\tfrac n2\log n$ trend and drives $\lambda_n<0$.  Injecting a low off-line quartet at
$\rho_0=0.9+3i$ (with $\max|w|=1.0434$ over the quartet) into the true $\lambda_n$ turns it negative
first at $n=97$ in a floating-point computation ($\lambda_{97}^{\rm inj}=-5.95$ against the true $\lambda_{97}\approx113.4$), while an on-line
control $\rho_0=\tfrac12+3i$ keeps every $\lambda_n>0$ (it only adds $4\sin^2$).  In this example
positivity is destroyed by the geometric growth $|w|>1$ of an off-line zero.  Exactly,
\[
   \lambda_n\ge0
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \lambda_n^{\rm prime}\ge-\lambda_n^{\rm arch}.
\]
The stronger absolute estimate
$|\lambda_n^{\rm prime}|<\lambda_n^{\rm arch}$ is only a sufficient strategy when
$\lambda_n^{\rm arch}>0$; it is not equivalent to $\Omega_7$.  For the split used here,
$\lambda_n^{\rm arch}<0$ exactly for $1\le n\le7$ and is positive for $n\ge8$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:li-arch-sign}).
\end{remark}

\subsubsection*{Which inputs cannot close $\Omega_7$: an insufficiency proposition and the height--range correspondence}

We consider bounding $\lambda_n^{\rm prime}$ against $\lambda_n^{\rm arch}$ for all $n$ using inputs
weaker than RH.  The outcome is an \emph{input-class restriction}, not an impossibility: it rules out
one family of hypotheses (zero-location statements weaker than RH) and leaves a direct arithmetic
argument open.

\begin{proposition}[insufficiency of zero-location inputs]\label{prop:unboundable}
If $\xi$ has a zero off the critical line then $\lambda_n<0$ for some $n$ \cite{Li} (see
\cite{BombieriLagarias1999} for the version for multisets of points).  Consequently, an argument that
uses about the zeros only a constraint satisfied by some multiset with a point off the line (for
instance the classical zero-free region $\beta<1-c/\log(|\gamma|+2)$ or a zero-density estimate
$N(\sigma,T)\ll T^{a(1-\sigma)}$, applied to an abstract multiset satisfying the Bombieri--Lagarias
conditions) cannot yield $\lambda_n\ge0$ for all $n$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof sketch, with attribution]
The full statement is Bombieri--Lagarias \cite{BombieriLagarias1999} applied to the zero multiset of
$\xi$; we record the mechanism for a single dominant quartet.  By the functional equation and
conjugation, $\rho_0$ belongs to a quartet containing a zero $\rho$ with $\Re\rho<\tfrac12$, for which
$r:=|w_\rho|=|\rho-1|/|\rho|>1$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:li-sos}).  Its quartet contributes
$4-2(r^n+r^{-n})\cos(n\varphi)$, $\varphi=\arg w_\rho$, of amplitude $2r^n$ exceeding, for large
$n$ along a positive-density set where $\cos(n\varphi)\ge\tfrac12$ (Weyl equidistribution if
$\varphi/2\pi$ is irrational, periodicity if rational), both the trend
$\lambda_n^{\rm arch}\sim\tfrac n2\log\tfrac n{2\pi}$ and the contribution of the remaining zeros
\emph{provided} $\rho$ realizes the maximal $|w|$ alone.  When several off-line zeros share the maximal
modulus, cancellations among their phases must be excluded; this is exactly what the
Bombieri--Lagarias argument handles in general, and we rely on their theorem rather than the sketch for
the full claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[scope: what this does and does not say]\label{rmk:unboundable-scope}
Proposition~\ref{prop:unboundable} restricts the \emph{input class}: $\Omega_7$ cannot be closed by
citing a zero-location statement weaker than RH, because any such statement tolerates the zero that
destroys positivity.  It does \emph{not} say that $\Omega_7$ is unprovable, and the slogan ``the only
sufficient input is RH itself'' would be circular: since $\lambda_n\ge0\ \forall n$ is
\emph{equivalent} to RH \cite{Li}, any proof of $\Omega_7$ tautologically yields RH --- that is the
goal of the chain, not an obstruction to it.  The open route is the one the decomposition itself
exhibits: prove $\lambda_n^{\rm prime}\ge-\lambda_n^{\rm arch}$ directly on the \emph{prime side}
(explicit-formula / Weil-positivity inputs, test-function constructions, or operator-theoretic
arguments), none of which passes through a zero-location hypothesis and
none of which is excluded here.  $\Omega_7$ therefore remains \textbf{open}, with
Proposition~\ref{prop:unboundable} serving only to exclude certain input families.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[height--range heuristic for a single quartet]\label{rmk:height-range}
The geometric growth is slow for high zeros, and this is quantitative.  A putative off-line zero at
height $\gamma$ and displacement $\delta=\tfrac12-\beta$ has
$\log|w_\rho|=\dfrac{\delta}{\beta^2+\gamma^2}\,(1+o(1))$, so it can force $\lambda_n<0$ only once
$r^n\gtrsim\tfrac12\lambda_n^{\rm arch}$, i.e.\ for
\[
   n\ \gtrsim\ n_*(\gamma,\delta)\ \approx\ \frac{\beta^2+\gamma^2}{\delta}\,
   \log\!\Bigl(\tfrac n4\log\tfrac n{2\pi}\Bigr).
\]
This is a heuristic.  For the quartet of Remark~\ref{rmk:li-detection} ($\gamma=3$, $\delta=0.4$)
the first sign change is at $n=97$, where $n\log|w|=4.13\approx\log(\lambda_{97}/2)$.  This threshold concerns a single prescribed quartet; it gives no lower bound for the
contribution of all zeros above a height $H$, so it does not show $\lambda_n>0$ in any range of $n$.
No height--range statement enters the A1 closure chain.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection*{The prime side made exact: $\Omega_7$ as an inequality about an explicit prime sum}

Remark~\ref{rmk:unboundable-scope} leaves one route open: establish the one-sided lower bound for
$\lambda_n^{\rm prime}$ directly on the prime side.  A stronger absolute estimate is a sufficient
strategy for $n\ge8$, but it is not the exact target.  The first step is to make
$\lambda_n^{\rm prime}$ an \emph{explicit} prime sum.

\begin{proposition}[Laguerre kernel of the prime side]\label{prop:prime-kernel}
For every $n\ge1$ and $x>0$,
\[
   n\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n-1}{k-1}\frac{(-x)^k}{k!}\;=\;-\,x\,L^{(1)}_{n-1}(x),
\]
with $L^{(1)}_{n-1}$ the generalized Laguerre polynomial.  Consequently, for $\Re s_0>1$,
applying the Li binomial transform to the Taylor coefficients
$\sigma_k^{\zeta}(s_0)=[(s-s_0)^k]\log\zeta(s)$ gives the absolutely convergent representation
\[
   T_n(s_0)\;:=\;n\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n-1}{k-1}\,\sigma_k^{\zeta}(s_0)
   \;=\;-\sum_{m\ge2}\frac{\Lambda(m)}{m^{s_0}}\,L^{(1)}_{n-1}(\log m),
\]
and the prime part of the Li coefficient is the boundary value
\[
   \lambda_n^{\rm prime}
   \;=\;\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\Bigl[\;
   n\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n-1}{k-1}\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k\,\varepsilon^{k}}
   \;-\;\sum_{m\ge2}\frac{\Lambda(m)}{m^{1+\varepsilon}}\,L^{(1)}_{n-1}(\log m)\Bigr],
\]
the divergences of the pole and prime pieces cancelling in the limit.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since $L^{(1)}_{n-1}(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\binom{n}{i+1}\frac{(-x)^i}{i!}$, we get
$-xL^{(1)}_{n-1}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}k\binom nk\frac{(-x)^k}{k!}$, and $k\binom nk=n\binom{n-1}{k-1}$,
which is the kernel identity; it is applied to $\log\zeta(s)=\sum_m\Lambda(m)(\log m)^{-1}m^{-s_0}e^{-(s-s_0)\log m}$
with $x=\log m$.  Integrating term by term,
$\int_0^\infty e^{-\varepsilon u}L^{(1)}_{n-1}(u)\,du=\sum_{k=1}^n\binom nk\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{\varepsilon^k}=n\sum_{k=1}^n\binom{n-1}{k-1}\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k\varepsilon^k}$,
which identifies the pole term with $\int_1^\infty y^{-1-\varepsilon}L^{(1)}_{n-1}(\log y)\,dy$.  The representation for $T_n(s_0)$ is then term-by-term rearrangement of
the absolutely convergent double sum ($\Re s_0>1$).  The boundary statement is the same computation for
$\log\bigl((s-1)\zeta(s)\bigr)=\log(s-1)+\log\zeta(s)$ at $s_0=1+\varepsilon$, whose left side is
regular at $\varepsilon=0$ with Taylor data the Stieltjes constants, and the Taylor coefficients
at $s_0=1+\varepsilon$ converge to those at $1$ as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[what the representation buys, and what it costs]\label{rmk:prime-kernel-shape}
$\Omega_7$ is now the single explicit one-sided inequality
\[
   \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\bigl[\text{pole}_n(\varepsilon)-
   \textstyle\sum_m\Lambda(m)m^{-1-\varepsilon}L^{(1)}_{n-1}(\log m)\bigr]
   \;\ge\;-\lambda_n^{\rm arch}\qquad(\forall n),
\]
with two structural assets not available to zero-location inputs: the weights $\Lambda(m)\ge0$ are
nonnegative, and the kernel $L^{(1)}_{n-1}$ is classical (Plancherel--Rotach: oscillatory for
$\log m\lesssim4n$, monotone beyond).  The cost is equally visible: the kernel's effective support
reaches $m\approx e^{4n}$, so the inequality demands cancellation in $\psi(y)-y$ over exponentially
long ranges --- heuristically, at $\varepsilon=0$ the required cancellation must come from the oscillation of the prime counting error against the Laguerre
oscillation.  This is the precise prime-side face of $\Omega_7$.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection*{Pointwise bounds on the prime error}

With Proposition~\ref{prop:prime-kernel} in hand, a natural first argument is partial summation.  Set
\[
   f_{n,\varepsilon}(y)=y^{-1-\varepsilon}L^{(1)}_{n-1}(\log y),
   \qquad f_{n,\varepsilon}(1)=n.
\]
The pole term is the average-$\Lambda$ compensation
$\mathrm{pole}_n(\varepsilon)=\int_1^\infty f_{n,\varepsilon}(y)\,dy$.  Keeping the pole and prime
pieces paired and integrating by parts as Stieltjes integrals gives
\[
   \lambda_n^{\rm prime}
   =-\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\int_{1^-}^\infty
       f_{n,\varepsilon}(y)\,d(\psi(y)-y)
   =\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\left[
       -n+\int_1^\infty(\psi(y)-y)f'_{n,\varepsilon}(y)\,dy
     \right].
\]
Equivalently,
\[
   \lambda_n^{\rm prime}
   =\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\int_1^\infty
      (\psi(y)-y+1)f'_{n,\varepsilon}(y)\,dy .
\]
The boundary term $-n$ follows from $\psi(1)=0$ and cannot be discarded.  We then insert a pointwise
bound $|\psi(y)-y|\le B(y)$.  In floating-point computations (unverified) we evaluated the resulting ceiling
$A_n[B]:=\int_1^\infty B(y)\,\bigl|\tfrac{d}{dy}[L^{(1)}_{n-1}(\log y)/y]\bigr|\,dy$ against
$\lambda_n^{\rm arch}$ (true $|\psi(y)-y|$ to $10^7$, tails by model; the model
$B(y)=\sqrt y\log^2y/(8\pi)$ is Schoenfeld's bound under RH \cite{Schoenfeld1976}, valid for
$y\ge73.2$, so the column using it on all of $y\ge1$ is only a model).  With $u=\log y$ the
model column is the closed form
$A_n=\frac1{8\pi}\int_0^\infty u^2e^{-u/2}\bigl|L^{(1)\prime}_{n-1}(u)-L^{(1)}_{n-1}(u)\bigr|\,du$,
whose evaluation gives $37.83$, $311.36$, $1178.6$ for $n=8,20,35$, reproducing that column; the
other two $A_n$ columns are unverified floating-point computations.  The archimedean values are the
exact $\lambda_n^{\rm arch}$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:li-arch-sign}:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{r|rrrr}
$n$ & $\lambda_n^{\rm arch}$ (exact) & $A_n$ (true$+$RH tail) & $A_n$ (RH model $\tfrac{\sqrt y\log^2y}{8\pi}$) & $A_n$ (VK shape) \\\hline
 8 & $0.021$ & $45.3$ & $37.8$ & $2.8\times10^{11}$\\
20 & $8.10$ & $334$  & $311$  & $4.6\times10^{23}$\\
35 & $23.4$ & $1221$ & $1178$ & $1.0\times10^{33}$\\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{remark}[second insufficiency: pointwise bounds on $\psi(y)-y$]\label{rmk:pointwise-insufficient}
The triangle inequality applied to the identity above gives only
$\lambda_n^{\rm prime}\ge-n-A_n[B]$.  Thus a pointwise argument of this form would require
$n+A_n[B]\le\lambda_n^{\rm arch}$.  For $n=8,20,35$ the computed $A_n[B]$ exceeds $\lambda_n^{\rm arch}$, also for the
pointwise bound $B(y)=\sqrt y\,\log^2y/(8\pi)$ conditional on RH, and the three values fit
$A_n\approx0.27\,n^2\log n$.  With unconditional Vinogradov--Korobov shapes the computed $A_n$ is
far larger.  Heuristically, partial summation with absolute values overshoots by a factor of order
$n$, and closing $\Omega_7$ would require extracting a factor of order $n$ of sign-cancellation from the oscillation of $\psi(y)-y$ against the oscillation of the
Laguerre kernel (both oscillate in $\log m\lesssim4n$).  The nonnegativity $\Lambda(m)\ge0$ is considered next.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection*{Nonnegativity of the von Mangoldt weights in whitened coordinates}

We now consider the nonnegativity of the von Mangoldt weights.  At an
interior gauge $z_0=t_0-iy$ with $y>\tfrac12$ (so $\Re s_0=\tfrac12+y>1$) the prime jet decomposes
absolutely per prime power, $\mathcal P_\Lambda=\sum_m P_m$, with $P_m=\Lambda(m)P_m^{(1)}$, where
$P_m^{(1)}$ is the jet matrix of $m^{-1/2}(S_{\log m}+S_{\log m}^*)$ (decomposition cross-checked against the exact $\zeta$-derivative jet:
error $3\times10^{-4}$ at $N=4$, $2.5\times10^{-2}$ at $N=8$; $N=12$ is truncation-limited at
$X=2\times10^6$).  Whitening by $S=A_N^{-1/2}$, we computed (floating point, unverified) the per-term ceiling
$S_{\rm abs}(N)=\sum_m\Lambda(m)\,\|S P_m^{(1)}S\|$ (the best any term-by-term absolute argument can
achieve using only $\Lambda\ge0$) against the computed one-sided extremes of $S\mathcal P_\Lambda S$; the last column is
$I=S_{\rm abs}/\max(|\lambda_{\max}|,|\lambda_{\min}|)$:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{rr|rrrr}
$t_0$ & $N$ & $\lambda_{\max}(S\mathcal P S)$ & $\lambda_{\min}$ & $S_{\rm abs}$ & interference $I$ \\\hline
10 & 4 & $0.99999993$ & $-0.097$ & $219$ & $2.2\times10^2$\\
10 & 8 & $1-10^{-12}$ & $-0.369$ & $1.9\times10^4$ & $1.9\times10^4$\\
30 & 4 & $0.9982$ & $-3.34$ & $59.5$ & $17.8$\\
30 & 8 & $1-2\times10^{-12}$ & $-7.62$ & $4.5\times10^3$ & $5.8\times10^2$\\
30 & 12 & $1^{-}$ & $-11.90$ & $\gtrsim2.9\times10^5$ & $\gtrsim2.5\times10^4$\\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{remark}[third insufficiency: observations]\label{rmk:whitened-interference}
In the computed cases ($t_0=10,30$; $N\le12$) $\lambda_{\max}(S\mathcal P_\Lambda S)$ is within
$10^{-12}$ of $1$ for $N\ge8$, the per-term sum $S_{\rm abs}$ grows rapidly with $N$, and
$\lambda_{\min}$ decreases roughly linearly.  These suggest, but do not prove, that arguments bounding
prime-power contributions separately (using only $\Lambda(m)\ge0$), or two-sided norm bounds
(contraction arguments, unitary dilations of $\mathcal T$), cannot give $\lambda_{\max}\le1$; the
positivity would then have to come from cross-prime interference, as in the decoherence mechanism of
Remark~\ref{rmk:H1-matrix}.
\end{remark}



\subsection{The direct route: the far-tail theorem A0, the compact inequality A1, and a weighted gate}
\label{subsec:omega7-a0a1}

The preceding discussion identifies $\Omega_7$ with the uniform Li inequalities.  We now record the
further reduction obtained by keeping the pole, the prime powers, the Gamma factor, and every endpoint
paired until after the cutoff is taken.  The algebraic reduction is exact, but the unconditional sign
theorem remains open; the remaining inequality implies RH by Li's criterion and is therefore at least as strong as RH; the converse $\RH\Rightarrow\mathrm{A1}$ is known only for large $n$.  We distinguish throughout between the \emph{minimal compact target} A1 and the stronger
\emph{strong-margin} target used in several later arguments.

\begin{remark}[on the sign of the incomplete Li block]\label{rmk:lagarias-sign-erratum}
Lagarias~\cite[Theorem 1.1, (1.17); Theorem 6.1, (6.2); (6.7)]{Lagarias2007} states
$\lambda_n=A_n-\lambda_n(\sqrt n)+O(\sqrt n\log n)$ in the present normalization, where
$\lambda_n(T):=\sum_{|\Im\rho|<T}(1-(1-1/\rho)^n)$ (counted with multiplicity).  In the residue step
preceding (6.7) the residue of $k_n(s)(-L'/L)(s+1)$ at $s=\rho-1$ is $-m_\rho k_n(\rho-1)$, which gives
the opposite sign; we record the computation as a proof sketch, and nothing below depends on it.  (The
comparison was made with the arXiv version, math/0404394.)  Here $L$ is the completed $L$-function
of an automorphic representation $\pi$ (for us $\zeta$), $\pi^\vee$ its contragredient,
$\lambda_n(T,\pi^\vee)$ the analogue of $\lambda_n(T)$, $F_n(s)=k_n(s)(-L'/L)(s+1)$ the integrand,
$C_2$ the outer contour and $S_f(n,\pi)$ the finite-prime (Dirichlet-series) part of the outer
integral, all as in the source.  For
$k_n(s)=(1+1/s)^n-1$, at $s=\rho-1$ one has
\[
\operatorname*{Res}_{s=\rho-1}
 k_n(s)\left(-\frac{L'}L(s+1)\right)=-m_\rho k_n(\rho-1).
\]
Both the small and large contours in \S6 are counterclockwise, so the outer
integral minus the inner integral is the sum of these residues.  Moreover the
functional-equation symmetry used there gives
\[
 \sum_{|\Im\rho|<T}m_\rho k_n(\rho-1)=-\lambda_n(T,\pi^\vee).
\]
Consequently, with normalized outer integral \(\widehat I_2\), the correct
assembly is
\[
 \widehat I_2=S_f(n,\pi)+\lambda_n(T,\pi^\vee)+O(1),
\]
with a plus sign in front of $\lambda_n(T,\pi^\vee)$.  Keeping this sign gives, in the zeta normalization,
\[
 \lambda_n=A_n+\lambda_n(\sqrt n)+O(\sqrt n\log n).
\]
For the normalization we set
$\widehat I_2=(2\pi i)^{-1}\int_{C_2}F_n(s)\,ds$.  On a horizontal line at height
$T=\sqrt n+\varepsilon_n$ the geometric estimate has available ratio $e/2>1$, so we take instead
$T=2\sqrt n+\varepsilon_n$.  With $G_n=k_n+1$,
\[
 {G_n(s+1)\over G_n(s)}=\left(1-{1\over(s+1)^2}\right)^n,
 \qquad {e^{1/4}\over2}<1,
\]
where the factor $1/2$ is applied to the absolute Dirichlet-series majorant of $L'/L$, not to a
quotient of its values.  This sketches $\widehat I_2=O(\sqrt n\log n)$.  Finally, on
$\sqrt n\le|\gamma|\le2\sqrt n+1$ one has
$|1-1/\rho|^n\le e^{1/2}$ and there are $O(\sqrt n\log n)$ zeros, so returning to the exact cutoff
$\sqrt n$ costs only $O(\sqrt n\log n)$.
Thus the small remainder furnished by this contour argument (proof sketch) is
\[
 \widetilde\varepsilon_n:=A_n-\lambda_n+\lambda_n(\sqrt n)
       =O(\sqrt n\log n),                                  \tag{L$'$}
\]
whereas
\[
 \varepsilon_n^-:=A_n-\lambda_n-\lambda_n(\sqrt n)
 =\widetilde\varepsilon_n-2\lambda_n(\sqrt n)
\]
is merely an exact bookkeeping identity and has no known unconditional
$O(\sqrt n\log n)$ bound, so no finite-range statement is based on a bound for $\varepsilon_n^-$.

Nothing in Proposition~\ref{prop:li-compact-tail}, A0, or A1 below uses this incomplete-zero
decomposition.  The exact A1 coordinate, if one elects to display the remainder $\widetilde\varepsilon_n$, is
\[
 -\lambda_n(\sqrt n)+\widetilde\varepsilon_n+R_n(T_n)
 \le\frac34A_n,
\]
which is identically $A_n-\lambda_n+R_n(T_n)\le3A_n/4$.  Hence the sign question does not
affect the direct prime--Laguerre inequality.
\end{remark}

Put
\[
 A_n:=\lambda_n^{\rm arch},\qquad
 E(u):=\psi(e^u)-e^u,\qquad
 K_n(u):=e^{-u}L_{n-1}^{(2)}(u),
 \qquad a:=\log2.
\]
For $T>0$ define the compact coefficient and the paired tail by
\begin{align}
 C_n(T)&:=\frac34A_n-n-\int_0^T E(u)K_n(u)\,du, \label{eq:compact-a1-def}\\
 R_n(T)&:=-\int_T^\infty E(u)K_n(u)\,du.       \label{eq:a0-tail-def}
\end{align}
The integrals are understood through the pole--prime Abel regularization already used in
Proposition~\ref{prop:prime-kernel}; at every finite $T$ the first integral is an ordinary integral of
the right-continuous Chebyshev discrepancy.

\begin{proposition}[exact Li--compact--tail assembly]\label{prop:li-compact-tail}
For every $n\ge1$ and every admissible cutoff $T$,
\[
 \boxed{\quad C_n(T)=\lambda_n-\frac14A_n-R_n(T).\quad}       \tag{A}
\]
Consequently, if $A_n>0$, $|R_n(T_n)|\le A_n/4$, and $C_n(T_n)\ge0$, then
$\lambda_n\ge0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The Stieltjes integration by parts preceding Remark~\ref{rmk:pointwise-insufficient}, with
$\varepsilon\downarrow0$ taken under the integral by dominated convergence (the bound on
$f'_{n,\varepsilon}$ in the proof of Lemma~\ref{thm:a0-tail-direct} and the prime number theorem),
gives
\[
 \lambda_n^{\rm prime}
 =-n-\int_0^\infty E(u)e^{-u}L_{n-1}^{(2)}(u)\,du.
\]
Split the integral at $T$ and use
$\lambda_n=A_n+\lambda_n^{\rm prime}$ together with
\eqref{eq:compact-a1-def}--\eqref{eq:a0-tail-def}.  This gives (A) without a
limit interchange or an unpaired divergent term.  The last assertion follows from
\[
 \lambda_n=C_n(T_n)+\frac14A_n+R_n(T_n)
 \ge C_n(T_n).\qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

The far tail contains no RH-strength input.  The exact version needed here is the following.

\begin{lemma}[A0: uniform far-tail theorem]\label{thm:a0-tail-direct}
Assume a Vinogradov--Korobov prime-number-theorem estimate
\[
 |\psi(y)-y|\le Ay\exp[-\eta(\log y)]\qquad(y\ge e^{U_0}),
 \quad \frac{\eta(u)}{\log(1+u)}\longrightarrow\infty,
\]
and let $0<\beta_n\le A_n$ for $n\ge8$.  If $T_n\ge U_0$ is chosen so that
\[
 \eta(u)\ge(n+1)\log(1+u)+\log\frac{12An^2}{\beta_n}
 \qquad(u\ge T_n),                                      \tag{B}
\]
then
\[
 \boxed{\quad |R_n(T_n)|\le\frac14A_n\qquad(n\ge8).\quad} \tag{A0}
\]
One may take $\beta_n=A_n$: by Lemma~\ref{lem:li-arch-sign}, $A_n>0$ for every $n\ge8$
(numerically $A_7=-0.3557\ldots$, $A_8=0.0209\ldots$).
\end{lemma}

Such constants $A,U_0$ and such an $\eta$ are supplied by the classical
Vinogradov--Korobov prime-number theorem \cite{Vinogradov1958,Korobov}; explicit constants are
given by Ford~\cite{Ford2002}.  Their numerical optimization is not
needed for the logical implication below: for each fixed $n$, the growth condition on $\eta$ guarantees
the existence of a finite $T_n$.  Inserting a chosen published numerical triple only makes that cutoff
computable.

\begin{proof}
The finite Laguerre expansion gives, uniformly for $u\ge0$,
\[
 |L_{n-1}^{(1)}(u)|\le n(1+u)^{n-1},\qquad
 |(L_{n-1}^{(1)})'(u)|\le n^2(1+u)^{n-2}.
\]
Equivalently, for the regularized kernel
$f_{n,\varepsilon}(y)=y^{-1-\varepsilon}L_{n-1}^{(1)}(\log y)$,
\[
 |f'_{n,\varepsilon}(y)|\le3n^2y^{-2}(1+\log y)^{n-1}
 \qquad(0\le\varepsilon\le1).
\]
After $u=\log y$, condition (B) therefore gives
\[
 |R_n(T_n)|
 \le3An^2\int_{T_n}^\infty(1+u)^{n-1}e^{-\eta(u)}\,du
 \le\frac{\beta_n}{4}\int_{T_n}^\infty(1+u)^{-2}\,du
 \le\frac14A_n.
\]
The choice $\beta_n=A_n$ is admissible by Lemma~\ref{lem:li-arch-sign}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[retained A0 factor and cubic sufficient margin]
\label{cor:a0-retained-cubic-margin}
Under the hypotheses and notation of Lemma~\ref{thm:a0-tail-direct}, the
proof gives the sharper bound
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 |R_n(T_n)|\le \frac{\beta_n}{4(1+T_n)}
              \le \frac{A_n}{4(1+T_n)}.\quad}       \tag{A0$^+$}
\]
Consequently, if $T_n\ge2$ and
\[
 D_n^{[3]}:=3\lambda_n-A_n\ge0,
\]
then $C_n(T_n)\ge0$, equivalently the compact inequality A1 holds
at index $n$.  More generally, $D_n^{[r]}:=r\lambda_n-A_n\ge0$ is
sufficient whenever
\[
 1<r\le {4(1+T_n)\over T_n+2}.                    \tag{A0$^{++}$}
\]
For the fixed cutoff $T_n\ge1000$, the uniform choice
\[
 r_*={4\cdot1001\over1002}={2002\over501}
\]
is therefore sufficient for every index.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The first assertion retains the exact value
$\int_{T_n}^{\infty}(1+u)^{-2}\,du=(1+T_n)^{-1}$ in the penultimate line of
the preceding proof.  If $D_n^{[3]}\ge0$, then $\lambda_n\ge A_n/3$; hence
Proposition~\ref{prop:li-compact-tail} and (A0$^+$) give
\[
 C_n(T_n)
 =\lambda_n-\frac14A_n-R_n(T_n)
 \ge \frac1{12}A_n-\frac{A_n}{4(1+T_n)}
 =\frac{A_n(T_n-2)}{12(1+T_n)}\ge0.
\]
For general $r$, the same calculation gives
\[
 C_n(T_n)\ge A_n\left({1\over r}-{1\over4}
                    -{1\over4(1+T_n)}\right),
\]
which is nonnegative precisely in the range (A0$^{++}$).  The function
$4(1+T)/(T+2)$ is increasing, so $T_n\ge1000$ gives the stated fixed value
$r_*$.
For an abstract cutoff one may replace $T_n$ by $\max\{T_n,2\}$, since
condition (B) is preserved when the cutoff is increased.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[cofinal A0 freedom and the strict quarter margin]
\label{cor:a0-cofinal-quarter-margin}
Fix $n\ge8$, and let $T_n^0$ be any cutoff satisfying condition \emph{(B)}.
Then (A0$^+$) holds, with $T$ in place of $T_n$, for every $T\ge T_n^0$.
If
\[
 \delta_n:=\lambda_n-\frac14A_n>0,
\]
then every choice
\[
 T\ge\max\left\{T_n^0,{A_n\over4\delta_n}\right\}          \tag{A0$^{\rm cof}$}
\]
satisfies $C_n(T)\ge0$.  Consequently
\[
 \forall n\ge150:\quad4\lambda_n>A_n                       \tag{A0$^{\rm q}$}
\]
is sufficient for A1 after choosing an admissible cutoff separately at each
index.  The value $2002/501$ in the preceding corollary is therefore only
the uniform coefficient obtained from the raw floor $T\ge1000$; it is not
an intrinsic constant of the direct route.

Moreover, $4\lambda_n-A_n\ge0$ for every $n\ge150$, together with the
$\lambda_n>0$ for $n<150$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:a1-finite-conditional}(i) for $9\le n\le149$;
classical numerical values for $n\le8$), already implies RH directly by Li's criterion.
The strict inequality in (A0$^{\rm q}$) is needed only for this particular
finite-cutoff transfer to A1: equality leaves no positive $\delta_n$ with
which to choose (A0$^{\rm cof}$).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Condition (B) is preserved when the lower endpoint is increased, so the
proof of A0$^+$ gives
\[
 |R_n(T)|\le {A_n\over4(1+T)}\qquad(T\ge T_n^0).
\]
Under (A0$^{\rm cof}$) the right side is at most $\delta_n$, and (A) gives
$C_n(T)=\delta_n-R_n(T)\ge0$.  The final assertion uses $A_n>0$ and hence
$\lambda_n\ge A_n/4>0$.
\end{proof}

For a cutoff fixed in advance this lowers the sufficient margin strictly: the stronger condition
$2\lambda_n-A_n\ge0$ implies $D_n^{[3]}>0$, and the cubic target implies
$D_n^{[r_*]}>0$, whereas neither converse algebraic implication holds.  It
does not by itself prove the fixed-cutoff margin $r_*\lambda_n\ge A_n$, which is at least as strong as RH.

The remaining compact inequality collapses further because there are no prime powers below $2$.

\begin{theorem}[exact A1 collapse]\label{thm:a1-direct-collapse}
For every $n\ge8$ and every A0 cutoff $T_n$,
\[
 C_n(T_n)\ge0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \boxed{\quad
 \int_{\log2}^{T_n}\!E(u)e^{-u}L_{n-1}^{(2)}(u)\,du
 \le q_n,
 \quad}                                                     \tag{A1}
\]
where the reserve is the completely explicit quantity
\[
 q_n:=\frac34A_n+1-L_n^{(1)}(\log2).                         \tag{C}
\]
Moreover
\[
 q_n=\frac38n\bigl(\log n+\gamma-1-\log(2\pi)\bigr)
       +O(n^{1/4}+\log n)
     =\frac38n\log n\,(1+o(1)).                             \tag{D}
\]
No base reserve, pole term, or exponentially large endpoint contribution remains in (A1).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $0\le u<a$ one has $\psi(e^u)=0$, hence $E(u)=-e^u$.  Since
$(L_n^{(1)})'=-L_{n-1}^{(2)}$ and $L_n^{(1)}(0)=n+1$,
\[
 \int_0^aE(u)K_n(u)\,du
 =-\int_0^aL_{n-1}^{(2)}(u)\,du
 =-(n+1)+L_n^{(1)}(a).
\]
Substitution in \eqref{eq:compact-a1-def} proves (A1)--(C).  In the equivalent
Stieltjes form, the moving-cutoff weight is
\[
 \omega_n(u)=e^{-T_n}L_{n-1}^{(1)}(T_n)-e^{-u}L_{n-1}^{(1)}(u),
 \qquad \omega_n(T_n)=0,
\]
so a prime power at the upper endpoint contributes zero; the identity is convention-free there.
Finally, the odd-harmonic formula and standard fixed-argument Laguerre asymptotics give
\[
 A_n=\frac n2\bigl(\log n+\gamma-1-\log(2\pi)\bigr)+O(\log n),
 \qquad L_n^{(1)}(a)=O_a(n^{1/4}),
\]
which proves (D).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[proved forward chain from A1 to $\Omega_7$]\label{cor:a1-to-omega7}
Assume the classical explicit PNT input of Lemma~\ref{thm:a0-tail-direct}, the numerical
inequalities $\lambda_n>0$ for $1\le n\le7$, and (A1) for every $n\ge8$.
Then
\[
 \lambda_n\ge0\quad(n\ge1),\qquad \Omega_7\ \text{holds},\qquad \textnormal{RH}\ \text{holds}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $n\ge8$, combine Theorem~\ref{thm:a1-direct-collapse},
Lemma~\ref{thm:a0-tail-direct}, and Proposition~\ref{prop:li-compact-tail}.  Add the seven numerically
checked base indices and apply the Li criterion.
\end{proof}

There is a useful stronger gate.  Define
\[
 D_n:=2\lambda_n-A_n.
\]
Then $D_n\ge0$ says $\lambda_n\ge A_n/2$; A0 and
Proposition~\ref{prop:li-compact-tail} imply $C_n(T_n)\ge0$.  Thus the strong margin proves A1,
but it is not the minimal compact statement and has not been proved equivalent to it.

\begin{proposition}[finite computation and effective conditional range]\label{prop:a1-finite-conditional}
The following two statements hold with their stated scopes; (i) is a computer-assisted
finite check conditional on a coefficient-tail bound, and (ii) is conditional on RH.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item A rational interval computation gives $D_n>0$ for every $9\le n\le149$; for $1\le n\le8$,
$D_n>0$ follows from the numerical values of $\lambda_n$ and $A_n$, which are not certified here.  For
$21\le n\le149$ it uses the Euler-transformed (Hasse--Sondow) series of the eta function: with
$q(t)=t\zeta(1+t)$ and
\[
 q_K(t):=\frac{t}{1-2^{-t}}\sum_{k<K}2^{-(k+1)}\sum_{j\le k}(-1)^j\binom kj(j+1)^{-1-t},
\]
it assumes the coefficient-tail bound
\[
 \bigl|[t^m](q-q_K)\bigr|
 \le\frac{(27+9\log(K+1))2^{m-K}}{K+1},
\]
for $1\le m<K$, which we have tested numerically but do not prove here, followed by outward
fixed-point logarithms and the exact logarithmic-coefficient recurrence.  Assuming this bound, a
rational interval computation gives $D_n>0$ for $21\le n\le149$.  This is a finite statement, not
an all-$n$ theorem.
\item Assuming RH, (A1) holds for all sufficiently large $n$: by (A) and (A0),
$C_n(T_n)\ge\lambda_n-\tfrac12A_n$, and under RH $\lambda_n=A_n+O(\sqrt n\log n)$
\cite[(1.18)]{Lagarias2007}, while $A_n\asymp n\log n$.  No effective threshold is claimed.
\end{enumerate}
The range $n\ge150$ below this ineffective threshold is not covered.  Hence these results do not prove the converse
$\RH\Rightarrow\mathrm{A1}$ for all indices and do not prove RH.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For (i), the coefficient enclosure of $q_K$, widened by the assumed tail bound, is propagated using only rational interval operations through
the exact identity
\[
 \lambda_n^{\rm prime}
 =\sum_{k=1}^n n\binom{n-1}{k-1}[t^k]\log(t\zeta(1+t))
\]
and the corresponding finite archimedean formula.  With $K=830$ series terms and $800$ terms in each
logarithm series, the
lower endpoints of the enclosures of $D_n/2$ are positive throughout $21\le n\le149$ (script
\texttt{scripts/fixed\_margin\_eta\_21\_149.py}, which uses
\texttt{scripts/eta\_fixed\_generator.py}, \texttt{scripts/stieltjes\_em\_interval\_pilot.py} and
\texttt{scripts/omega7\_point4\_interval\_verify.py}); the range $9\le n\le20$ is covered in the
same way by \texttt{scripts/fixed\_margin\_9\_20.py}.

Part (ii) is proved in its statement.
\end{proof}

The following representation places the strong margin directly in the Weil test space.

\begin{proposition}[completed Weil autocorrelation]\label{prop:completed-weil-autocorrelation}
Let
\[
 f_n(x)=\mathbf1_{[0,\infty)}(x)L_{n-1}^{(1)}(x),\qquad
 f_n^\#(x)=e^xf_n(-x),\qquad h_n=f_n*f_n^\#.
\]
Then, with $w=(s-1)/s$,
\[
 \mathcal Lf_n(s)=1-w^n,\qquad
 \mathcal Lh_n(s)=(1-w^n)(1-w^{-n})=2-w^n-w^{-n},
\]
and the symmetrically grouped zero side satisfies
\[
 \mathcal W(h_n)=2\lambda_n,\qquad
 \boxed{\quad D_n=\mathcal W(h_n)-A_n.\quad}                 \tag{E}
\]
On the critical line $\mathcal Lh_n(s)=|1-w^n|^2\ge0$.  A noncritical reciprocal quartet
$w=e^{-\alpha+i\theta}$ instead contributes
\[
 8-8\cosh(n\alpha)\cos(n\theta),                              \tag{F}
\]
which has both signs.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The Laplace transform of $L_{n-1}^{(1)}$ gives
$\mathcal Lf_n(s)=1-((s-1)/s)^n$.  The Weil involution sends $s$ to $1-s$ and therefore $w$ to
$w^{-1}$, proving the transform of $h_n$.  Each summand in the zero sum is $O_n(|\rho|^{-2})$, so the
functional-equation pairing is absolutely legitimate and gives
\[
 \mathcal W(h_n)=\sum_\rho(2-w_\rho^n-w_\rho^{-n})
 =2\sum_\rho^*(1-w_\rho^n)=2\lambda_n.
\]
Equation (E) follows from the definition of $D_n$; grouping a noncritical quartet gives (F).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[hard-edge fixed-vector bridge]\label{prop:hard-edge-fixed-vector}
Put
\[
 g_n(x)=e^{-x/2}\mathbf1_{[0,\infty)}(x)L_{n-1}^{(1)}(x),
 \qquad
 \phi_k(x)=e^{-x/2}\mathbf1_{[0,\infty)}(x)L_k(x).
\]
Then $\{\phi_k\}_{k\ge0}$ is orthonormal and
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 g_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\phi_k,\qquad \|g_n\|_2^2=n.
 \quad}                                                       \tag{G}
\]
Moreover
\[
 f_n*f_n^\#=f_n+f_n^\#,\qquad
 (g_n*\widetilde g_n)(a)=g_n(|a|),
\]
so, for the right-shift semigroup,
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 \langle S_ag_n,g_n\rangle
 =e^{-a/2}L_{n-1}^{(1)}(a).
 \quad}                                                       \tag{H}
\]
Consequently, if
\[
 \mathfrak p_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}[g_n]
 :=\sum_{m\ge2}{\Lambda(m)\over m^{1/2+\varepsilon}}
 \langle(S_{\log m}+S_{\log m}^*)g_n,g_n\rangle,
\]
then
\[
 A_n-\lambda_n
 =\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}
 \left[
 {1\over2}\mathfrak p_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}[g_n]
 -n\sum_{k=1}^n\binom{n-1}{k-1}{(-1)^{k-1}\over k\varepsilon^k}
 \right].                                                     \tag{I}
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The Laguerre identity
$L_{n-1}^{(1)}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}L_k$ proves (G).  If
$F_n(s)=1-((s-1)/s)^n$, then
$F_n(s)F_n(1-s)=F_n(s)+F_n(1-s)$; uniqueness of the Laplace transform gives
the first identity in (H), and centering gives the second.  Formula (I)
follows from (H) and Proposition~\ref{prop:prime-kernel}, with its polar
counterterm retained before the Abel limit.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[fixed ray versus spectral gap]\label{rmk:fixed-ray-whitening}
Proposition~\ref{prop:hard-edge-fixed-vector} makes the family-specific
nature of the target exact: $g_n$ is the Dirichlet prefix, equivalently the
normalized Laguerre reproducing-kernel state at the hard edge.  It does not,
however, create a Rayleigh quotient for the split $A_n-\lambda_n$.  The
natural Toeplitz polarization of $A_n$ has diagonal
\[
 A_1=1-\tfrac12(\gamma+\log4\pi)<0,
\]
and hence is not a metric, even though its value on the prefix is positive
for $n\ge8$.  The positive reference Gram normalizes (G) by $n$, not by
$A_n\asymp(n/2)\log n$.  Finally, the archimedean-whitened operators used at
interior, large-height gauges do not transport level by level to the Li
gauge $s=1$.  Thus a numerical overlap with their top eigenspace is not an
overlap for (I).  A valid spectral successor would first need a positive,
closed prime--pole renormalized form with a uniform boundary-transfer
theorem; constructing that form is new content, not bookkeeping.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[compensated split at the first prime]\label{prop:log2-compensated}
For $r>1$ define $D_n^{[r]}=r\lambda_n-A_n$ and
\[
 \kappa_{r,\varepsilon}(dx)
 =r e^{-(1+\varepsilon)x}\,d\psi(e^x)
 -r e^{-\varepsilon x}\,dx
 +(r-1){e^{-(3+\varepsilon)x}\over1-e^{-2x}}\,dx.
\]
With $\varphi_n(x)=L_{n-1}^{(1)}(x)-n$ and $a=\log2$,
\[
 -D_n^{[r]}=-nD_1^{[r]}+
 \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}
 \left(J_n^<(\varepsilon)+J_n^\ge(\varepsilon)\right),         \tag{J}
\]
where
\[
 J_n^<(\varepsilon)=
 \int_0^a\varphi_n(x)e^{-\varepsilon x}
 \left[-r+(r-1){e^{-3x}\over1-e^{-2x}}\right]dx               \tag{K}
\]
and
\[
 \begin{split}
 J_n^\ge(\varepsilon)={}&
 r\sum_{m\ge2}{\Lambda(m)\over m^{1+\varepsilon}}
 \varphi_n(\log m)\\
 &+\int_a^\infty\varphi_n(x)e^{-\varepsilon x}
 \left[-r+(r-1){e^{-3x}\over1-e^{-2x}}\right]dx .
 \end{split}                                                   \tag{L}
\]
In particular, the inequality on $J_n^\ge$ obtained by moving (K) and the
base term in (J) to the other side is identically $D_n^{[r]}\ge0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{prop:prime-kernel} and $\lambda_1^{\rm prime}=\gamma$ (the limit of
$-\zeta'/\zeta(1+\varepsilon)-1/\varepsilon$), the prime--pole part of
$\int\varphi_n\,d\kappa_{r,\varepsilon}$ tends to $-r\lambda_n^{\rm prime}+nr\lambda_1^{\rm prime}$.
Expanding $e^{-3x}/(1-e^{-2x})=\sum_{j\ge0}e^{-(3+2j)x}$ and using
$\int_0^\infty e^{-\rho x}L_{n-1}^{(1)}(x)\,dx=1-(1-1/\rho)^n$ with Lemma~\ref{lem:li-arch-sign}
(which gives $A_n-nA_1=\sum_{r'\ge3\ \mathrm{odd}}[(1-1/r')^n-1+n/r']$) yields
$(r-1)\int_0^\infty\varphi_n e^{-3x}(1-e^{-2x})^{-1}dx=-(r-1)(A_n-nA_1)$; the integral converges
since $\varphi_n(0)=0$.  Adding, and using $\lambda_k=A_k+\lambda_k^{\rm prime}$, the total is
$-D_n^{[r]}+nD_1^{[r]}$, which is (J).  The first atom of $d\psi(e^x)$ is at $a=\log2$, so splitting
the integral at $a$ gives (K) and (L).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[the mass comparison is not coercivity]\label{rmk:log2-mass}
The continuous density in (K) is negative at
$x=\frac12\log2$, where it equals
$-r+(r-1)/\sqrt2<-1$.  This is the localized obstruction to a positive
completed measure.  It does not provide a mass budget for (J): the positive
prime mass is
$-r\zeta'/\zeta(1+\varepsilon)\sim r/\varepsilon$, whereas the negative
mass of a compact subinterval of $(0,\log2)$ is $O_r(1)$.  Thus unweighted
mass domination holds automatically without implying the Laguerre
coefficient sign.  Once $\varphi_n$ is restored, the separated pole and
prime moments are both of order $\varepsilon^{-n}$ and cancel only in the
completed expression.  The fixed cut is exact and localizes the
support, but the surviving weighted inequality (J) is the same
coercive margin in another coordinate.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[positive flag reference for the hard-edge rays]
\label{prop:a1-positive-flag}
Put $B_n=A_n-\lambda_n$, let $\phi_k$ and $g_n$ be as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:hard-edge-fixed-vector}, and set
\[
 \mathsf B_a(f,g)=\frac12\bigl(\langle S_af,g\rangle+
                         \langle f,S_ag\rangle\bigr).
\]
For $\varepsilon>0$ the jointly regulated prime--pole form
\[
 \mathsf Q_\varepsilon(f,g)
 =\sum_{m\ge2}\frac{\Lambda(m)}{m^{1/2+\varepsilon}}
       \mathsf B_{\log m}(f,g)
 -\int_0^\infty e^{(1/2-\varepsilon)a}\mathsf B_a(f,g)\,da
                                                               \tag{M}
\]
is a bounded symmetric form on
$\mathcal H_\eta=L^2((0,\infty),e^{2\eta x}dx)$ whenever
$\max\{0,1/2-\varepsilon\}<\eta<1/2$; every $g_n$ belongs to this space.  It satisfies
\[
 \mathsf Q_\varepsilon[g_n]
 =\sum_{m\ge2}\frac{\Lambda(m)}{m^{1+\varepsilon}}
       L_{n-1}^{(1)}(\log m)
  -\int_0^\infty e^{-\varepsilon a}L_{n-1}^{(1)}(a)\,da,
 \qquad
 \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\mathsf Q_\varepsilon[g_n]=B_n. \tag{N}
\]
Its algebraic boundary matrix in the orthonormal Laguerre basis is Toeplitz:
\[
 \mathsf Q_0(\phi_j,\phi_k)=q_{|j-k|},\qquad
 q_0=-\gamma,\qquad
 q_d=\frac12\bigl(B_{d+1}-2B_d+B_{d-1}\bigr)\quad(d\ge1),   \tag{O}
\]
where $B_0=0$.

Fix $M=150$ and use the orthogonal flag
\[
 \mathcal H_{\rm flag}=\operatorname{span}
       \{g_M,\phi_M,\phi_{M+1},\ldots\}.
\]
The diagonal form
\[
 \mathsf A_{\rm flag}\left[a_0g_M+\sum_{k\ge M}a_k\phi_k\right]
 =A_M|a_0|^2+\sum_{k\ge M}(A_{k+1}-A_k)|a_k|^2              \tag{P}
\]
is positive and satisfies, exactly,
\[
 \mathsf A_{\rm flag}[g_n]=A_n\qquad(n\ge M).                \tag{Q}
\]
Consequently every section
$\mathcal V_{M,N}=\operatorname{span}\{g_M,\phi_M,\ldots,\phi_{N-1}\}$
with $N\ge n$ has a legitimate normalized matrix
$\mathsf K=\mathsf G^{-1/2}\mathsf Q\mathsf G^{-1/2}$ (with $\mathsf G$ the diagonal Gram matrix of
$\mathsf A_{\rm flag}$ and $\mathsf Q$ that of $\mathsf Q_0$ on the section) and a unit vector
$u_n$ for which
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 \langle u_n,\mathsf Ku_n\rangle=\frac{B_n}{A_n}
 =1-\frac{\lambda_n}{A_n}.
 \quad}                                                       \tag{R}
\]
Thus the fixed-cutoff margin $r_*\lambda_n\ge A_n$ is the fixed-ray estimate
$\langle u_n,\mathsf Ku_n\rangle\le1501/2002$, not a spectral-gap statement for
all test functions.  It is not asserted to be necessary; the cofinal
refinement is Corollary~\ref{cor:a0-cofinal-quarter-margin}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Equation (H) inserted in (M) gives (N).  Polarizing the Laguerre translation
identity gives a Toeplitz matrix.  The prefix identity
\[
 B_n=nq_0+2\sum_{d=1}^{n-1}(n-d)q_d
\]
then yields (O) by two finite differences.  For the reference form, the
odd-harmonic formula gives
\[
 \Delta^2A_k=\sum_{\substack{r\ge1\\r\ \mathrm{odd}}}
             \frac{(1-1/r)^k}{r^2}>0.
\]
Lemma~\ref{lem:li-arch-sign} gives $\Delta A_M>0$; hence
$\Delta A_k>0$ for $k\ge M$, so (P) is
positive.  Finally
$g_n=g_M+\sum_{k=M}^{n-1}\phi_k$ and (Q) follows by telescoping.  The same
telescoping in the numerator proves (R).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[what the flag construction does and does not prove]
\label{rmk:a1-flag-gate}
The reference (P) repairs the type error in trying to whiten the indefinite
archimedean Toeplitz polarization.  It does not supply the estimate in (R).
The exact coefficient needed by Gershgorin or Schur is, with
$\nabla_c^2B_d=B_{d+1}-2B_d+B_{d-1}$,
\[
 q_d=\tfrac12\nabla_c^2(A-\lambda)_d;
\]
putting absolute values around its prime--pole representation removes the
required cancellation.  No finite-section eigenvalue bound is proved or used
here.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[adjacent flag--Schur criterion]
\label{prop:a1-adjacent-schur}
Put
\[
 c=\frac{501}{2002},\qquad \kappa=1-c=\frac{1501}{2002},
 \qquad H_n=\kappa A_n-B_n=\lambda_n-cA_n,
\]
and
\[
 d_n=\kappa(A_{n+1}-A_n)+\gamma>0.
\]
Use the one-step extension of (P),
\[
 \mathsf A_{\rm flag}^{(149)}
 \left[a_0g_{149}+\sum_{k\ge149}a_k\phi_k\right]
 =A_{149}|a_0|^2+\sum_{k\ge149}(A_{k+1}-A_k)|a_k|^2,
\]
which is positive by the same argument.  For the form
$\mathsf M=\kappa\mathsf A_{\rm flag}^{(149)}-\mathsf Q_0$, its compression to
$\operatorname{span}\{g_n,\phi_n\}$ is exactly
\[
 \mathsf M_n=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 H_n&-\frac12(H_n+d_n-H_{n+1})\\
 -\frac12(H_n+d_n-H_{n+1})&d_n
 \end{pmatrix}.                                              \tag{RS}
\]
Consequently the scalar inequalities
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 \mathcal T_n:=4H_nd_n-(H_n+d_n-H_{n+1})^2\ge0
 \qquad(n\ge149)
 \quad}                                                       \tag{RT}
\]
imply $H_n\ge0$ for every $n\ge149$, hence the fixed-cutoff margin
$r_*\lambda_n\ge A_n$ and A1.  Equivalently, (RT) asks for nonnegativity of the one-parameter
tapered family
\[
 \mathsf M[g_n+t\phi_n]
 =H_n-t(H_n+d_n-H_{n+1})+t^2d_n.                             \tag{RU}
\]
The coefficientwise convexity condition
$H_{n+1}-2H_n+H_{n-1}\ge0$ is not available: with the interval method of
Proposition~\ref{prop:a1-finite-conditional}(i) (script
\texttt{scripts/fixed\_margin\_eta\_21\_149.py --top 221 --first 219 --K 1100 --terms 1000}), and
subject to the tail bound assumed there, one finds
$-0.010580<H_{221}-2H_{220}+H_{219}<-0.010578$.  Thus (RT) is a sufficient condition that is
stronger than $H_n\ge0$, which is itself at least as strong as RH.  We have
not proved that RH implies (RT).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The Toeplitz identities give
\[
 \mathsf Q_0(g_n,\phi_n)
 =\sum_{d=1}^nq_d=\frac12(\Delta B_n+\gamma),
 \qquad \mathsf Q_0[\phi_n]=-\gamma,
\]
which proves (RS).  Since $d_n>0$, (RT) gives $4H_nd_n\ge0$, hence $H_n\ge0$, for each
$n\ge149$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[the adjacent criterion is sufficient, not necessary]
\label{rmk:a1-adjacent-triangle}
When $H_n,H_{n+1}\ge0$ and $d_n>0$, the determinant (RT) factors as
\[
 \mathcal T_n=
 \bigl((\sqrt{H_n}+\sqrt{d_n})^2-H_{n+1}\bigr)
 \bigl(H_{n+1}-(\sqrt{H_n}-\sqrt{d_n})^2\bigr).
\]
Thus (RT) imposes a two-sided triangle inequality in addition to the
one-sided margin $H_{n+1}\ge0$.  This extra regularity is genuine.  A
critical-line quartet contributes
$L_n^{\rm crit}=4-4\cos(n\pi/2)\ge0$ to every Li coefficient; the model
$\widehat H_n=1+L_n^{\rm crit}$, $\widehat d_n=1$ has all
$\widehat H_n>0$ but
\[
 4\widehat H_4\widehat d_4-
 (\widehat H_4+\widehat d_4-\widehat H_5)^2=-5.
\]
Consequently Li positivity, even with critical support, does not imply
(RT).  For the Riemann zeta function we have not proved that RH implies
(RT), even for large $n$; (RT) is a sufficient condition only, and the
minimal scalar obligation is still $H_n\ge0$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[sharp regulator-domain collision]
\label{prop:a1-epsilon-flow}
Define the ordinary-Laguerre prime--pole coefficients
\[
 c_k(\varepsilon)=
 \sum_{m\ge2}\frac{\Lambda(m)}{m^{1+\varepsilon}}L_k(\log m)
 -\frac{(\varepsilon-1)^k}{\varepsilon^{k+1}}.
\]
Before removal of the regulator they obey the exact flow
\[
 c_k'=(k+1)c_{k+1}-(2k+1)c_k+kc_{k-1},\qquad c_{-1}=0.       \tag{S}
\]
Write $\mathcal G$ for the tridiagonal generator on the right of (S).
Under the unitary Laguerre transform from $\ell^2(\mathbb N_0)$ to
$L^2((0,\infty),e^{-x}dx)$, $\mathcal G$ is multiplication by
$-x$.  For the prefix $P_n=L_{n-1}^{(1)}=\sum_{k<n}L_k$,
\[
 e^{\varepsilon x}P_n\in L^2(e^{-x}dx)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \varepsilon<\frac12.          \tag{T}
\]
In contrast, constructing the separated Euler shift operator in norm
requires
\[
 \sum_{m\ge2}\frac{\Lambda(m)}{m^{1/2+\varepsilon}}<\infty
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \varepsilon>\frac12.          \tag{U}
\]
Neither condition holds at the endpoint.  Moreover the unitarily equivalent
flag $\operatorname{span}\{P_M,L_M,L_{M+1},\ldots\}$ is not invariant under
this generator; at $M=150$ the squared norm of the escaping component of
$\mathcal G P_M$ is
$M(M-1)=22350$.  Hence the domains (T) and (U) are disjoint and the flag is not invariant under
the flow; we observe that this particular transport argument does not yield the estimate
$\langle u_n,\mathsf Ku_n\rangle\le1501/2002$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate term by term and use
\[
 -xL_k=(k+1)L_{k+1}-(2k+1)L_k+kL_{k-1};
\]
the polar Laplace coefficient satisfies the identical recurrence.  This
also proves the unitary realization.  Condition (T) follows because the
integrand is $|P_n(x)|^2e^{-(1-2\varepsilon)x}$, while (U) is the abscissa
of absolute convergence of $-\zeta'/\zeta$.  Necessity for norm convergence
in (U) follows by testing finite tails on functions nearly constant over an
interval much longer than every translation in that tail; the operator norm
is then arbitrarily close to twice the corresponding scalar sum.  Finally telescoping the
tridiagonal recurrence gives
$\mathcal G P_M=M(L_M-L_{M-1})$; projection onto the flag yields the stated
escape norm.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[higher prime powers are an explicit linear correction]
\label{prop:a1-higher-prime-powers}
For $N=n-1$ put
\[
 R_N^{\rm pp}:=\sum_p\sum_{k\ge2}\frac{\log p}{p^k}
                     L_N^{(1)}(k\log p).
\]
This series converges absolutely and, unconditionally for every $n\ge1$,
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 |R_{n-1}^{\rm pp}|
 \le1+(11+\log2)n+\sqrt{2\log2\,n(n+1)}<14n+1.
 \quad}                                                       \tag{V}
\]
If $\Pi_n$ denotes the joint Abel limit of the simple-prime
channel and the pole, then
\[
 \lambda_n^{\rm prime}=\Pi_n-R_{n-1}^{\rm pp}. \tag{W}
\]
In particular the nearly quartic sufficient margin is exactly
\[
 3A_n+4\Pi_n
 \ge \frac{A_n}{1001}+4R_{n-1}^{\rm pp}.                     \tag{X}
\]
Thus all prime powers $p^k$, $k\ge2$, have been removed from the singular
regularization; for the fixed-cutoff sufficient condition (X),
the remaining proportional problem is the joint simple-prime--pole channel.
Corollary~\ref{cor:a0-cofinal-quarter-margin} shows that neither the
coefficient $2002/501$ nor the margin $A_n/1001$ is intrinsic when the A0
cutoff is allowed to move cofinally.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $\theta(x)=\sum_{p\le x}\log p$.  For the square block set
\[
 S_{2,N}=\sum_p\frac{\log p}{p^2}L_N^{(1)}(2\log p),\qquad
 M_{2,N}=\int_1^\infty x^{-2}L_N^{(1)}(2\log x)\,dx.
\]
The Laguerre generating function gives $M_{2,N}={\bf1}_{2\mid N}$.
Put $f(x)=x^{-2}L_N^{(1)}(2\log x)$, so that
$S_{2,N}-M_{2,N}=\int_{[1,\infty)}f\,d(\theta(x)-x)$.  On $[1,2)$ one has
$d\theta=0$, and the classical bound $|L_N^{(1)}(u)|\le(N+1)e^{u/2}$ \cite[22.14.13]{AbramowitzStegun} gives
$|f(x)|\le(N+1)/x$, so
\[
 \Bigl|\int_1^2f(x)\,dx\Bigr|\le(\log2)(N+1).
\]
On $[2,\infty)$ put $H(x)=\theta(x)-(x-2)$, so that $H(2^-)=0$.  Chebyshev's
bound $\theta(x)\le2(\log2)x$ and $\theta(x)\le\log6$ for $x<5$ give
$|H(x)|\le x$ for $x\ge2$, while $f(x)H(x)\to0$ as $x\to\infty$.
Integration by parts, the substitution $u=2\log x$ (so
$f'(x)\,dx=-e^{-u}L_N^{(2)}(u)\,du$ and $x=e^{u/2}$), and Cauchy--Schwarz
with the Laguerre norm
$\int_0^\infty e^{-u}u^2[L_N^{(2)}(u)]^2du=(N+1)(N+2)$ give
\begin{align*}
 \Bigl|\int_{[2,\infty)}f\,dH\Bigr|
 &\le\int_{2\log2}^\infty e^{-u/2}|L_N^{(2)}(u)|\,du\\
 &\le\Bigl(\int_{2\log2}^\infty\frac{du}{u^2}\Bigr)^{1/2}
    \sqrt{(N+1)(N+2)}
 =\sqrt{\frac{(N+1)(N+2)}{2\log2}}.
\end{align*}
Since $1/(2\log2)<2\log2$, adding the two pieces gives
\[
 |S_{2,N}-{\mathbf 1}_{2\mid N}|
 \le(\log2)(N+1)+\sqrt{2\log2\,(N+1)(N+2)}.                 \tag{Y}
\]
For $k\ge3$, the same bound
$|L_N^{(1)}(u)|\le(N+1)e^{u/2}$ gives
\[
 \sum_p\sum_{k\ge3}\frac{\log p}{p^k}|L_N^{(1)}(k\log p)|
 \le (N+1)\sum_{m\ge2}
       \frac{\log m}{m^{3/2}(1-m^{-1/2})}<11(N+1).           \tag{Z}
\]
Equations (Y)--(Z) prove (V).  Finally apply the regularized Euler identity
to $\log((s-1)\zeta(s))$.  Dominated convergence is legitimate termwise in
the $k\ge2$ block and gives (W); substituting
$\lambda_n=A_n+\lambda_n^{\rm prime}$ into
$4\lambda_n-A_n\ge A_n/1001$ gives (X).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[simple-prime Selberg inversion is not coercive]
\label{prop:a1-simple-prime-selberg}
Let $E_\theta(u)=\theta(e^u)-e^u$.  The channel isolated in
Proposition~\ref{prop:a1-higher-prime-powers} has the exact, unregularized
representations
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 \Pi_n
 =-n-\int_0^\infty E_\theta(u)e^{-u}L_{n-1}^{(2)}(u)\,du
 =1-L_n^{(1)}(\log2)
  -\int_{\log2}^\infty E_\theta(u)e^{-u}L_{n-1}^{(2)}(u)\,du.
 \quad}                                                       \tag{SP1}
\]

Let $d\alpha=\sum_p\log p\,\delta_{\log p}$,
$d\beta=e^u du$, and $d\mu=d\alpha-d\beta$.  The Selberg measure built
only from primes and semiprimes is
\[
 d\mathcal A_p=u\,d\alpha+\alpha*\alpha\ge0,
\]
and its centered form satisfies the exact identity
\[
 d\mathcal B_p:=d\mathcal A_p-2u\,d\beta
 =u\,d\mu+2\beta*\mu+\mu*\mu.                               \tag{SP2}
\]
For the regulated test
$w_{n,a}(v)=ae^{-av}L_{n-1}^{(1)}(av)$, $a>1$, define
\[
 \Pi_{n,a}:=\langle w_{n,a},\beta-\alpha\rangle,
\]
and define
\[
 (\mathcal Sg)(v)=vg(v)+2e^{-v}\int_v^\infty e^tg(t)\,dt.
\]
The decaying solution of $\mathcal Sg_{n,a}=w_{n,a}$ is
\[
 g_{n,a}(v)=\frac1v\left[w_{n,a}(v)-2v^2e^{-v}
       \int_v^\infty\frac{e^tw_{n,a}(t)}{t^3}\,dt\right],    \tag{SP3}
\]
and the collective identity, with no absolute values, is
\[
 \Pi_{n,a}
 =-\langle g_{n,a},\mathcal B_p\rangle
  +\iint g_{n,a}(u+v)\,d\mu(u)d\mu(v).                       \tag{SP4}
\]
Nevertheless its Hankel kernel has both signs.  Explicitly,
\[
 g_{n,a}(0)=an\left(\frac{a(n+1)}2-1\right)>0,
\]
whereas, with $N=n-1$,
\[
 g_{n,a}(v)=\frac{(-1)^Na^{N+1}}{N!}e^{-av}v^{N-1}
             \{1+O_{n,a}(v^{-1})\}.
\]
Thus for every even $n$ it is negative in the tail, and the quadratic term
in (SP4) is not a positive energy.  Positivity of the prime/semiprime
Selberg measure therefore does not prove (X), even after all higher prime
powers have been removed.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $a>1$, the continuous Laguerre transform equals
\[
 a\int_0^\infty e^{-(a-1)u}L_{n-1}^{(1)}(au)\,du
 =n[z^n]\log\left(\frac a{1-z}-1\right).
\]
Subtract the prime transform and let $a\downarrow1$ jointly.  Stieltjes
integration by parts, using
$\mu([0,u])=E_\theta(u)+1$ and
$(e^{-u}L_{n-1}^{(1)})'=-e^{-u}L_{n-1}^{(2)}$, proves (SP1), including
the boundary $-n$.  Expanding $\alpha=\beta+\mu$ and using
$\beta*\beta=u\,d\beta$ proves (SP2).

For every $g$ for which the displayed integrals converge absolutely, Fubini gives
\[
 \langle g,\mathcal B_p\rangle
 =\langle\mathcal Sg,\mu\rangle
  +\iint g(u+v)\,d\mu(u)d\mu(v).
\]
Solving the first-order equation for $\mathcal S$ gives (SP3), and
$\Pi_{n,a}=-\langle w_{n,a},\mu\rangle$ gives (SP4).
Taylor expansion at zero and the leading Laguerre coefficient at infinity
give the two displayed signs.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[the optimal adjacent taper does not restore Hankel positivity]
\label{rmk:a1-tapered-selberg-gate}
For $h_{n,t}=g_n+t\phi_n$, the simple-prime test occurring in
(SP4), before removal of the regulator $a>1$, is exactly
\[
 w_{n,t,a}(u)=ae^{-au}
 \left\{L_{n-1}^{(1)}(au)+tL_n(au)+t(t-1)\right\}.
\]
Thus the value
$t=t_n^*=(H_n+d_n-H_{n+1})/(2d_n)$ evaluates the adjacent Schur
minimum exactly:
\[
 \mathsf M[h_{n,t_n^*}]=\frac{\mathcal T_n}{4d_n}.
\]
Let $G_{n,t,a}$ be the decaying solution of
$\mathcal SG_{n,t,a}=w_{n,t,a}$.  If $k_0$ is the degree of the polynomial
inside braces and $d=k_0-1$, then for $n\ge3$ (so that $d\ge1$)
\[
 G_{n,t,a}(v)=Ce^{-av}v^d\{1+O_{n,t,a}(v^{-1})\},\qquad C\ne0,
\]
and its two-point Hankel compression satisfies
\[
 \det\!\begin{pmatrix}
 G_{n,t,a}(2R)&G_{n,t,a}(3R)\\
 G_{n,t,a}(3R)&G_{n,t,a}(4R)
 \end{pmatrix}
 =C^2e^{-6aR}R^{2d}
   \{8^d-9^d+O_{n,t,a}(R^{-1})\}<0
\]
for all sufficiently large $R$.  This holds for every real $t$, including
$t_n^*$ and $t=0$, and the two-point witness can also be placed on
logarithms of actual primes by Bertrand's postulate.  Hence optimizing the
adjacent taper does not turn Selberg inversion into a positive Hankel
energy.  This rules out that proof mechanism, not (RT): the latter could
still hold only through a special signed cancellation of the canonical
$\log p$ weights between the linear and quadratic terms of (SP4), together
with the archimedean and higher-prime-power corrections.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Selberg's quadratic term cancels before it can pay Schur]
\label{rmk:a1-selberg-square-cancellation}
The obstruction is algebraic, not merely a bad Hankel estimate.  With
$d\mu=d\alpha-d\beta$ and
$d\mathcal B_p=u\,d\mu+2\beta*\mu+\mu*\mu$, Fubini gives, for every
admissible $G$,
\[
 -\langle G,\mathcal B_p\rangle+\mathcal Q_G(\mu)
 =-\langle\mathcal SG,\mu\rangle,
 \qquad
 \mathcal Q_G(\mu)=\langle G,\mu*\mu\rangle.
\]
Hence the only Selberg square cancels identically when the full centered
identity is retained.  If it is instead reserved to dominate the square
of the adjacent cross entry, the residual kernel is
\[
 \mathsf R_{n,a}(u,v)=g_{n,a}(u+v)
 -\frac{\ell_{n,a}(u)\ell_{n,a}(v)}{4d_n},
 \qquad
 \ell_{n,a}(u)=ae^{-au}\{L_n(au)-1\}.
\]
where $g_{n,a}$ is (SP3) and $d_n=\kappa(A_{n+1}-A_n)+\gamma>0$ for every $n\ge1$
($d_1=0.3369\ldots$ and $A_{n+1}-A_n$ is increasing).  For every $n\ge2$ and $a>1$,
\[
 \mathsf R_{n,a}(R,R)=
 -\frac{a^{2n+2}}{4d_n(n!)^2}
 e^{-2aR}R^{2n}\{1+O_{n,a}(R^{-1})\}<0
\]
for all sufficiently large $R$.  Taking $R=\log p$ places this one-point
witness on an actual prime with its canonical weight.  This refutes a PSD
completion of the Schur square; it does not determine the value of the
full signed arithmetic measure.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[the global prime--pole Cholesky norm has an RH-strength domain]
\label{prop:a1-prime-cholesky-domain}
Let $\mathsf T_t=e^{-t/2}S_t$ on $L^2(0,\infty)$ and, for $a>2$, define the
Bochner integral
\[
 V_{n,a}=a\int_1^\infty \mathsf T_{a\log y}g_n\{dy-d\theta(y)\}.
\]
(It converges because $\|\mathsf T_{a\log y}g_n\|_2=y^{-a/2}\|g_n\|_2$.)  For
$1<a\le2$ define $V_{n,a}$ instead through its Fourier transform,
$\widehat V_{n,a}(t):=a\widehat g_n(t)M(a(1/2+it))$, so that (CH2) below is
the definition of $\|V_{n,a}\|_2^2\in[0,\infty]$.  For $a>2$,
\[
 \Pi_{n,a}=\langle V_{n,a},g_n\rangle,         \tag{CH1}
\]
and, if
\[
 Q(s)=\sum_p\frac{\log p}{p^s},\qquad M(s)=\frac1{s-1}-Q(s),
\]
Plancherel gives the exact collective factorization
\[
 \|V_{n,a}\|_2^2
 =\frac{a^2}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}|\widehat g_n(t)|^2
       |M(a(1/2+it))|^2dt.                                  \tag{CH2}
\]
All prime--prime and prime--continuous cross terms are retained inside the
square in (CH2).

Nevertheless this norm cannot be continued to the Li point.  For $a>2$ the
Laplace transform of $V_{n,a}$ is
\[
 \mathcal LV_{n,a}(z)=aG_n(z)M(a(z+1/2)),\qquad
 G_n(z)=1-\left(\frac{z-1/2}{z+1/2}\right)^n.                \tag{CH3}
\]
For every $a>1$ the function $aG_n(z)M(a(z+1/2))$, meromorphic in $\Re z>-1/2$, has, at
each zeta zero $\rho$ of multiplicity $m_\rho$, a pole at
$z=\rho/a-1/2$ with residue
\[
 m_\rho\left[1-\left(1-\frac a\rho\right)^n\right]         \tag{CH4}
\]
whenever this quantity is nonzero.  For $a>2$ all these poles lie in
$\Re z<0$.  For $1<a\le2$: if $\Re\rho>a/2$ the pole lies in $\Re z>0$,
so (when the residue is nonzero) no $L^2(0,\infty)$ function has this
Laplace transform; for a critical-line zero the pole lies at distance
$(a-1)/(2a)$ from the imaginary axis, and if $(1-1/\rho)^n\ne1$ the right side of (CH2) satisfies
\[
 \|V_{n,a}\|_2^2\gg_{n,\rho}(a-1)^{-1}\qquad(a\downarrow1). \tag{CH5}
\]
Thus, in any extension of (CH1) to $1<a\le2$, Cauchy--Schwarz would lose the factor
$\|V_{n,a}\|_2\gg(a-1)^{-1/2}$, even under RH;
the finite signed projection, not the full Cholesky norm, is essential.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The hard-edge identity gives
$\langle \mathsf T_tg_n,g_n\rangle=e^{-t}L_{n-1}^{(1)}(t)$, proving
(CH1).  Fourier transformation of the Bochner integral gives
\[
 \widehat V_{n,a}(t)=a\widehat g_n(t)M(a(1/2+it)),
\]
and hence (CH2).  Formula (CH3) follows in the same way from the Laplace
semigroup.  Möbius inversion
$Q(s)=\sum_{k\ge1}\mu(k)(-\zeta'/\zeta)(ks)$ shows that $M$ has residue
$m_\rho$ at $s=\rho$, giving (CH4) after the substitution $s=a(z+1/2)$.
Since $0<\Re\rho<1$, the pole $z=\rho/a-1/2$ has real part
$(\Re\rho-a/2)/a$, which is negative for $a>2$ and positive when
$\Re\rho>a/2$.  An $L^2(0,\infty)$ Laplace transform is holomorphic in
$\Re z>0$.  For $\rho=1/2+i\gamma$ the real part is $-(a-1)/(2a)$; on the
line $\Re z=0$ the function $a\widehat g_n(t)M(a(1/2+it))$ behaves near
$t=\gamma/a$ like a nonzero multiple of $(it-\rho/a+1/2)^{-1}$, and
integrating the square of this Cauchy kernel over a fixed neighborhood of
$\gamma/a$ gives $\gg(a-1)^{-1}$, hence (CH5).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[positive Fourier preconditioning cannot repair the Cholesky gate]
\label{rmk:a1-euler-resolvent-gate}
The failure of (CH2) persists for the pointwise bound obtained from any
positive translation-invariant preconditioner.  For $a>2$ (and for
$1<a\le2$ as long as no zero has $\Re\rho\ge a/2$) (CH1) has the scalar form
\[
 B_{n,a}^{(p+{\rm pole})}
 =\frac a{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}|\widehat g_n(t)|^2
   \Re M(a(1/2+it))\,dt.
\]
Bounding $|\Re M|\le|M|$, which is the best that any positive multiplier
combined with Young's inequality can give pointwise, yields
\[
 B_{n,a}^{(p+{\rm pole})}\ge-\mathcal J_{n,a},
 \qquad
 \mathcal J_{n,a}=\frac a{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}
 |\widehat g_n(t)|^2|M(a(1/2+it))|\,dt.
\]
The integral $\mathcal J_{n,a}$ is defined for every $a>1$, and the
divergence below is unconditional.
For each fixed $n$ there is a critical-line zero
$\rho=1/2+i\gamma$ for which $\widehat g_n(\gamma)\ne0$: Hardy supplies
infinitely many distinct critical ordinates, whereas the resonance equation
for $\widehat g_n$ has only finitely many solutions.  Near such a zero,
$M(s)=m_\rho/(s-\rho)+O(1)$, whence
\[
 \mathcal J_{n,a}\gg_{n,\rho}\log\frac1{a-1}\longrightarrow\infty
 \qquad(a\downarrow1).
\]
Thus this pointwise modulus bound destroys the
signed phase responsible for the finite Abel limit.  A surviving argument
must be noncommutative/degree-adapted or must retain that phase to the end.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[the complete Mangoldt phase is small on the Euler side]
\label{prop:a1-full-mangoldt-phase}
Put
\[
 F(s)=(s-1)\zeta(s),\qquad
 M_\Lambda(s)=\frac1{s-1}+\frac{\zeta'}\zeta(s),
\]
and, for $a>1$,
\[
 \mathcal B_{n,a}
 =a\sum_{m\ge2}\frac{\Lambda(m)}{m^a}
       L_{n-1}^{(1)}(a\log m)
 -a\int_1^\infty y^{-a}L_{n-1}^{(1)}(a\log y)\,dy .       \tag{AD}
\]
Then the coefficient formula
\[
 \mathcal B_{n,a}=-n[z^n]\log F\!\left(\frac a{1-z}\right) \tag{AE}
\]
continues the joint expression to a neighborhood of $a=1$, where
$\lim_{a\downarrow1}\mathcal B_{n,a}=B_n=A_n-\lambda_n$.
For the direct phase representation assume $a>2$.  If
\[
 \widehat g_n(t)=1-\left(\frac{-1/2+it}{1/2+it}\right)^n,
 \qquad W_n(t)=|\widehat g_n(t)|^2,
\]
and $\vartheta_a(t)$ is the continuous phase of
$F(a(1/2+it))$, then
\[
 \mathcal B_{n,a}
 =-\frac1{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}W_n(t)\,d\vartheta_a(t),
 \qquad \operatorname {TV}_{\mathbb R}(W_n)=8n.             \tag{AF}
\]
Consequently
\[
 \boxed{|\mathcal B_{n,a}|<3n\qquad(a\ge4).}               \tag{AG}
\]
Nevertheless, lowering $a$ to one crosses a zero $\rho$ with
$\Re\rho>1/2$ exactly at $a=2\Re\rho$, since
\[
 \left|1-\frac a\rho\right|^2-1
 =\frac{a(a-2\Re\rho)}{|\rho|^2}.                          \tag{AH}
\]
The resulting defect is the off-line residue sum in
Proposition~\ref{prop:a1-flag-residues}; it is not controlled by (AG).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The Euler product and the Laguerre generating function give (AE), with
all prime powers and the pole combined before the limit.  The hard-edge
translation formula and Plancherel give
\[
 \mathcal B_{n,a}=-\frac a{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}W_n(t)
      \Re M_\Lambda(a(1/2+it))\,dt.
\]
Since $\vartheta_a'=a\Re M_\Lambda$, this is (AF).  On each half-line the
Cayley angle traverses $[0,\pi]$ and
$W_n=2-2\cos(n\theta)$, so its total variation is $8n$.
For $a\ge4$, $|\arg(s-1)|<\pi/2$ and
$|\zeta(s)-1|\le\zeta(2)-1<2/3<\sin(\pi/4)$; hence
$|\vartheta_a|<3\pi/4$.  Integration by parts in (AF) proves (AG).
Finally, (AH) is direct expansion.  The limit at $a=1$ is the continuation
defined by (AE); the prime series in (AD) is direct for $a>1$, whereas the
phase representation (AF) is used only in its direct domain $a>2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[noncommutative positive preconditioning has no algebraic gain]
\label{rmk:a1-noncommutative-preconditioner}
Let $v_{n,a}$ be the complete Mangoldt--pole vector whose pairing with
$g_n$ is $\mathcal B_{n,a}$.  For every bounded strictly positive
invertible operator $R$,
\[
 \mathcal C_R(v,g)=\frac12\{\langle Rv,v\rangle+
                         \langle R^{-1}g,g\rangle\}
 \ge \langle v,g\rangle,
\]
but in fact
\[
 \inf_{R>0}\mathcal C_R(v,g)=|\langle v,g\rangle|.         \tag{AI}
\]
Indeed, the lower bound follows from Cauchy--Schwarz in the $R$ metric;
on $\operatorname{span}\{v,g\}$ one may choose $Rv=\operatorname{sgn}
\langle v,g\rangle\,g$ and extend $R$ positively.  Thus, in the
case $B_n>0$, the algebraic optimum is $B_n$ itself.  This does not forbid
a new arithmetic estimate for one explicit $R$; it proves that choosing or
optimizing a positive preconditioner supplies no margin by itself.

One can also try to bound $B_n$ by projecting to the first $n$ Laguerre
states.  With $B_0=0$ and $\Delta B_j=B_{j+1}-B_j$, consider a bound of the
form
\[
 B_n\le\frac{\sqrt n}{2}
 \left\{\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}
 (\Delta B_j+\Delta B_{n-1-j})^2\right\}^{1/2}             \tag{AJ}
\]
(not proved here).  Since $B_n$ enters only through the terms $j=0$ and
$j=n-1$, holding $B_0,\ldots,B_{n-1}$ fixed and sending $B_n=x\to+\infty$
makes the right side $\sqrt{n/2}\,x+O_n(1)$.  Hence a bound of the form
(AJ) cannot propagate a bound on $B_n$ for $n\ge3$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[exact zeta size-bias and its zero-gap divisor transport]
\label{prop:a1-zeta-size-bias}
Fix $s=1+\varepsilon>1$ and set
\[
 \mathfrak m(s)=-\frac{\zeta'}\zeta(s).
\]
Let $N$ have zeta law $\Pr(N=k)=k^{-s}/\zeta(s)$, and let $N^\star$ be
its logarithmic size-bias.  There are independent variables $D,N'$ with
\[
 \Pr(D=d)=\frac{\Lambda(d)d^{-s}}{\mathfrak m(s)},\qquad
 \Pr(N'=k)=\frac{k^{-s}}{\zeta(s)},                         \tag{AK}
\]
such that
\[
 N^\star\buildrel d\over=DN',\qquad
 \Pr(D=d\mid DN'=n)=\frac{\Lambda(d)}{\log n}{\bf1}_{d\mid n}. \tag{AL}
\]
The continuous polar analogue is
$\Gamma(2,\varepsilon)=Y_0+Y_0'$ with independent exponential summands;
conditioned on their sum, $Y_0$ is uniform on the interval.

For every polynomial $f$ the exact signed marginal difference is
\[
 \mathfrak M_\varepsilon(f)
 =\sum_{d\ge2}\frac{\Lambda(d)}{d^{1+\varepsilon}}f(\log d)
  -\int_0^\infty e^{-\varepsilon x}f(x)\,dx.                \tag{AM}
\]
For $f_t(x)=e^{-tx}$,
\[
 \mathfrak M_\varepsilon(f_t)
 =-\frac{\zeta'}\zeta(1+\varepsilon+t)-\frac1{\varepsilon+t}. \tag{AN}
\]
Thus the probabilistic comparison is exactly the scalar Mangoldt--pole
gate, rather than a new estimate for it.

Moreover, the conditional divisor operator
\[
 (\mathsf T_sf)(n)=\frac1{\log n}\sum_{d\mid n}\Lambda(d)f(d)
\]
is a Markov contraction whose norm on the mean-zero subspace is exactly
one.  The continuous conditional operator contracts the scaled Laguerre
mode $L_r(\varepsilon x)$ by $1/\sqrt{r+1}$, but
\[
 L_r(u)=\sum_{j=0}^r\binom rj\varepsilon^{-j}
 (1-\varepsilon^{-1})^{r-j}L_j(\varepsilon u),              \tag{AO}
\]
whose coefficient load for $0<\varepsilon<1$ is
$(2/\varepsilon-1)^r$.  At $\varepsilon=1/n$, $r=n-1$, this is
$\exp((1+o(1))n\log n)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The joint mass in $(d,k)$ from (AK) is
$\Lambda(d)(dk)^{-s}/(-\zeta'(s))$.  Summing over $dk=n$ and using
$\sum_{d\mid n}\Lambda(d)=\log n$ proves (AL).  The Gamma assertion is
the elementary change of variables from two independent exponentials.
Equations (AM)--(AN) follow by taking Laplace transforms.

For the norm assertion, let $e_p={\bf1}_{\{p\}}$ and
$q_p=\Pr(D=p)$.  A direct conditional calculation gives
\[
 \frac{\|\mathsf T_s(e_p-q_p)\|_2^2}{\|e_p-q_p\|_2^2}
 =\frac{R_p(s)-q_p}{1-q_p},\qquad
 R_p(s)=\frac1{\zeta(s)}\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{k^{-s}\log p}
                                      {\log p+\log k}.
\]
Dominated convergence gives $R_p(s)\to1$ as $p\to\infty$, proving zero
gap.  The Laguerre integral identity gives the factor $1/\sqrt{r+1}$, and
the dilation formula gives (AO); the binomial theorem gives its stated
absolute load.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[canonical crossed Stein kernel and exact homotopy flux]
\label{prop:a1-crossed-stein-flow}
Let $s=1+\varepsilon>1$,

\[
 \mathcal R(q)=-\frac{\zeta'}\zeta(1+q)-\frac1q,
 \qquad
 \mathfrak M_\varepsilon(f)
 =\sum_{m\ge2}\frac{\Lambda(m)}{m^s}f(\log m)
  -\int_0^\infty e^{-\varepsilon x}f(x)\,dx .              \tag{AP}
\]

After adding a cemetery atom at zero to the arithmetic law in (AK), its
mass can be coupled to the exponential polar law.  For every such coupling
of coordinates $Y,Y_0$, and every $C^1$ polynomially growing test,

\[
 \mathfrak M_\varepsilon(f)
 =\mathcal R(\varepsilon)f(0)
   +\frac1\varepsilon\mathbb E\{f(Y)-f(Y_0)\}
 =\mathcal R(\varepsilon)f(0)+\int_0^\infty f'(x)K_s(x)\,dx, \tag{AQ}
\]

where the first-order kernel is independent of the coupling and equals

\[
 K_s(x)=\sum_{\log m>x}\frac{\Lambda(m)}{m^s}
       -\frac{e^{-\varepsilon x}}\varepsilon .             \tag{AR}
\]

It is genuinely signed.  With $b=\log2$,

\[
 \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}
 (K_s(0+),K_s(b-),K_s(b+))
 =(-\gamma,\log2-\gamma,\tfrac12\log2-\gamma),            \tag{AS}
\]

whose signs are respectively negative, positive and negative.  On the
other hand, the exponential subcone has the true one-sided estimate

\[
 \mathfrak M_\varepsilon(e^{-t\,\cdot})
 =\mathcal R(\varepsilon+t)<0\qquad(t\ge0).                \tag{AT}
\]

This estimate does not extend by complete monotonicity: if
$\mathcal M=-\mathcal R$, then

\[
 \mathcal M^{(32)}(32/\log2)<0.                             \tag{AU}
\]

For the actual Laguerre test, $n\ge2$, (AQ) is the exact identity

\[
 \mathcal B_{n,s}
 =sn\mathcal R(\varepsilon)
  -s^2\int_0^\infty L_{n-2}^{(2)}(sx)K_s(x)\,dx .          \tag{AV}
\]

Moreover the complete Mangoldt--pole homotopy satisfies

\[
 a\,\partial_a\mathcal B_{n,a}
 =n\{\mathcal B_{n+1,a}-\mathcal B_{n,a}\}.               \tag{AW}
\]

Thus neither choosing the coupling nor integrating the homotopy supplies
a margin: the left side of (AV) tends to $B_n$, and integrating (AW)
from $a=1$ to an Euler point $a_0$ gives exactly
$\mathcal B_{n,a_0}-B_n$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We use the inequality
$-\zeta'/\zeta(\sigma)<1/(\sigma-1)$ for $\sigma>1$, equivalently the
statement that $F(s)=(s-1)\zeta(s)$ is increasing on $(1,\infty)$
\cite{AlzerKwong} (the monotonicity theorem for $(s-1)\zeta(s)/s^\alpha$,
case $\alpha=0$).  It gives
$F'(1+q)/F(1+q)>0$, hence
$\mathcal R(q)=-F'(1+q)/F(1+q)<0$.  Therefore
$0<\varepsilon(-\zeta'/\zeta)(1+\varepsilon)<1$, which is
exactly the mass needed for the cemetery completion.  Taking the two
marginals proves the first equality in (AQ).  The oriented-segment identity

\[
 f(Y)-f(Y_0)=(Y-Y_0)\int_0^1
 f'(Y_0+u(Y-Y_0))\,du
\]

shows that its occupation density is the difference of the two tail
functions.  Those tails give (AR), independently of the coupling.  Below
the first prime,
$K_s(x)=\mathcal R(\varepsilon)+(1-e^{-\varepsilon x})/\varepsilon$;
crossing $b$ subtracts $(\log2)2^{-s}$.  This proves (AS), using
$1/2<\gamma<3/5$ and $2/3<\log2<1$.  Equation (AT) follows by Laplace
transform.

For the obstruction in (AU), differentiation gives

\[
 (-1)^j\mathcal M^{(j)}(q)
 =\frac{j!}{q^{j+1}}
  -\sum_{m\ge2}\frac{\Lambda(m)(\log m)^j}{m^{1+q}}.
\]

At $j=32$, $q=32/\log2$, the $m=2$ atom alone exceeds the first
term because $32^{33}>2\cdot32!e^{32}$; the latter follows, for example,
from $e<68/25$ and the corresponding integer comparison.  This proves
(AU).  Finally $f(x)=L_{n-1}^{(1)}(sx)$ in (AQ) gives (AV).  The
polynomial identity

\[
 P_n(x)+xP_n'(x)-xP_n(x)
 =nL_n(x)=n\{P_{n+1}(x)-P_n(x)\},
 \qquad P_n=L_{n-1}^{(1)},
\]

applied termwise in the absolutely convergent domain gives (AW), and
coefficient continuation gives the stated endpoint identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[the flag moment is boundary plus off-line residues]
\label{prop:a1-flag-residues}
The analytic germ associated with (O) has the exact meromorphic continuation
\[
 h(z):=q_0+2\sum_{d\ge1}q_dz^d
 =-\frac{\zeta'}{\zeta}\!\left(\frac1{1-z}\right)
  -\frac1{(1-z)^{-1}-1}.                                    \tag{AA}
\]
Let
\[
 \mathcal F_n(z)=n+\sum_{d=1}^{n-1}(n-d)(z^d+z^{-d}),
 \qquad w_\rho=1-\rho^{-1}.
\]
Let $\mathcal I_n^\partial(h)$ denote the limit obtained by truncating the
Hadamard divisor in functional-equation-symmetric orbits, applying the
radial boundary integral, and then taking its symmetric finite part as the
radius tends to one; we assume that this limit exists and is independent
of the symmetric truncation (this is not proved here).  With these symmetric radial boundary values, its
prefix moment decomposes as
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 B_n=\mathcal I_n^\partial(h)
 +\sum_{\Re\rho>1/2}
   \frac{m_\rho\,\mathcal F_n(w_\rho)}{\rho(\rho-1)}.
 \quad}                                                       \tag{AB}
\]
For fixed $n$ the residue sum is absolutely convergent.  The first term
includes the boundary masses of critical-line zeros.

For a model function obtained by adjoining an off-line reciprocal quartet
to the divisor, $\Re h$ on $|z|=1$ is unchanged while the second term of
(AB) changes by the full quartet Li contribution.  This indicates (but does
not prove) that bounds depending only on the real boundary symbol cannot
yield (R).  Holomorphy of (AA) in the disk is equivalent to RH.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Summing (O) with the regularized Euler series gives (AA).  For a
real-meromorphic function $H(z)=a_0+\sum_{d\ge1}a_dz^d$ with simple poles
$p$ of residues $r_p$ inside the disk, subtracting the principal parts and
applying Fourier orthogonality gives
\[
 na_0+\sum_{d=1}^{n-1}(n-d)a_d
 =\frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
       \Re H(e^{i\vartheta})\mathcal F_n(e^{i\vartheta})\,d\vartheta
  -\sum_{|p|<1}r_p\frac{\mathcal F_n(p)}p.                    \tag{AC}
\]
A zero $\rho$ with $\Re\rho>1/2$ gives an interior pole at $w_\rho$ of
residue $-m_\rho/\rho^2$.  Equation (AB) follows from (AC); absolute
convergence follows from $\mathcal F_n(w_\rho)=n^2+O_n(|\rho|^{-1})$ and
$\sum_\rho(1+|\Im\rho|^2)^{-1}<\infty$.  Truncating the Hadamard product
symmetrically and then taking radial finite parts incorporates the poles on
the circle into $\mathcal I_n^\partial$.

For a reciprocal quartet with $w=e^{-\alpha+i\vartheta}$,
\[
 \frac{\mathcal F_n(w)}{\rho(\rho-1)}=w^n+w^{-n}-2.
\]
Its logarithmic derivative is purely imaginary on $\Re s=1/2$, so the real
boundary symbol is unchanged, while the right-half-plane residue pair equals
the negative of the quartet Li term.  This is the model computation behind
the final paragraph; holomorphy of (AA) in the disk means that no zero has
$\Re\rho>1/2$, which by the functional equation is RH.
\end{proof}

The criteria in Propositions~\ref{prop:li-syndetic-density-criteria}--\ref{prop:li-finite-diagonal-fermi}
are reformulations of Li's criterion.  Each follows from the fact that, if
RH fails, $\lambda_n\le-cR^n$ on a syndetic set (a consequence of the
dominant expansion of Bombieri--Lagarias \cite{BombieriLagarias1999},
proved below).  The equivalences with RH are proved below; none of the
equivalent conditions is verified here, and none of them is easier to
verify than $\lambda_n\ge0$ for all $n$.

\begin{proposition}[syndetic negative excursions and relaxed Li quantifiers]
\label{prop:li-syndetic-density-criteria}
If RH is false, there exist $R>1$, $c>0$, and a set
$\mathcal S\subset\mathbb N$ of positive natural density which is syndetic
such that, for all sufficiently large $n\in\mathcal S$,
\[
 \lambda_n\le-cR^n,
 \qquad D_n^{[4]}:=4\lambda_n-A_n<0.                       \tag{LS1}
\]
Here the upper logarithmic density of $E\subset\mathbb N$ is
\[
 \overline\delta_{\log}(E):=\limsup_{X\to\infty}{1\over\log X}
      \sum_{\substack{n\le X\\n\in E}}{1\over n}.
\]
Thus RH is equivalent to each of the following conditions:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $D_n^{[4]}>0$ outside a set of upper logarithmic density zero;
\item there are consecutive blocks of unbounded length on every index of
      which $D_n^{[4]}>0$;
\item for any fixed sequence $b_n\ge0$ with
      $\log(1+b_n)=o(n)$, the inequality $\lambda_n\ge-b_n$ holds outside
      a set of upper logarithmic density zero;
\item for such a sequence $b_n$, there are consecutive blocks of unbounded
      length on every index of which $\lambda_n\ge-b_n$.
\end{enumerate}
In particular,
\[
 \RH\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \lambda_n\ge-1\ \hbox{on consecutive blocks of unbounded length}.       \tag{LS2}
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Dominant expansion.}  Assume $\neg\RH$ and put
$w_\rho=1-1/\rho$, so that $\lambda_n=\sum_\rho(1-w_\rho^n)$ (sum over
zeros with multiplicity, in symmetric order).  Since
$|w_\rho|^2=1+(1-2\beta)/|\rho|^2$ for $\rho=\beta+i\gamma$, some zero has
$|w_\rho|>1$, and for every $r>1$ only finitely many zeros have
$|w_\rho|\ge r$.  Hence $R=\max_\rho|w_\rho|>1$ exists, the set
$\{\rho:|w_\rho|=R\}$ is finite, and we fix $R_1$ strictly between the next
modulus and $R$.  We claim
\[
 \lambda_n=-R^nP(n)+O(R_1^n),\qquad
 P(n)=\sum_{|w_\rho|=R}m_\rho\cos(n\arg w_\rho),
\]
where the sum runs over distinct zeros with multiplicity $m_\rho$.  Indeed,
group the zeros into the orbits $\{\rho,\bar\rho,1-\rho,1-\bar\rho\}$;
since $w_{1-\rho}=1/w_\rho$, the zeros with $|w_\rho|=R$ contribute
$-R^nP(n)+O(1)$.  Each of the finitely many remaining zeros with
$|\gamma|\le\sqrt n$ (there are $O(\sqrt n\log n)$ of them) contributes at
most $1+R_1'^{\,n}$, where $R_1'<R_1$ is the next modulus.  For an orbit
with $|\gamma|>\sqrt n$ write $w_\rho=re^{i\theta}$; then
$0\le\log r\le 1/(2\gamma^2)$, so $n\log r\le1/2$, and the orbit
contributes $2(2-w_\rho^n-w_\rho^{-n})$ up to conjugation, which differs
from $4(1-\cos n\theta)$ by at most $4(\cosh(n\log r)-1)\ll n^2\gamma^{-4}$.
Since $|\theta|\ll1/|\gamma|$, the sum over these orbits is
$\ll\sum_{|\gamma|>\sqrt n}(n^2\gamma^{-2}+n^2\gamma^{-4})\ll n^{3/2}\log n$.
All error terms are $O(R_1^n)$, proving the claim.

On the compact monothetic group generated by the finitely many phases,
$P$ is positive in a neighborhood of the identity.  Choose a regular
level $\eta>0$ with Haar-null boundary for $\{P>\eta\}$; such levels exist
because a probability distribution has at most countably many atoms.
Unique ergodicity then gives positive natural density for its return set,
while minimality gives an open cover by translates and compactness a finite
subcover, hence syndeticity.  On this return set the main term dominates and proves (LS1),
since $A_n=O(n\log n)$.  A syndetic set has positive logarithmic density
and meets every sufficiently long interval.  Finally
$b_n=e^{o(n)}=o(R^n)$, so the same set violates
$\lambda_n\ge-b_n$.  Under RH, Li's criterion gives $\lambda_n\ge0$ for
all $n$, while the Lagarias asymptotic
$\lambda_n=A_n+O(\sqrt n\log n)$ gives $D_n^{[4]}>0$ eventually.  The
asserted converse implications follow.
\end{proof}

We note without proof that the density of $\mathcal S$ obtained in this
way depends on the multiset of maximal-modulus zeros and has no positive
lower bound independent of that multiset; this is why conditions (1) and
(3) are stated with exceptional sets of density zero.

\begin{proposition}[bounded Fermi partition and exact arithmetic lift]
\label{prop:li-nonlinear-degree-partition}
Let $H_X=\sum_{n\le X}n^{-1}$, let $b_n\ge0$ satisfy
\[
 b_n\longrightarrow\infty,
 \qquad \log(1+b_n)=o(n),
\]
and fix $t>0$.  Define
\[
 \mathfrak F_{t,b}(X):={1\over H_X}\sum_{n\le X}{1\over n}
 {1\over1+\exp(t(\lambda_n+b_n))}.                         \tag{FD1}
\]
Then
\[
 \boxed{\quad\RH\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathfrak F_{t,b}(X)\longrightarrow0.\quad}               \tag{FD2}
\]
This bounded partition detects a positive logarithmic density of deep
negative excursions and does not impose a bound at every degree.

For comparison, for $X\ge2$ define the unbounded auxiliary partition
\[
 \mathcal Z_t(X):=\sum_{n\le X}{e^{-t\lambda_n}\over n}.                  \tag{LS3}
\]
Then
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 \RH\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal Z_t(X)=O_t(\log X)\ (X\ge2)\ \hbox{for some }t>0.
 \quad}                                                                  \tag{LS4}
\]
This is stronger in its quantifiers: it implies coefficient by coefficient
\[
 \lambda_n\ge-{1\over t}\{\log n+\log\log n+O_t(1)\}.                  \tag{FD3}
\]
For $\varepsilon>0$, put
\[
 p_n(\varepsilon)
 :=n\sum_{k=1}^n{n-1\choose k-1}{(-1)^{k-1}\over k\varepsilon^k},
\]
\[
 \mathcal E_{n,\varepsilon}:=p_n(\varepsilon)
 -\sum_{m\ge2}{\Lambda(m)\over m^{1+\varepsilon}}
       L_{n-1}^{(1)}(\log m).
\]
The prime--Laguerre formula gives
$\lambda_n=A_n+\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\mathcal E_{n,\varepsilon}$.
Indeed, $\sum_{n\ge1}L^{(1)}_{n-1}(x)z^n/n=(1-e^{-xz/(1-z)})/x$, whence
$\sum_n(p_n(\varepsilon)-Q_{n,\varepsilon})z^n/n=\log F(s(z)+\varepsilon)-\log F(1+\varepsilon)$
with $F(s)=(s-1)\zeta(s)$, $s(z)=(1-z)^{-1}$ and $Q_{n,\varepsilon}$ the
prime sum in $\mathcal E_{n,\varepsilon}$; letting $\varepsilon\to0$ and
comparing with Li's generating function gives the formula.  Moreover,
for every finite $X$, the bounded partition has the exact pre-limit form
\[
\boxed{
 \mathfrak F_{t,b}(X)
 =\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}{1\over H_X}
