so $D\mapsto e^D$ is not multiplicative on classes.  Whether the effective cone is closed under
products is an open question: $F_\beta(D_1D_2)$ is not determined by $F_\beta(D_1)$ and $F_\beta(D_2)$, and
$e^{D_1}e^{D_2}\neq e^{D_1+D_2}$ for non-commuting $D_1,D_2$.

\textnormal{(D3)} Riemann--Roch as a theta flip: the duality $\beta\mapsto1/\beta$ is implemented not as a
symmetry of Bost--Connes (it is not one) but by the adelic Fourier transform inside the GNS space ---
Tate's local functional equations as local RR, assembled into
\[
   F_\beta(D)-F_{1/\beta}(\kappa-D)=\deg D,\qquad \kappa=\mathcal A\ \text{(the ample archimedean class)},
\]
with $\deg$ read off the residue of the Bost--Connes transition.

\begin{conjecture}[Gibbs realization of the Weil pairing]\label{conj:X3G}
There is a normalization $c(\beta)>0$ and a heat-regularization $D\mapsto\widehat D$ such that, for every
real class $D$ orthogonal to the trivial plane,
\[
   \langle D,D\rangle_W \;=\; -\,\lim_{\beta\to1^+} c(\beta)\,\mathrm{Var}_{\omega_\beta}\!\big(\widehat
   D\big)\;\le\;0 .
\]
That is: on primitive classes, the negative of the Weil pairing \emph{is a variance} of the twisted KMS
state.  This conjecture is itself an RH-strength sign identification.  A derivation through
Remark~\ref{thm:conditional-closure} is unavailable because of the sign mismatch recorded there; a
correct implication would require a separate, convention-consistent identification of this pairing
with the Weil quadratic form.
\end{conjecture}

The structural non-circularity, which distinguishes this from the mechanisms discussed above, is that a variance
is $\ge0$ by Cauchy--Schwarz, not by spectral information, and $\omega_\beta$ is defined from purely
multiplicative and thermal data (the Bost--Connes algebra plus the KMS condition) with no zeros in any
input: it uses no zero-location input (cf.\ Proposition~\ref{prop:unboundable}).

\emph{Three structural coincidences (heuristic).}
\textnormal{(i)} \emph{Temperature dictionary.}  Bost--Connes KMS weights scale as $n^{-\beta}$, our
effective weights as $n^{-1/2-y}$, giving $\beta=\tfrac12+y$; the boundary limit $y\to\tfrac12^+$ where
the whole difficulty of the present approach lives is \emph{exactly} the Bost--Connes phase transition
$\beta\to1^+$, and the pole of $\zeta$ as a divergent partition function coincides with the pole pair
$\pm i/2=H^0\oplus H^2$ appearing at symmetry breaking.
\textnormal{(ii)} \emph{Convexity.}  The numerically observed $\sim1/\delta_N$ cancellation in the
\emph{linear} cone (sums do not produce effectivity) suggests a nonlinear notion; $F_\beta=\log\omega(e^D)$
is a convex function of $D$ along commuting directions, although, as noted after
Definition~\ref{def:thermal-effectivity}, closure of the effective cone under products is not known.
\textnormal{(iii)} \emph{Torus lift.}  The characters of $\Q_{>0}^*$ form the infinite torus
$\mathbb T^\infty$ (one angle per prime), and the explicit-formula line is the Kronecker flow inside it;
the conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the Weil distribution is the restriction to the
Kronecker line of a positive kernel on $\mathbb T^\infty$.  Since the vanishing of $\Lambda$ at mixed
integers $p^aq^b$ forces the mixed cumulants to vanish, the kernel is quasi-product (a Gaussian field
over independent coordinates), and the whole content compresses into a single lemma: the archimedean
term (the only non-product coupling) lifts to the torus diagonal dominating the per-prime negatives.

\emph{Three questions.}
\textnormal{(L1)} Compute the cumulants of the regularized $[p]$ in
$\omega_\beta$ from the known Bost--Connes KMS formulae and compare with the explicit-formula
coefficients; if the candidate's mixed cumulants do not vanish at non-prime-powers, the state
family is not the right one.
\textnormal{(L2, where circularity could enter)} The twisted state
$\omega_\beta^\kappa(\cdot):=\omega_\beta(e^{\kappa/2}\cdot e^{\kappa/2})/\omega_\beta(e^\kappa)$ must
produce the archimedean density as a diagonal conditional variance; circularity could enter through
the positivity of the $\kappa$-twist --- if proving that $\omega_\beta^\kappa$ is a state turns out to
be equivalent to $W\le0$, the proposal is circular.
\textnormal{(L3)} Bost--Connes lacks the flip symmetry; the proposal derives it from
adelic Poisson à la Tate inside the GNS space, to be proved first in the semilocal $\{p,\infty\}$ case
(two places, fully explicit, with the Connes--Consani semilocal trace formulae as control).


\subsubsection*{Bost--Connes moments and twisted states}

Three requirements are imposed: (a) the essential point is the identity
$\langle D,D\rangle_W=-c\,\mathrm{Var}(\widehat D)$, not the positivity of the state; (b) the flip
requires resolution across the phase transition; (c) the regularization $\widehat D$ and the effective
window must be built explicitly.  Regarding (b): in Bost--Connes the
$\mathrm{KMS}_\beta$ state is unique for $\beta\in(0,1]$ --- unique at $\beta=1$ exactly --- and the
Galois simplex opens only for $\beta>1$.  So $\omega_1$ is well defined; the flip difficulty is instead
that $\beta\leftrightarrow1/\beta$ carries the unique-state region to the simplex region, hence cannot be
a system isomorphism.  This is used below.

\emph{Realization.}  In the type-I representation ($\beta>1$): $\mathcal H=\ell^2(\N)$, $He_n=(\log
n)e_n$, $\mu_me_n=e_{mn}$, and $[n]:=\mu_n+\mu_n^*$ is the shift $S_{\log n}$ verbatim; the Gibbs state is
$\omega_\beta(\cdot)=\mathrm{Tr}(e^{-\beta H}\cdot)/\zeta(\beta)$.

\begin{lemma}[raw covariance is exactly product]\label{lem:bc-covariance}
For $m,n\ge2$, $\ \omega_\beta([n])=0$ and
$\mathrm{Cov}_{\omega_\beta}([m],[n])=\delta_{mn}\,(1+n^{-\beta})$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
$\mathrm{Tr}(e^{-\beta H}\mu_m\mu_n^*)=\sum_k k^{-\beta}\langle e_k,\mu_m e_{k/n}\rangle[n\mid k]
=\delta_{mn}\sum_{n\mid k}k^{-\beta}=\delta_{mn}n^{-\beta}\zeta(\beta)$, so
$\omega_\beta(\mu_m\mu_n^*)=\delta_{mn}n^{-\beta}$; likewise $\omega_\beta(\mu_m^*\mu_n)=\delta_{mn}$; and
$\omega_\beta(\mu_{mn})=\sum_kk^{-\beta}\delta_{mnk,k}=0$.  Expanding
$[m][n]=(\mu_m+\mu_m^*)(\mu_n+\mu_n^*)$ into its four terms and using $\omega_\beta([n])=0$ gives
$\mathrm{Cov}=\delta_{mn}(1+n^{-\beta})$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[no-go for diagonal twists]\label{lem:bc-nogo-diagonal}
If $\kappa=f(H)$, the twisted state $\omega_\beta^\kappa$ still has
$\mathrm{Cov}([m],[n])\propto\delta_{mn}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
With $\kappa=f(H)$ diagonal, $\mathrm{Tr}(e^{-\beta H}e^{\kappa/2}\mu_m\mu_n^*e^{\kappa/2})=\sum_k
k^{-\beta}e^{f(\log k)}\langle e_k,\mu_m\mu_n^*e_k\rangle\propto\delta_{mn}$: no diagonal weight creates
off-diagonal correlation.
\end{proof}
\noindent Hence a twist producing off-diagonal covariances cannot be a function of the number operator;
the natural place to look for it is the transverse (flow) variable on the adelic GNS space rather than
$\ell^2(\N)$.

\begin{lemma}[twist positivity is free]\label{lem:bc-twist-positivity}
For any admissible $\kappa$, $\omega_\beta(e^{\kappa/2}\,\cdot\,e^{\kappa/2})$ is positive (it is
$\omega(B^*\!\cdot B)$), modulo domain questions only.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
$A\ge0\Rightarrow B^*AB\ge0$.
\end{proof}

Three readings.  Lemma~\ref{lem:bc-covariance}: the raw state is \emph{exactly} the product kernel ---
zero arithmetic content, so all of the Weil information must be created by the twist.
Lemma~\ref{lem:bc-nogo-diagonal}: the archimedean twist is \emph{forced} onto the variable conjugate to
the flow, which is exactly where $\mathcal A$ was built (on the boundary flow, not the number operator) ---
the structure pushes towards the correct geometry by itself.  Lemma~\ref{lem:bc-twist-positivity}
settles (a): the difficulty lands entirely on the moment identity, not on the positivity of the state.

\emph{A symmetrized duality (flip with Galois averaging).}  Since the flip crosses
unique~$\leftrightarrow$~simplex, define the symmetrized free energy
\[
   \widehat F_\beta(D):=
   \begin{cases}
      F_\beta(D), & \beta\le1,\\[3pt]
      \displaystyle\int_{\hat\Z^\times}F_\beta^{\sigma}(D)\,d\sigma\ \ (\text{Haar over the extremal
      KMS states}), & \beta>1,
   \end{cases}
\]
and consider the symmetrized thermal Riemann--Roch identity $\widehat F_\beta(D)-\widehat F_{1/\beta}(\kappa-D)=\deg D$, which is not proved here.  The average
expresses the expectation that Serre duality of the arithmetic square passes through averaging
over $\mathrm{Gal}(\Q^{\rm ab}/\Q)$ --- the $H^2$ side carries the Galois torsor.  Both sides are canonical.  Explicit regularization:
$\widehat D_\varepsilon:=e^{-\varepsilon H}De^{-\varepsilon H}$, window $\beta\in(1,1+\eta)$ open to the
right of $1$ --- criticality is reached as a limit, never touched; everything converges in the window
because $\zeta(\beta)<\infty$, which settles (c).

\emph{The torus lift.}  \textnormal{(Atomic part.)}  For purely almost-periodic
kernels (the atomic part), Bochner on compacts together with uniqueness of Bohr coefficients along the
dense winding give: positive-definiteness on $\mathbb T^\infty$ $\iff$ positive-definiteness on the
Kronecker line.  Thus for the atomic part the torus is a
change of coordinates and supplies no new positivity.  \textnormal{(The remainder.)}  The archimedean density is
\emph{not} almost-periodic (its Bohr coefficients vanish), so it does not live on $\mathbb T^\infty$; the
correct home of the lift is $\R\times\mathbb T^\infty$ --- the adele class space --- and there the
positive-definite completion freedom is exactly one: the transverse profile with which the archimedean
mass is distributed among the prime coordinates.

\subsubsection*{The archimedean partition problem}

\begin{problem}[archimedean partition problem]\label{prob:arch-partition}
Find nonnegative measures $\sigma_p$ on the log-scale line such that \textnormal{(i)}
$\hat\sigma_p(\xi)\ge D_p(\xi)$ for all $\xi\in\R$ and all primes $p$, with $D_p$ as in
\eqref{eq:sigma-domination} below, and \textnormal{(ii)} $\mathcal A-\sum_p\sigma_p$ is a
positive-definite distribution, where $\mathcal A$ is the archimedean-plus-pole distribution of the
explicit formula (Theorem~\ref{thm:explicit-channel}).
\end{problem}

\noindent This formulation is schematic.  If \textnormal{(i)} and \textnormal{(ii)} hold, the Weil
functional is a sum of positive-definite pieces, so Weil positivity and hence \textnormal{RH} follow.
By Proposition~\ref{prop:W5} no $\sigma_p\in L^1$ satisfies \textnormal{(i)}; the atomic choice
$\sigma_p=\sum_{k\ne0}(\log p)p^{-|k|/2}\delta_{k\log p}$ satisfies \textnormal{(i)} with equality, and
then \textnormal{(ii)} is Weil positivity itself, so the problem is equivalent to RH and is not a
reduction.

Each subproblem is classical: a single-frequency train against a measure is one-dimensional
Herglotz/Toeplitz, completely characterized.  The global problem is a resource allocation with LP/SDP
duality structure.  Heuristically, the total prime demand is comparable to the archimedean density at
leading order, so the margin is expected to be of RH size; we do not carry out this computation.  What
changed is the format:
from a monolithic spectral positivity to (i) a moment identity with a free variance, plus (ii) an
allocation with an explicit dual and classical subproblems.  The per-prime symbol changes sign (as noted under route elimination),
so the \emph{uniform} partition fails, and a partition, if it exists, is necessarily \emph{adaptive}
--- $\sigma_p$ loaded near the resonances of $p$ and emptied far from them.

\emph{Remaining questions.}  (1) Lemma~\ref{lem:bc-covariance} computes the raw moments; guided by
Lemma~\ref{lem:bc-nogo-diagonal} (twist on the flow variable), one should
compute $\mathrm{Cov}^\kappa([m],[n])$ for the simplest Gaussian twist in the adelic flow generator and
compare with the explicit-formula coefficients.  (2) Single-prime partition (treated below:
Proposition~\ref{prop:W5}, Lemma~\ref{lem:perprime-feasible}): the density formulation fails at large
resonances for every prime, while every bounded-window subproblem is feasible with mass arbitrarily
close to the peak demand.  (3) Global accounting (treated below, heuristically): the stacked per-prime
demand diverges.  (4) The flip (L3), and the semilocal $\{p,\infty\}$ case with the symmetrized (Galois-averaged)
duality.  The new content of the thermal proposal is thus contained in two statements: the
Weil--variance identity (Conjecture~\ref{conj:X3G}) and the archimedean partition problem
(Problem~\ref{prob:arch-partition}).


\subsubsection*{The single-prime partition problem and the global accounting}

We now treat the single-prime part of the partition problem.  The outcome closes no gap, but it removes
the density formulation.

\emph{Exact single-prime condition.}  Testing the per-prime Weil piece against positive-definite
autocorrelations $g=h\star\tilde h$ (so $\hat g\ge0$) and dualizing, the single-prime domination
$\int\sigma_p\,g\ge\sum_k(\log p)p^{-k/2}g(k\log p)$ for all admissible $g$ is equivalent, by Bochner,
to the pointwise frequency inequality
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sigma-domination}
   \hat\sigma_p(\xi)\;\ge\;D_p(\xi):=2\sum_{k\ge1}(\log p)\,p^{-k/2}\cos\!\big(k\,\xi\log p\big)
   \qquad(\forall\xi\in\R),
\end{equation}
whose right-hand side is \emph{almost-periodic} in $\xi$ with period $2\pi/\log p$ and peak value
$D_p(0)=2\log p/(\sqrt p-1)$ at every resonance $\xi\in(2\pi/\log p)\Z$.

\begin{proposition}[the $L^1$-density formulation is per-prime infeasible]\label{prop:W5}
If $\sigma_p$ is an $L^1$ density then $\hat\sigma_p$ is a positive-definite continuous function with
$\hat\sigma_p(\xi)\to0$ as $|\xi|\to\infty$ (Riemann--Lebesgue), while the demand $D_p$ does not decay.
Hence \eqref{eq:sigma-domination} fails at all sufficiently large resonances, for \emph{every} prime and
\emph{every} finite mass budget.  The archimedean class $\sigma_p$ cannot be a density: it must be a
measure with an atomic (almost-periodic) part, or the test class must be cut off.  In the classification
of \S\ref{sec:walls} this is $I(\mathcal I;C)$ with $\mathcal I=L^1$-density archimedean allocations and
$C=$ the per-prime domination \eqref{eq:sigma-domination}.
\end{proposition}
\noindent For example, a centred Gaussian density of mass $10\,D_p(0)$ and standard deviation $10^{-2}$
has $\hat\sigma_p(\xi)=10\,D_p(0)e^{-10^{-4}\xi^2/2}$, which is below $10^{-11}D_p(0)$ at the resonance
$\xi=2\pi n/\log p$ with $n=86$ for $p=2$ and $n=199$ for $p=5$; there the deficit is essentially the
full peak.  Proposition~\ref{prop:W5} is the frequency-side twin of the pole-pair obstruction
(Lemma~\ref{lem:pole-pair}): a smooth archimedean object cannot, unaided, dominate an atomic prime
demand across all scales.

\emph{The bounded-window problem is feasible per prime.}  If the frequency inequality
\eqref{eq:sigma-domination} is required only on a bounded window $|\xi|\le2R$ (the frequency-side
counterpart of a cutoff in the spirit of the compression of Connes--Consani~\cite{connesconsani2021}),
it becomes trivially solvable, with mass arbitrarily close to the peak demand.

\begin{lemma}[per-prime cutoff feasibility]\label{lem:perprime-feasible}
For each prime $p$, each $R<\infty$ and each $\varepsilon>0$ there is a nonnegative Gaussian density
$\sigma_p$ with $\int\sigma_p=(1+\varepsilon)\,\tfrac{2\log p}{\sqrt p-1}$ such that
$\hat\sigma_p(\xi)\ge D_p(\xi)$ for $|\xi|\le2R$; no nonnegative measure of mass smaller than
$D_p(0)=\tfrac{2\log p}{\sqrt p-1}$ satisfies this inequality at $\xi=0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Since $\cos\le1$, $D_p(\xi)\le2\log p\sum_{k\ge1}p^{-k/2}=D_p(0)$ for all $\xi$.  A centred Gaussian of
mass $M$ and variance $s^2$ has $\hat\sigma(\xi)=Me^{-s^2\xi^2/2}\ge Me^{-2s^2R^2}$ on $|\xi|\le2R$; take
$M=(1+\varepsilon)D_p(0)$ and $s$ so small that $e^{-2s^2R^2}\ge(1+\varepsilon)^{-1}$.  The lower bound
is $\hat\sigma_p(0)=\int\sigma_p\ge D_p(0)$.
\end{proof}
\noindent Thus the infimal mass on every bounded window equals the peak $D_p(0)$ ($3.35$, $3.00$, $2.60$
for $p=2,3,5$); it is attained by the atom $D_p(0)\delta_0$, and among $L^1$ densities it is not
attained once the window contains a nonzero resonance ($2R\ge2\pi/\log p$), since
$\hat\sigma(2\pi/\log p)=\hat\sigma(0)$ forces support on a lattice.  One may interpret this as saying that the per-prime
obstruction appears only in the limit of an unbounded window; this is an interpretation, not a theorem
about $\Omega_7$.

\emph{The global accounting (heuristic).}  Summing the per-prime peaks,
$\sum_{p\le P}2\log p/(\sqrt p-1)$ \emph{diverges} ($\sim2\sum_p\log p/\sqrt p$); the cumulative demand
is $13.4,\,23.6,\,41.5,\,129.2,\,405.1,\,1272.0$ for $P=11,31,101,10^3,10^4,10^5$ (direct summation).
The masses do add: by Lemma~\ref{lem:perprime-feasible}, $\int\sigma_p\ge D_p(0)$ for each $p$, and
$\xi=0$ is a common resonance of all $D_p$, so $\sum_p\sigma_p$ has infinite total mass.  This is not a
contradiction only because the archimedean-plus-pole distribution is not a finite measure in the
log-scale variable (the pole terms contribute $e^{\pm x/2}$); the comparison must be made locally in
$x$, where the atoms $k\log p$ of different primes are disjoint.  Heuristically, this local comparison is the boundary symbol positivity that underlies $\Omega_7$; we do not prove
an equivalence.  In this format every single-prime bounded-window subproblem is feasible
(Lemma~\ref{lem:perprime-feasible}), and the difficulty is the simultaneous treatment of all primes as
the window opens.

\emph{An operator-side analogue (heuristic).}  This frequency-side picture has an operator-side
analogue through Connes's colligation route, which we describe without proof: a regularized
colligation margin that survives the critical continuation $\omega\downarrow0$
and was positive for $\zeta$ in numerical tests, the Kre\u\i n--Langer negative-square index
$\mathrm{ind}_-$, which is expected to count off-line zeros, and a finite-prime colligation whose
\emph{scalar} truncation diverges at $\omega=0$ because the Euler product is a boundary object, an
operator-side analogue of Proposition~\ref{prop:W5}.  On both sides the difficulty appears as uniformity across the full family
(all configurations, all primes, the open cutoff) rather than in any single member.  The
archimedean construction of Connes--Consani~\cite{connesconsani2021} is the model for the per-prime
densities of Lemma~\ref{lem:perprime-feasible}; and their Riemann--Roch for $\overline{\operatorname{Spec}\Z}$
\cite{ConnesConsani2023} realizes (X3) in dimension one, leaving the surface (self-intersection / Hodge
index) version as the open part of the closure specification (Definition~\ref{def:closure-spec}).


\subsubsection*{A scale-uniform envelope inequality on the Li side}

On the Li side the limit over all scales has a clean shape.  Write,
via $\Omega_6$, $\lambda_n=\lambda_n^{\mathrm{arch}}+\lambda_n^{\mathrm{prime}}$ with the
Stieltjes-built split of $\log\xi$, where the prime part is defined by the regularized limit
\[
   \lambda_n^{\mathrm{prime}}:=-\lim_{N\to\infty}\Bigl(\sum_{m\le N}\Lambda(m)m^{-1}L^{(1)}_{n-1}(\log m)
   -\bigl(1-L_n(\log N)\bigr)\Bigr),\qquad L_n:=L_n^{(0)},
\]
cf.\ Bombieri--Lagarias~\cite{BombieriLagarias1999}; the counter-term is
$\int_0^{\log N}L^{(1)}_{n-1}(u)\,du=1-L_n(\log N)$, the main term of the sum by the prime number theorem.  The archimedean part is the \emph{ceiling}
\[
   \lambda_n^{\mathrm{arch}}=\tfrac{n}{2}\log\tfrac{n}{2\pi}+O(n),
\]
growing with scaling exponent $1$.  Numerical values of $\lambda_n^{\mathrm{prime}}$ and of the
envelope $E(n):=\max_{m\le n}|\lambda_m^{\mathrm{prime}}|$:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
\toprule
$n$ & $10$ & $40$ & $80$ & $120$ & $150$ & $170$\\
\midrule
$\lambda_n^{\mathrm{prime}}$ & $1.32$ & $1.16$ & $0.36$ & $2.09$ & $0.68$ & $-1.01$\\
ceiling $\tfrac{n}{2}\log\tfrac{n}{2\pi}$ & $2.32$ & $37.0$ & $101.8$ & $177.0$ & $238.0$ & $280.3$\\
ratio ceiling$/|\lambda^{\mathrm{prime}}|$ & $1.8$ & $31.8$ & $284$ & $84.8$ & $350$ & $276$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\noindent The ceiling row and the ratio row are consistent with $\tfrac n2\log\tfrac n{2\pi}$; the values
of $\lambda_n^{\mathrm{prime}}$ are not accompanied by a reproducible computation and are
reported as unverified.  In these numerical experiments a least-squares fit gives $E(n)\sim n^{\alpha}$
with $\alpha=0.264$, and $E(n)/(\sqrt n\log n)$ has maximum $0.111$ over $n\le170$ (also unverified).
This does not prove an asymptotic bound.  On the tested range the prime oscillation is dominated by the
archimedean term, and $\lambda_n>0$ for all $n\le170$ with margin increasing on the tested range.
Extrapolation to all scales is Conjecture~\ref{conj:envelope}; no finite fit supplies that uniform
bound.

\begin{conjecture}[a scale-uniform envelope]\label{conj:envelope}
There is $c>0$ with
$\bigl|\lambda_n-\tfrac n2\log\tfrac n{2\pi}-\tfrac{\gamma-1}2n\bigr|\le c\sqrt n\log n$ for all $n\ge2$.
\end{conjecture}

\noindent Under RH this holds (Lagarias~\cite[Thm.~6.1]{Lagarias2007}).  Conversely, the envelope gives
$\lambda_n>0$ for all large $n$, in particular $\lambda_n\ge-ce^{\varepsilon n}$, which implies RH by
\cite[Thm.~1]{BombieriLagarias1999}; directly, if RH fails,
$\lambda_n$ has exponentially growing oscillations (an off-line zero $\rho$ contributes a term of size
$|1-1/\rho|^n$ with $|1-1/\rho|>1$ for the member of the pair with $\Re\rho<\tfrac12$), which no
polynomial envelope accommodates.  The conjecture is therefore equivalent to RH, i.e.\ to the Li
criterion.

\begin{remark}[the finite window and its exact scope]\label{rmk:finite-window}
The measured constant in the preceding table is diagnostic only and cannot be extrapolated.  The
rigorous finite statement available is Proposition~\ref{prop:a1-finite-conditional}(i): a rational
interval computation (\texttt{scripts/fixed\_margin\_9\_20.py}, \texttt{scripts/fixed\_margin\_eta\_21\_149.py})
proves $D_n=2\lambda_n-A_n>0$ for $9\le n\le20$, and for $21\le n\le149$ conditionally on the
coefficient-tail bound stated there, which is tested numerically but not proved; for $1\le n\le8$ the
inequality rests on uncertified numerical values.  Under that bound this gives A1 on $9\le n\le149$
without using zeros or RH.  It does not supply the all-$n$ envelope of
Conjecture~\ref{conj:envelope}, and it does not bridge the range from $n=150$ to the ineffective threshold of
Proposition~\ref{prop:a1-finite-conditional}(ii).
\end{remark}

In this form the question is a \emph{single classical, scale-uniform inequality} on the regularized
prime-built sequence $\lambda_n^{\mathrm{prime}}$ defined above, with no surface, no $F_1$ and no Hodge
index in the statement; it is equivalent to RH and is not a reduction of it.  Heuristically, in the oscillatory (Plancherel--Rotach) regime
$L_{n-1}^{(1)}(\log m)\sim (\text{amplitude})\cos\!\big(2\sqrt{n\log m}-\theta\big)$, so
$\lambda_n^{\mathrm{prime}}$ is a stationary-phase sum of $\Lambda(m)m^{-1}$ against a chirp; the
$\sqrt n\log n$ envelope is exactly the square-root cancellation of that sum, and proving it is the
prime-number-theorem error term at RH strength.  For a function with an off-line zero $\rho$ the Li
coefficients acquire a term of size $|1-1/\rho|^n$, growing geometrically, which eventually overtakes
the ceiling; this is the Davenport--Heilbronn behaviour recorded earlier in the paper.

\emph{An incorrect separation of the zero side.}  One may attempt to isolate the zero-side
quantity
\[
   \lambda_n=(\text{arch.\ ceiling})-O(n),\qquad O(n):=\sum_\rho\Big(1-\tfrac1\rho\Big)^{\!n}.
\]
For a zero on the critical line $\rho=\tfrac12+i\gamma$ one has $1-\tfrac1\rho=e^{i\theta(\gamma)}$ with
$\theta(\gamma)=2\arctan\tfrac{1}{2\gamma}$ \emph{real} and $|1-\tfrac1\rho|=1$ \emph{exactly}, so
$(1-\tfrac1\rho)^n=e^{in\theta(\gamma)}$ is pure oscillation; pairing $\rho$ with $\bar\rho$ and using
$\theta(\gamma)\sim1/\gamma$ together with the density
$dN(\gamma)=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\log\tfrac{\gamma}{2\pi}\,d\gamma$,
\[
   O(n)\approx2\,\mathrm{Re}\!\int e^{in/\gamma}\,\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\log\tfrac{\gamma}{2\pi}\,d\gamma .
\]
The phase $\varphi(\gamma)=n/\gamma$ has $\varphi'(\gamma)=-n/\gamma^2\neq0$ (no stationary point); the
substitution $u=n/\gamma$ exhibits $\asymp\sqrt n$ effective oscillations against a $\log$ weight, and
the square-root cancellation gives $|O(n)|\asymp\sqrt n\log n$.  This reasoning is invalid: even
under RH the summands in $O(n)$ have modulus one and do not tend to zero, so the isolated series is not
convergent.  Only the paired, symmetrically regularized Li combination
$\sum_\rho[1-(1-1/\rho)^n]$ is defined.  Replacing its counting measure by a density before preserving
that cancellation is therefore not justified.

\begin{remark}[an invalid envelope reduction]\label{prop:envelope-reduction}
The following chain is recorded only to identify its error.  It treats the undefined $O(n)$ as though it were a convergent series and asserts that its sole
load-bearing hypothesis is $\theta(\gamma)
\in\R$ for every zero, i.e.\ $|1-\tfrac1\rho|=1$ for every $\rho$.  If some zero has $\rho=\beta+i\gamma$
with $\beta\neq\tfrac12$, then $(1-\tfrac1\rho)^n=r^n e^{in\theta}$ with
$r=|1-\tfrac1\rho|=\big(1+\tfrac{1-2\beta}{|\rho|^2}\big)^{1/2}$; the functional-equation partner with
$\beta<\tfrac12$ has $r>1$, so its term grows geometrically and no cancellation can hold, whence
$|O(n)|\gtrsim r^n$ eventually exceeds $\sqrt n\log n$.  Therefore
\[
   |O(n)|\le c\,\sqrt n\,\log n\ (\forall n)
   \iff |1-\tfrac1\rho|=1\ (\forall\rho)
   \iff \textnormal{RH},
\]
with no slack at any step.  Equivalently: the square-root cancellation would be contingent on
the reality of $\theta(\gamma)$; every phase-blind method (stationary phase, saddle point,
Plancherel--Rotach) is by construction insensitive to zero locations and hence structurally unable to
supply this hypothesis.  Since $O(n)$ is not defined, the displayed equivalence is not a theorem and
is not used anywhere in this paper.  Any zero-side proof must retain the full regularized Li
combination throughout.
\end{remark}
\noindent The heuristic still locates the expected RH-strength obstruction, but it does not constitute
a lossless reduction of Conjecture~\ref{conj:envelope} to the statement that no zero has $\beta\neq\tfrac12$
--- which classical zero-free-region technology narrows (to $\beta>1-c/\log|\gamma|$) but has never
pushed to the critical line.  The gap between the classical zero-free region and $\beta=\tfrac12$ is
the open part, and it is not closed by any finite numerical verification (which certifies finitely
many $n$ over zeros already known to be on the line, never the $n\to\infty$ tail of a putative off-line
zero at arbitrary height).

\emph{Three classical routes.}  A classical attack on
Conjecture~\ref{conj:envelope} can be mounted from three directions; each is followed heuristically
to the line where $\sqrt n\log n$ is produced, and each ends at a classical statement of RH strength.

\begin{remark}[three heuristic routes, with the convergence defect exposed]\label{prop:three-routes}
Write $w_\rho:=1-\tfrac1\rho$.  The three routes below are diagnostic only.  Any occurrence of
$O(n)=\sum_\rho w_\rho^{\,n}$ is formal, because that isolated series is divergent; the legitimate
object is the regularized Li combination.

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{(R1) Direct bound.} The functional equation gives the exact identity
$w_{1-\rho}=1/w_\rho$ and $|w_\rho|^2-1=(1-2\beta)/|\rho|^2$; hence the pair $\{\rho,1-\rho\}$ contributes
$w_\rho^{\,n}+w_\rho^{-n}$, which is bounded \emph{iff} $|w_\rho|=1$.  The triangle bound diverges
($\sum_\rho|w_\rho|^n=\infty$ on the line), so cancellation in $\sum_\gamma\cos(n\theta_\gamma)$ is
mandatory, and it requires $\theta_\gamma\in\R$.  \emph{Load-bearing step:} $|w_\rho|=1$ for all $\rho$.

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{(R2) Closing the strip.} Off-line zeros contribute $\sum 2r_\rho^{\,n}\cos(n\theta_\rho)$
with $r_\rho>1$ for the $\beta<\tfrac12$ partner; a single such zero forces $|O(n)|>\sqrt n\log n$ beyond
a finite $n_*$.  Unconditional zero-density estimates ($N(\sigma,T)\ll T^{a(1-\sigma)}\log T$; Ingham
$a=3$~\cite{Ingham}, improved by Huxley~\cite{Huxley} and Guth--Maynard~\cite{GuthMaynard}) lower the exponent but never give
$0$ in the open strip.  \emph{Load-bearing step:} $N(\sigma,T)=0$ on $\tfrac12<\sigma<1$.

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{(R3) Prime-side rewrite.} With $L_{n-1}^{(1)}(\log m)\sim A\cos(2\sqrt{n\log m}-\varphi)$,
$\lambda_n^{\mathrm{prime}}\sim-\int x^{-1}A\cos(2\sqrt{n\log x})\,d\psi(x)$.  The main term ($d\psi\approx
dx$, unconditional) is $O(\sqrt n\log n)$; the error $\psi(x)-x=-\sum_\rho x^\rho/\rho$ re-injects the
zeros, and its contribution is $O(\sqrt n\log n)$ \emph{iff} $\psi(x)-x=O(\sqrt x\log^2 x)$.
\emph{Load-bearing step:} the RH-strength prime-counting error term.
\smallskip
The three load-bearing steps are classical equivalents of RH (the Riemann hypothesis itself; its
zero-density formulation; von~Koch's error term, H.~von~Koch, Acta Math.\ \textbf{24} (1901)).  In
every route the main term is unconditional and yields $\sqrt n\log n$, and the RH-strength content sits
in the tail or error term.  These comparisons explain why the familiar classical estimates reach RH-strength inputs.  They do not
prove a three-front equivalence, because R1--R2 use the divergent separated series and R3 is an
asymptotic sketch.  They are not used as closure lemmas.
\end{remark}

\emph{Why operator-norm comparisons with the archimedean gap fail (heuristic).}  A candidate source
of positivity is the archimedean place dominating the prime term (Connes--Consani~\cite{connesconsani2021},
the partition Problem~\ref{prob:arch-partition}, the Gibbs proposal of Conjecture~\ref{conj:X3G}).
The following comparison of growth rates indicates why a \emph{perturbative} form of this idea cannot
work; it is not a theorem excluding every perturbative argument.
\emph{(i)} The archimedean positivity margin of the Weil form restricted to test functions supported
in an interval of length $a$ grows only logarithmically as $a\to0^+$ (like $\log(1/a)$), and
decreases as the support grows.  \emph{(ii)} The prime perturbation grows without bound
as the support opens (each prime power enters once $k\log p$ is below the support, with oscillatory
weight, and the $\ell^1$ mass of these weights is $\asymp e^{a/2}$).  A slowly varying margin cannot
dominate an unboundedly growing perturbation in norm, so one does not expect a uniform operator-norm
comparison $\|\text{prime}\|<(\text{archimedean gap})$ to hold without deciding RH.  The
unconditional positivity in this family is the small-support regime, where finitely many primes
contribute and the archimedean term dominates a \emph{finite} sum; this regime never reaches the
arithmetic scale that decides RH.  A related precedent: Conrey--Li~\cite{ConreyLi} showed that the
de~Branges positivity conditions that would have delivered RH fail.  The heuristic conclusion is that
an archimedean route to $\Omega_7$ would have to exploit the fine cancellation of the prime comb against
the archimedean symbol, not a norm bound, and that cancellation is of RH strength.


\subsubsection*{Summary of the attempts on \texorpdfstring{$\Omega_7$}{Omega7}}

The approaches in this subsection describe alternative coordinates and the points where they
fail.  The shortest conditional reduction is not the cone specification M2: the exact arithmetic
bookkeeping of \S\ref{subsec:omega7-a0a1} reduces the forward proof to A0 plus the single compact
Chebyshev--Laguerre inequality A1.  A0 follows from the classical Vinogradov--Korobov remainder; A1 is
checked by interval arithmetic for $9\le n\le149$ (for $21\le n\le149$ conditionally on an unproved
coefficient-tail bound; numerically observed for $n\le8$), and under RH
Proposition~\ref{prop:a1-finite-conditional}(ii) gives it for all sufficiently large $n$, with no
effective threshold.  Thus neither the conic experiments nor the numerical envelopes are links of that reduction.  Their unconditional by-products remain useful diagnostics, but the sole
RH-strength input in the direct route is uniform A1.  Uniform A1 implies $\lambda_n>0$ for all
$n$, which by Li's criterion is equivalent to RH; the open step is therefore at least as strong as RH
itself, and the reduction makes no progress towards RH.  No statement in this subsection supplies
that missing sign.

\subsection{Step 5 substructure (continued): de Bruijn--Newman cascade characterization}\label{subsec:cascade}

This subsection is self-contained: it characterizes the de Bruijn--Newman constant by finite terminal
sections, but it does not evaluate that constant and therefore does not prove RH.

\begin{definition}[de Bruijn--Newman flow and terminal cascade]\label{def:flow-defect}
Let
\[
   \Xi_t(z)=8\int_0^\infty \Phi(u)e^{4tu^2}\cos(2zu)\,du=8H_{4t}(2z),
   \qquad \partial_t\Xi_t=-\partial_z^2\Xi_t,\qquad \Xi_0=\Xi,
\]
with $H_t$ as in No-go~\ref{ng:dbn} (so $\Xi_0(z)=8H_0(2z)=\Xi(z)$, and the heat equation is preserved
because $\partial_t$ and $\partial_z^2$ both acquire the factor $4$).  The zeros of $\Xi_t$ are half
the zeros of $H_{4t}$, so they are all real exactly when $4t\ge\Lambda_{\mathrm{dBN}}$.  Let
$\Lambda_\Xi$ be the de Bruijn--Newman constant of this flow, i.e.\ the infimum of the $t$ for which
$\Xi_t$ has only real zeros; thus $\Lambda_\Xi=\Lambda_{\mathrm{dBN}}/4$, and the known bounds give
$0\le\Lambda_\Xi\le0.05$.  Fix $z_0=t_0-iy$ and the reference  Fix $z_0=t_0-iy$ and the reference
form
\[
   c^*A_Nc=\frac L{2\pi}\|R_c\|_{L^2(\R)}^2.
\]
For the Pick jet matrix $J_N(t)$ of $-\Xi_t'/\Xi_t$ at $z_0$, define
\[
   \delta_N(t):=\min_{c\ne0}\frac{c^*J_N(t)c}{c^*A_Nc},
   \qquad
   t_N^*:=\sup\{t:\delta_N(t)<0\}.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[$\alpha1$: positivity in the real-zero region]\label{lem:alpha1}
For $t\ge\Lambda_\Xi$ all zeros of $\Xi_t$ are real, hence $J_N(t)$ is a Gram matrix and
$\delta_N(t)\ge0$.  Consequently
\[
   t_N^*\le\Lambda_\Xi\qquad(N\ge1).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
This is the defining property of $\Lambda_\Xi$ and the Cauchy-transform Pick-kernel Gram
identity of Lemma~\ref{lem:cauchy-pick}, applied to the zero measure of $\Xi_t$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[$\alpha2$: regular flow derivative]\label{lem:alpha2}
In the real-zero region, write the zeros as $\gamma_j(t)$.  With principal-value pairing,
\[
   \dot\gamma_j=2\sum_{k\ne j}\frac1{\gamma_j-\gamma_k}.
\]
Fix $c$, put $f=|R_c|^2$ and $g_j:=\operatorname{dist}(\gamma_j,\textnormal{other zeros})$, and assume
\textnormal{(a)} $\sum_j g_j^{-1}(\log(2+|\gamma_j|))^2(1+|\gamma_j|)^{-3}<\infty$, locally uniformly in
$t$, and \textnormal{(b)} the series $\sum_j f'(\gamma_j)\dot\gamma_j$ converges locally uniformly in
$t$.  Then $\frac{d}{dt}c^*J_N(t)c=\sum_jf'(\gamma_j)\,\dot\gamma_j$, with an absolutely convergent
outer sum, and formally (by antisymmetrization)
\[
   \frac{d}{dt}\,c^*J_N(t)c
   =
   2\sum_{j<k}
   \frac{f'(\gamma_j)-f'(\gamma_k)}{\gamma_j-\gamma_k},
\]
each summand being regular as $\gamma_j\to\gamma_k$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We assume the counting bound of Lemma~\ref{lem:alpha-L2}, whose proof is only sketched.
By hypothesis \textnormal{(b)} the zero-side Gram sum $c^*J_N(t)c=\sum_j f(\gamma_j(t))$ may be
differentiated term by term.  Substituting the Calogero equation gives $\frac{d}{dt}c^*J_Nc=\sum_j f'(\gamma_j)\,\dot\gamma_j
=2\sum_j f'(\gamma_j)\sum_{k\ne j}\frac1{\gamma_j-\gamma_k}$.  Antisymmetrizing $j\leftrightarrow k$ in
$f'(\gamma_j)/(\gamma_j-\gamma_k)$ turns this into the displayed paired divided difference; the numerator
$f'(\gamma_j)-f'(\gamma_k)$ vanishes linearly as $\gamma_j\to\gamma_k$, so every summand is regular.
It remains to bound the inner principal values and the outer sum.

\emph{Step 1: the inner field is a convergent principal value with a local collision term.}
Fix $j$ and set $S_j:=2\,\mathrm{PV}\sum_{k\ne j}(\gamma_j-\gamma_k)^{-1}$, the pairing being the
symmetric one $|\gamma_k-\gamma_j|\le R$, $R\to\infty$.  The block $|\gamma_k-\gamma_j|\le1$ contains
$O_t((\log(2+|\gamma_j|))^2)$ zeros by Lemma~\ref{lem:alpha-L2}, each contributing at most $g_j^{-1}$.
For the tail, Abel summation against the counting function of Lemma~\ref{lem:alpha-L2}, whose remainder
is $O_t((\log u)^2)$, gives
\[
   \sum_{1<|\gamma_k-\gamma_j|\le R}\frac1{\gamma_j-\gamma_k}
   =O_t\bigl((\log(2+|\gamma_j|))^2\bigr)
\]
uniformly in $R$: the smooth densities on the two sides of $\gamma_j$ differ by
$\tfrac1{2\pi}\log\tfrac{|\gamma_j|+u}{||\gamma_j|-u|}$, whose integral against $du/u$ is bounded, and the
remainder terms contribute $\int_1^\infty(\log(|\gamma_j|+u))^2u^{-2}\,du=O((\log(2+|\gamma_j|))^2)$.  Thus
$S_j$ exists as a symmetric principal value at every simple zero, and
\[
   |S_j|\le C_t\,(\log(2+|\gamma_j|))^2\bigl(1+g_j^{-1}\bigr).
\]

\emph{Step 2: the outer sum converges absolutely.}  Since $R_c(u)=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\overline{c_j}
(u-z_0)^{-(j+1)}$ has leading decay $(u-z_0)^{-1}$, the sampling weight $f=|R_c|^2$ satisfies
$f(u)=O(|u|^{-2})$ and $f'(u)=O(|u|^{-3})$ as $|u|\to\infty$, with constants depending only on $c$ and
$z_0$.  Hence
\[
   \Bigl|\sum_j f'(\gamma_j)S_j\Bigr|
   \le C\sum_j\frac{(\log(2+|\gamma_j|))^2(1+g_j^{-1})}{(1+|\gamma_j|)^{3}}<\infty,
\]
the series converging by $N(T)=O(T\log T)$ and hypothesis \textnormal{(a)}.  This proves
$\frac{d}{dt}c^*J_Nc=\sum_jf'(\gamma_j)S_j$ with an absolutely convergent outer sum.  The rearrangement
into the paired divided-difference form is formal: the double series is not absolutely convergent
(for fixed $j$, $\sum_k|\gamma_j-\gamma_k|^{-1}$ diverges), and the paired expression is to be read as
the limit of the same symmetric truncations; we do not prove that this rearrangement is legitimate in
general.
\end{proof}

\begin{conjecture}[$\alpha3$: Gronwall rigidity in the real-zero region]\label{thm:alpha3}
For $t$ in any compact subinterval of the open real-zero region there is a constant $C_N$, depending on
$N$, $L$ and the minimal gap of the zeros in the active window, such that the one-sided derivatives of
$\delta_N$ satisfy
\[
   \left|\frac{d}{dt}\delta_N(t)\right|\le C_N\sqrt{\delta_N(t)}.
\]
Thus positivity at level $\delta_N=m$ would persist backward in flow time for at least
$2\sqrt m/C_N$.
\end{conjecture}

\begin{remark}[heuristic for Conjecture~\ref{thm:alpha3}]
At a minimizing vector one would use a Danskin-type formula for the one-sided derivatives of the
minimum $\delta_N$, the elementary bound $|f'(\gamma_j)|^2\le2f(\gamma_j)\sup|f''|$ for $f\ge0$, and
Cauchy--Schwarz in the derivative formula of Lemma~\ref{lem:alpha2}.  The sampling sum of $f(\gamma_j)$
is $\delta_N$ times the reference denominator, but Cauchy--Schwarz also brings in
$\sum_j\sup|f''|\,S_j^2$, and $S_j$ contains the collision term $g_j^{-1}$; this is why $C_N$ cannot be
uniform near collisions and why the statement is not proved here.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[$\alpha4$-L1: de Bruijn confinement]\label{lem:alpha-L1}\label{lem:alpha-L1-full}
Let
\[
   B(t):=\sup\{|\Im\rho|:\Xi_t(\rho)=0\}.
\]
Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\roman*)}]
\item the non-real zeros of $\Xi_t$ occur in conjugate pairs;
\item \textnormal{(de Bruijn)} for $t_2\ge t_1$,
\[
   B(t_2)^2\le \max\{B(t_1)^2-2(t_2-t_1),0\};
\]
\item for every $t\ge0$, $B(t)\le\sqrt{\max\{\tfrac14-2t,0\}}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $\Phi$ is real and even, $\Xi_t$ is real on $\R$ and even; the zero set is therefore invariant
under conjugation, proving (i).  Assertion (ii) is de Bruijn's strip-narrowing theorem for the forward
heat flow \cite[Thm.~13]{deBruijn}, applied to the evolution from $t_1$ to $t_2$.  For (iii), apply (ii) from $0$ to $t$, using the
critical strip $B(0)\le1/2$.  No backward bound for $t<0$ is used in this paper.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[$\alpha4$-L2: uniform zero counting; proof sketched]\label{lem:alpha-L2}\label{lem:alpha-L2-full}
Fix a compact flow interval $t\in[-T_0,T_1]$ on which $B(t)<4$ (for $t\ge0$ this holds by
Lemma~\ref{lem:alpha-L1}(iii)).  There are constants $C,X_0$ such that, for
$T\ge X_0$,
\[
   N_{\Xi_t}(T+1)-N_{\Xi_t}(T)\le C(\log T)^2,
\]
\[
   N_{\Xi_t}(T+H)-N_{\Xi_t}(T)
   =
   \int_T^{T+H}\frac1{2\pi}\log\frac{u}{2\pi}\,du+O_{T_0,T_1}((\log T)^2),
\]
uniformly for $0<H\le T$ and uniformly in $t$ on the compact interval.  Equivalently, the flowed
argument-error term satisfies $S_t(T)=O((\log T)^2)$ uniformly on compact $t$-ranges.
\end{lemma}

\noindent For each fixed $t>0$ a sharper counting formula, with error $O(\log T)$, is known: Ki, Kim and
Lee (Adv.\ Math.\ \textbf{222} (2009)) and Rodgers and Tao~\cite{RodgersTao} give
$N_t(T)=\tfrac T{4\pi}\log\tfrac T{4\pi}-\tfrac T{4\pi}+\tfrac t{16}\log T+O(\log T)$ in the
normalization of $H_t$, which differs from the present one by fixed rescalings of $z$ and of $t$.  The proof below is a sketch of the weaker bound with explicit uniformity in $t$.

\begin{proof}[Proof (sketch)]
\emph{Step 1: upper bound by contour shift.}  The kernel $\Phi$ extends holomorphically to
$|\Im u|<\pi/4$ and satisfies
\[
   |\Phi(u+i\alpha)|\le C e^{9|u|/2}e^{-\pi\cos(2\alpha)e^{2|u|}}
\]
(by evenness of $\Phi$).
For $z=x+iy$, $|y|\le9$, and large $x$, shift $u\mapsto u+i\alpha$ with
$\delta:=\pi/4-\alpha$.  Then
\[
   |\Xi_t(z)|
   \le
   e^{-(\pi/4-\delta)x}
   \int_\R |\Phi(u+i\alpha)|e^{|t|u^2+9|u|}\,du
   \le
   e^{-(\pi/4-\delta)x}\exp\{C+|t|(\tfrac12\log(1/\delta))^2\}\delta^{-C}.
\]
Taking $\delta=1/x$ gives
\[
   |\Xi_t(z)|\le e^{-\pi x/4}\exp\{C_t(\log x)^2\},
\]
uniformly for $t$ in the chosen compact interval.

\emph{Step 2: saddle lower bound at height $4$.}  The $n=1$ term in $\Phi$ dominates with relative
error $O(e^{-2u})$.  For
\[
   h(u)=\frac92u+tu^2-\pi e^{2u}+izu
\]
the saddle is
\[
   u^*(z)=\frac12\log\frac{x}{2\pi}+\frac{i\pi}{4}+O_t\!\left(\frac{\log x}{x}\right),
   \qquad
   h''(u^*)=-2iz+O(1).
\]
At height $y=4$, Laplace's method gives
\[
   \Xi_t(x+4i)=A_t(x+4i)(1+O(x^{-1/2})),
\]
where
\[
   \log|A_t(x+4i)|
   =
   -\frac{\pi x}{4}
   +\frac{t}{4}\left(\log\frac{x}{2\pi}\right)^2
   +2\log\frac{x}{2\pi}
   +O_t(1).
\]
The terms $n\ge2$ of $\Phi$ carry the factors $e^{-\pi n^2e^{2u}}$ and are smaller than the
$n=1$ term near the saddle; we do not write out the estimate of their relative size.  In particular
$\Xi_t$ has no zeros on the horizontal line $\Im z=4$ for large $x$.

\emph{Step 3: Jensen.}  Apply Jensen to disks $D(x_0+4i,5)$, which cover the relevant strip over
$[x_0-3,x_0+3]$.  The boundary upper bound from Step 1 and the center lower bound from Step 2 give
\[
   n(D(x_0+4i,4.6))
   \le
   \log\frac{\max_{\partial D}|\Xi_t|}{|\Xi_t(x_0+4i)|}
   \le
   O_t((\log x_0)^2).
\]

\emph{Step 4: main term.}  The argument principle on
$[T,T+H]\times[-4,4]$, which contains all zeros with real part in $[T,T+H]$ because $B(t)<4$, using the saddle approximation on the horizontal sides and Step 3 on the
vertical sides, gives the Riemann--von Mangoldt main term with error $O((\log T)^2)$.  The sharper
$O(\log T)$ error for fixed $t$ is the cited result above; the $(\log T)^2$ bound suffices below.
\end{proof}

\noindent\emph{Notation for Propositions~\ref{lem:alpha-L3}--\ref{thm:alpha4}.}  $W=\pi N/L$;
$s=(u-t_0)/W$; $\widehat\varphi(s)=\psi(s)-\log|s|-1$, with $\psi$ as in
Proposition~\ref{thm:alpha4}, is the normalised logarithmic potential of the uniform root pattern on
$[-W,W]$ minus that of the pole, so that $\widehat\varphi(s)\to0$ as $|s|\to\infty$;
$\widehat\varphi_{\rm out}:=\sup_{|s|>2}\widehat\varphi(s)$; $t_c:=y/W$ is the normalisation point,
$\widehat\varphi(t_c)=\log(W/ey)+o(1)$; $d:=|\gamma_c-t_0|$ and $t_d:=\sqrt{d^2+y^2}/W$; $t_1\in(0,1)$
is a fixed fraction; $X_R$ is the abscissa beyond which $|R(u)|\le|u|^{-1}\|R\|_{L^2}$.  With these
conventions $e^{2N[\widehat\varphi_{\rm out}-\widehat\varphi(t_c)]}=(e^{1+\widehat\varphi_{\rm out}+o(1)}yL/\pi N)^{2N}$,
which is the second form displayed in Proposition~\ref{lem:alpha-L3}.

\begin{proposition}[$\alpha4$-L3: conditional interpolator tail]\label{lem:alpha-L3}\label{lem:alpha-L3-full}
Assume that the active-window interpolators have polynomial escape scale $X_R$ and that their empirical
root measures satisfy the potential-discrepancy estimate $O(L^2\log N)$ uniformly in the far region.
Let $R=P/(u-z_0)^N$, with all roots of $P$ in the active window
$|u-t_0|\le W=\pi N/L$.  Then
\[
   \sum_{|\gamma-t_0|>2W}|R(\gamma)|^2
   \le
   e^{2N[\widehat\varphi_{\rm out}-\widehat\varphi(t_c)](1+o(1))}\,c^*A_Nc
   =
   \left(\frac{CyL}{N}\right)^{2N(1+o(1))}c^*A_Nc.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Step 1: sampling to $L^2$ without exponential loss.}  In the far region $R$ has no zeros or
poles at distance less than a fixed fraction of the distance to the active window.  On each unit
interval $I_d$ at distance $d$, Nikolskii's inequality for $P$ gives
\[
   \sup_{I_d}|R|^2\le C N^2\int_{2I_d}|R|^2.
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:alpha-L2}, the number of zeros in $I_d$ is $O((\log d)^2)$.  Summing over far
intervals yields
\[
   \sum_{\rm far}|R(\gamma)|^2
   \le
   C N^2\sum_d(\log d)^2\int_{2I_d}|R|^2
   \le
   C N^2(\log X_R)^2\|R\|^2_{L^2({\rm far})},
\]
where polynomial control of $X_R$ is one of the stated hypotheses.  This is subfactorial.

\emph{Step 2: far $L^2$ mass by potential.}  For $|u-t_0|>2W$,
\[
   \log|R(u)|=N\bigl[U^{\mu_N}(u)-\log|u-z_0|\bigr],
\]
where $\mu_N$ is the empirical root measure.  Comparing $\mu_N$ with the uniform model by partial
summation against $N_t-\mathrm{mean}$ and using $S_t=O(L^2)$ from Lemma~\ref{lem:alpha-L2}, the exponent
error is $O(L^2\log N)=o(N\log N)$ in the regime of the theorem.  The potential calculation is the one described for the
upper side of Conjecture~\ref{thm:two-sided}; it is only sketched there, so this step is a sketch and the
proposition should be read with that proviso.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[$\alpha4$-L4: conditional central derivative]\label{lem:alpha-L4}\label{lem:alpha-L4-full}
Let the explicit interpolator have a real root at $\gamma_c$, with
$|\gamma_c-t_0|\le t_1W$, and set
\[
   \eta:=\min\left\{1,\frac{L}{2\pi}\operatorname{dist}(\gamma_c,\textnormal{another active object})\right\}.
\]
Assume uniformly that the remaining-root potential discrepancy is at most $CL^2\log N$ and that the
normalizing archimedean form has the asserted potential upper bound.  Then
\[
   \frac{2|R'(\gamma_c)|^2}{c^*A_Nc}
   \ge
   \frac{\pi L}{y}\,\eta^2 e^{-C L^2\log N}
   \exp\{2N[\widehat\varphi(t_d)-\widehat\varphi(t_c)](1+o(1))\}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We have
\[
   R'(\gamma_c)=\frac{\prod_j(\gamma_c-x_j)}{(\gamma_c-z_0)^N},
\]
where the product omits the root at $\gamma_c$.  The two nearest-neighbor factors contribute at least
$(\eta\,2\pi/L)^2$.  For the remaining factors, compare
\[
   \sum_j\log|\gamma_c-x_j|
\]
with the corresponding potential integral by partial summation.  Lemma~\ref{lem:alpha-L2} gives the
error bound $C L^2\log N$.  The pole contributes $(d^2+y^2)^{-N}$ exactly.  The denominator
$c^*A_Nc\le (L/2\pi)\|R\|^2$ is bounded from above by the same potential calculation, with the same
loss.  Thus the displayed lower bound follows.  Numerically observed (unverified) normalizations suggest
an $N^{-3/2}$ loss, but the proof only uses the subfactorial factor $e^{-C L^2\log N}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[$\alpha4$: conditional quadratic detection of a complex pair]\label{thm:alpha4}
Let $t<\Lambda_\Xi$ and let $\{z_w,\bar z_w\}$, $z_w=\gamma_c-iv$, be a non-real pair of
$\Xi_t$ with $|\gamma_c-t_0|\le t_1\pi N/L$.  Let the active window be the largest interval
$|u-t_0|\le W_N$ containing at most $N-1$ zeros of $\Xi_t$ (one counted per non-real pair), assume
$W_N\ge\pi N/L$, and assume the uniform hypotheses of
Propositions~\ref{lem:alpha-L3} and~\ref{lem:alpha-L4}, and assume that the cubic remainder below is
smaller than half of the displayed quadratic term.  Then
\[
   \delta_N(t)
   \le
   \left(\frac{C yL}{N}\right)^{2N(1+o(1))}
   -
   v^2\,\frac{\pi L}{y}\,\eta^2e^{-C L^2\log N}
   \exp\{2N[\widehat\varphi(t_d)-\widehat\varphi(t_c)](1+o(1))\}
   +O(v^3\|\cdot\|),
\]
where
\[
   \widehat\varphi(t)=\psi(t)-\log|t|-1,\qquad
   \psi(t)=\tfrac12[(1-t)\log|1-t|+(1+t)\log(1+t)].
\]
In particular, $\delta_N(t)<0$ once
\[
   v^2\ge 2\,\eta^{-2}\,\frac{y}{\pi L}\,e^{C L^2\log N}
   \exp\{2N[\widehat\varphi_{\rm out}-\widehat\varphi(t_d)](1+o(1))\}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\noindent\emph{Size of the active window.}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:alpha-L2} the interval $|u-t_0|\le\pi N/L$ contains about
$\tfrac NL\log\tfrac N{2eL}$ zeros once $\pi N/L\gg|t_0|$, which exceeds $N-1$ for
$N>(e|t_0|/\pi)\log(|t_0|/2\pi)$ (for example about $1500$ zeros against $999$ when $t_0=100$,
$N=1000$).  For such $N$ one has $W_N<\pi N/L$, the hypothesis $W_N\ge\pi N/L$ fails, and the tail
estimate of Proposition~\ref{lem:alpha-L3} is not available for the window; the passage $N\to\infty$ at
fixed $t_0$ is therefore open.  Even for smaller $N$ the hypothesis is restrictive: the interval
$|u-t_0|\le\pi N/L$ contains about $N$ zeros on average, so $W_N\ge\pi N/L$ holds only when the local
zero count is below its mean.  A formulation with $W:=W_N$ in place of $\pi N/L$ throughout
Propositions~\ref{lem:alpha-L3}--\ref{thm:alpha4} would remove this hypothesis; it is not carried out
here.

\begin{proof}
Use the explicit test function
\[
   R(u)=\frac{P(u)}{(u-z_0)^N}.
\]
The numerator $P$ is chosen with real roots at the real zeros in the active window, one real root at
$\gamma_c$, and one complex root at one member of every additional non-real pair in the window.
Those additional pairs contribute
$2\Re\{R(z')\overline{R(\bar z')}\}=0$ exactly.  For the target pair, expanding around the real
root at $\gamma_c$ gives, without any real-coefficient assumption,
\[
   R(z_w)\overline{R(\bar z_w)}
   =
   -v^2|R'(\gamma_c)|^2+O(v^3),
\]
so the rank-two hyperbolic contribution is
$-2v^2|R'(\gamma_c)|^2+O(v^3)$.  The real far-zero contribution is bounded by
Proposition~\ref{lem:alpha-L3}, while Proposition~\ref{lem:alpha-L4} supplies the displayed lower bound for the
central derivative.  The hypothesis on the number of zeros in the active window guarantees that
$\deg P\le N-1$, so that $R$ lies in the jet span.
\end{proof}

\begin{conjecture}[terminal cascade formula for de Bruijn--Newman]\label{thm:alpha5}
\label{thm:cascade-main}
\[
   \lim_{N\to\infty}t_N^*=\Lambda_\Xi.
\]
\end{conjecture}

\noindent The inequality $t_N^*\le\Lambda_\Xi$ for every $N$ is proved (Lemma~\ref{lem:alpha1}).
The following argument would give the reverse inequality if the hypotheses of
Proposition~\ref{thm:alpha4} held uniformly for every fixed non-real pair as $N\to\infty$, with a
detection threshold tending to zero; by the preceding paragraph the window hypothesis fails for large $N$
at fixed $t_0$, so this is not a proof.

\begin{proof}[Conditional argument]
Lemma~\ref{lem:alpha1} gives $t_N^*\le\Lambda_\Xi$ for every $N$.  Conversely, fix
$t<\Lambda_\Xi$.  By the definition of $\Lambda_\Xi$ and forward invariance of
real-rootedness~\cite{deBruijn}, $\Xi_t$ has a non-real conjugate pair, with finite
height $v>0$ and center $\gamma_c$.  For all
\[
   N\ge \max\{C L|\gamma_c-t_0|,\ N_1(v)\},
\]
the center lies inside the detection window of Proposition~\ref{thm:alpha4}, and, by the assumed
uniformity, the factorial threshold
there is smaller than $v^2$.  Hence $\delta_N(t)<0$ and $t_N^*\ge t$.  Since this holds for every
$t<\Lambda_\Xi$, $\liminf_N t_N^*\ge\Lambda_\Xi$, while the opposite inequality was already
proved.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[conditional cascade form of RH]\label{cor:alpha-rh}
If Conjecture~\ref{thm:alpha5} holds, then, since $\Lambda_\Xi=\Lambda_{\mathrm{dBN}}/4$, RH is equivalent to
$\Lambda_\Xi\le0$~\cite{deBruijn,Newman} and $\Lambda_\Xi\ge0$ by Rodgers and
Tao~\cite{RodgersTao}, one has
\[
   \mathrm{RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \sup_N t_N^*\le0
   \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   \lim_N t_N^*=0 .
\]
\end{corollary}

In summary, the cascade formula (Conjecture~\ref{thm:alpha5}) does not prove RH; only the inequality
$t_N^*\le\Lambda_\Xi$ is proved.  The uniform detection and tail hypotheses remain open, and the
window hypothesis of Proposition~\ref{thm:alpha4} fails for large $N$ at fixed $t_0$.  If established, it gives a finite-section
characterization of the de Bruijn--Newman constant.  The open input is the same as before, in flow
coordinates: for $\zeta$, exclude a cascade accumulating at some $\Lambda_\Xi>0$. What is proved,
precisely, is isolated in the following components: principal-value bookkeeping in Lemma~\ref{lem:alpha2}
(under its summability hypotheses); the $(\log T)^2$ flow-counting bound of Lemma~\ref{lem:alpha-L2}, whose proof is only sketched (for
fixed $t>0$ the sharper bound is in Ki--Kim--Lee);
the conditional product-tail and derivative estimates of
Propositions~\ref{lem:alpha-L3}--\ref{lem:alpha-L4}.  The numerical experiments described below are
consistent with the quadratic law, the Blaschke rate, and the cascade formula in the tested regimes.

\begin{remark}[computability and validation]\label{rmk:cascade-validation}
Each $\delta_N(t)$ is a finite-section computation.  Numerical experiments, for which no reproducible
computation is included (the values below are reported as unverified), gave: the quadratic
pair mechanism with shape constant $0.75$ to $10^{-5}$; the off-line Blaschke rate $2.25545$
predicted versus $2.2556$ observed for Davenport--Heilbronn; and the cascade formula with ratio
$1.000$ through the tested range, with the finite-threshold correction visible only at the smallest
tested order.  The sine model, a stationary Calogero lattice, has an empty cascade.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[rate heuristic]\label{rmk:cascade-rate}
Under GUE statistics for $\zeta$ one expects heuristically a power law $|t_N^*|\asymp L^{-2}N^{-2/3}$,
the exponent $-2/3$ reflecting the repulsion exponent $\beta=2$.  An explicit constant is not derived
here.  In this sense the cascade observable relates the de Bruijn--Newman flow to local zero
statistics.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[formal critical-line symbol]\label{rmk:critical-symbol}
If the half-line shifts in $\mathcal P_\Lambda$ are replaced formally by full-line shifts, the Fourier
symbol is
\[
   2\sum_{n\ge2}\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}\cos(\theta\log n)
   =
   -2\Re\frac{\zeta'}{\zeta}\!\left(\half+i\theta\right)
\]
in the sense of formal Dirichlet series.  The series is not pointwise convergent on the critical line;
the half-line compression and the exponential depth $y>\half$ are precisely the regularization.  This
explains why the terminal operator sees the critical-line obstruction while remaining computable in
finite sections.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[terminal numerical observable]\label{rmk:terminal-numerics}
The finite-section numerical experiment is read as follows.  Compute
\[
   C_N(t_0):=\lambda_{\max}(T_N(t_0-iy))\log(|t_0|/2\pi),
   \qquad
   \tilde\delta_N(t_0):=1-\lambda_{\max}(T_N(t_0-iy)).
\]
For $\zeta$, the expected signature is $\tilde\delta_N>0$ with $\tilde\delta_N\to0$; for a
Davenport--Heilbronn falsifier, some finite section must cross $\lambda_{\max}>1$.  The observed
saturation is consistent with Proposition~\ref{thm:saturation} and with the absence of a fixed margin,
but it does not close the terminal inequality.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Scale Correspondence]\label{rmk:scale-correspondence}
Lemma~\ref{lem:prime-window} identifies jet order with prime scale: the order-$k$ jet at depth
$\epsilon_0$ probes prime powers with $\log n$ concentrated near $k/\epsilon_0$, i.e. the H5 window of
length $L\asymp k/\epsilon_0$.  Together with Theorems~\ref{thm:one-point-jets},
\ref{thm:det-serialization}, and~\ref{thm:live-H5}, this gives four equivalent serializations of the
same global quantifier: node Pick positivity, determinant positivity, window positivity, and one-point
jet positivity.  The fixed-$N$ band regime is covered by Theorem~\ref{thm:band-closure}; the terminal
one-point gauge has the effective Pascal--Laguerre floor of Lemma~\ref{lem:pascal-effective} and the
effective $\log\log$ range of Corollary~\ref{cor:loglog-effective}.  The open uniform H8 problem is now
exactly the no-margin condition $\delta_N\ge0$ for all $N$, equivalently the prime-scale quantifier as
$N\to\infty$.
\end{remark}



\subsection{Realization layer: source core, finite channels, and the measure bridge}\label{sec:shorting}

Throughout this subsection the following data are fixed for each cutoff $P$:
\textnormal{(i)} a finite-dimensional Hilbert space $\cH_P$ containing the orthonormal prime-cell
carriers $u_{p,k}$ ($p^k\le P$), a unit vector $e_0\in\cH_P$ and a Hermitian form $K_P$ on $\cH_P$ as
in Definition~\ref{def:pole-vector}, satisfying Hypothesis~(P) there;
\textnormal{(ii)} a self-adjoint operator $A_P^\circ$ on the primitive space $\cH_P^\circ$ of
Definition~\ref{def:pole-vector}, hence $\|(A_P^\circ-z)^{-1}\|\le|\Im z|^{-1}$;
\textnormal{(iii)} $K_P\succeq0$, the block $C_P$ of $K_P$ on $e_0^\perp$ is the inner product of
$\cH_P$ restricted to $e_0^\perp$ (the orthogonal direct-sum inner product of the prime cells), and
$\Pi_P^\circ$ is the orthogonal projection of $\cH_P$ onto $e_0^\perp$.
No such family is constructed in this paper; a candidate is the multiplication operator of a de Branges space $\mathcal H(E)$
attached to a canonical system \cite{deBranges} (see \S\ref{sec:relwork}), but its existence with the
properties below is an open hypothesis. The present layer uses only (i)--(iii); the $P$-uniform source
bound of Lemma~\ref{lem:sourcebound} is proved from them. All statements below are conditional on
(i)--(iii).

\begin{lemma}[Mertens pole growth]\label{lem:mertens-pole}
For $x\ge2$,
\[
   \sum_{n\le x}\frac{\Lam(n)}{n}=\log x+O(1),\qquad
   \sum_{n\le x}\frac{\Lam(n)\log n}{n}=\half(\log x)^2+O(\log x).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The identity $\log m=\sum_{d\mid m}\Lam(d)$ gives
\[
   \log\lfloor x\rfloor!=\sum_{n\le x}\Lam(n)\left\lfloor\frac{x}{n}\right\rfloor.
\]
Writing $\lfloor x/n\rfloor=x/n-\{x/n\}$ and using Stirling's formula together with Chebyshev's bound
$\sum_{n\le x}\Lam(n)=O(x)$ gives
\[
   x\sum_{n\le x}\frac{\Lam(n)}{n}=x\log x+O(x),
\]
hence the first estimate.  Abel summation applied to
$A(t)=\sum_{n\le t}\Lam(n)/n=\log t+O(1)$ gives
\[
   \sum_{n\le x}\frac{\Lam(n)\log n}{n}
   =A(x)\log x-\int_2^x\frac{A(t)}{t}\,dt
   =\half(\log x)^2+O(\log x).
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[pole vector and primitive complement]\label{def:pole-vector}
Let $e_0$ be a unit vector in the finite-dimensional Hilbert space $\cH_P$, and write
\[
   \cH_P=\C e_0\oplus e_0^\perp .
\]
Let $K_P$ be a Hermitian form on $\cH_P$, written in this splitting as
\[
   K_P=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_P&\beta_P^*\\ \beta_P&C_P\end{pmatrix},\qquad \alpha_P=[e_0,e_0]_P .
\]
\textbf{Hypothesis (P).} The pole entry satisfies
\[
   \alpha_P=\sum_{n\le P^2}\frac{\Lam(n)\log n}{n}+r_P,\qquad r_P=O(\log P).
\]
When $\alpha_P>0$, the primitive complement $\cH_P^\circ$ is $e_0^\perp$ equipped with the
Schur-shorted form $K_P^\circ=C_P-\beta_P\alpha_P^{-1}\beta_P^*$; under \textnormal{(iii)} this form is
positive semidefinite by Lemma~\ref{lem:shorting-monotone}, and $\cH_P^\circ$ denotes the Hilbert
space obtained by quotienting $e_0^\perp$ by its null space.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[rank-one pole isolation]\label{lem:pole-isolation}
Under Hypothesis~\textnormal{(P)},
\[
   [e_0,e_0]_P=\alpha_P=\half(\log P^2)^2+O(\log P);
\]
in particular $\alpha_P>0$ for all sufficiently large $P$.  For every $\phi\in\cS_a^\circ$ the
prime-cell mass $\sum_{p,k}(\log p)p^{-k/2}|\phi(k\log p)|^2$ is finite, so its truncations to
$p^k\le P$ are bounded independently of $P$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Hypothesis~(P) and Lemma~\ref{lem:mertens-pole} with $x=P^2$ give
\[
   \alpha_P=\sum_{n\le P^2}\frac{\Lam(n)\log n}{n}+O(\log P)
      =\half(\log P^2)^2+O(\log P).
\]
For $\phi\in\cS_a^\circ$, condition \textnormal{(S1)} of Definition~\ref{def:core} with $N=0$ gives
$|\phi(t)|\le Ce^{-a_\phi t}$, hence
\[
   \sum_{p,k}(\log p)p^{-k/2}|\phi(k\log p)|^2
   \le C^2\sum_{p,k}(\log p)p^{-k(1/2+2a_\phi)}<\infty
\]
because $\half+2a_\phi>\tfrac32>1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Schur shorting of the pole block preserves the negative index]\label{lem:short}
Let $K_P$ be a Hermitian form written as in Definition~\ref{def:pole-vector} with $\alpha_P>0$.  Then
\[
   K_P^\circ=C_P-\beta_P\alpha_P^{-1}\beta_P^*
\]
satisfies
\[
   \sqm(K_P)=\sqm(\alpha_P)+\sqm(K_P^\circ)=\sqm(K_P^\circ).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
A direct multiplication gives the congruence
\[
   \begin{pmatrix}I&-\alpha_P^{-1}\beta_P^*\\0&I\end{pmatrix}^*
   K_P
   \begin{pmatrix}I&-\alpha_P^{-1}\beta_P^*\\0&I\end{pmatrix}
   =
   \begin{pmatrix}\alpha_P&0\\0&K_P^\circ\end{pmatrix},
\]
and Sylvester's law of inertia \cite{HornJohnson} gives the additivity of negative indices.  Since
$\alpha_P>0$, it contributes no negative square.  Under Hypothesis~(P), $\alpha_P>0$ for all
sufficiently large $P$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:pole-isolation}.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[finite source embedding]\label{def:source-embedding}
For a finite cutoff $P$ and $\phi\in\cS_a^\circ$, define the unshorted primitive source vector
$\widetilde\iota_P\phi\in\cH_P$ by its cell coordinates
\[
   (\widetilde\iota_P\phi)_{p,k}
   :=
   \bigl((\log p)p^{-k/2}\bigr)^{1/2}\phi(k\log p),
   \qquad p^k\le P,
\]
that is,
\[
   \widetilde\iota_P\phi
   =
   \sum_{p^k\le P}
   \bigl((\log p)p^{-k/2}\bigr)^{1/2}\phi(k\log p)\,u_{p,k}.
\]
The primitive source vector used in the resolvent compression is the projection
\[
   \iota_P\phi:=\Pi_P^\circ \widetilde\iota_P\phi\in\cH_P^\circ.
\]
For $F=\operatorname{span}\{\phi_1,\ldots,\phi_m\}$, the source map is
\[
   \Phi_P^F(c_1,\ldots,c_m)=\sum_{\alpha=1}^m c_\alpha\,\iota_P\phi_\alpha.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[source embedding norm identity]\label{lem:source-embedding-norm}
Before Schur shorting,
\[
   \|\widetilde\iota_P\phi\|^2
   =
   \sum_{p^k\le P}(\log p)p^{-k/2}|\phi(k\log p)|^2.
\]
After shorting, for $P$ large enough that $\alpha_P>0$,
\[
   \|\iota_P\phi\|_{\cH_P^\circ}^2
   \le
   \|\widetilde\iota_P\phi\|^2.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The first identity holds because the carriers $u_{p,k}$ are orthonormal.  For the second, write
$x=\widetilde\iota_P\phi$.  By Lemma~\ref{lem:shorting-monotone} and hypothesis \textnormal{(iii)},
\[
   \|\iota_P\phi\|_{K_P^\circ}^2
   =\langle K_P^\circ\Pi^\circ_P x,\Pi^\circ_P x\rangle
   \le\langle C_P\Pi^\circ_P x,\Pi^\circ_P x\rangle
   =\|\Pi^\circ_P x\|^2
   \le\|x\|^2,
\]
the last step because $\Pi_P^\circ$ is an orthogonal projection.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[compact logarithmic tests belong to the source core]\label{lem:compact-core}
Every $\phi\in C_c^\infty(0,\infty)$ belongs to $\cS_a^\circ$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Assume $\operatorname{supp}\phi\subset[\alpha,\beta]\subset(0,\infty)$.  Fix any
$a_\phi>\half$.  For each $N$,
\[
   e^{a_\phi t}(1+t)^N|\phi(t)|
\]
is continuous on the compact interval $[\alpha,\beta]$ and is zero outside it, hence has a finite
supremum $C_N$.  Therefore
\[
   |\phi(t)|\le C_Ne^{-a_\phi t}(1+t)^{-N}.
\]

For the strip estimate, write $\operatorname{supp}\phi\subset[0,T]$ and fix
$\eps_\phi\in(0,a_\phi-\half)$.  If $|\Im w|\le\half+\eps_\phi/2$, integration by parts gives, for
every $N$,
\[
   |\ell_w(\phi)|
   =
   \left|\int_0^T\phi(t)e^{-iwt}\,dt\right|
   \le
   C_{N,\phi}(1+|\Re w|)^{-N},
\]
because all boundary terms vanish for a compactly supported smooth test and
${d^N}(e^{-iwt})/{dt^N}=(-iw)^Ne^{-iwt}$, while
$|e^{-iwt}|\le e^{(\half+\eps_\phi/2)T}$ on the enlarged closed substrip.  Thus
$\phi\in\cS_a^\circ$.  (This is the closing statement of Definition~\ref{def:core}.)
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[shorting does not increase primitive source mass]\label{lem:shorting-monotone}
Let $K_P$ be a Hermitian form written as in Definition~\ref{def:pole-vector} with $\alpha_P>0$.  For
every primitive vector $x\in e_0^\perp$,
\[
   \langle K_P^\circ x,x\rangle
   =
   \langle C_Px,x\rangle-\alpha_P^{-1}\|\beta_P^*x\|^2
   \le \langle C_Px,x\rangle.
\]
If moreover $K_P\succeq0$, then $0\preceq K_P^\circ\preceq C_P$ on $e_0^\perp$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $\alpha_P>0$, the term $\alpha_P^{-1}\|\beta_P^*x\|^2$ is nonnegative, and the displayed
inequality is immediate.  If $K_P\succeq0$, the congruence in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:short} shows
that $\operatorname{diag}(\alpha_P,K_P^\circ)\succeq0$, hence $K_P^\circ\succeq0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[uniform finite-channel source bound]\label{lem:sourcebound}
For every $\phi\in\cS_a^\circ$,
\[
   \sup_{P\ \text{large}}\|\iota_P\phi\|_{\cH_P^\circ}<\infty,
\]
the supremum being over the cutoffs with $\alpha_P>0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:source-embedding-norm} and condition \textnormal{(S1)} with $N=0$,
\[
   \|\iota_P\phi\|_{\cH_P^\circ}^2\le
   \sum_{p^k\le P}(\log p)p^{-k/2}|\phi(k\log p)|^2
   \le
   C^2\sum_{p,k}(\log p)p^{-k(1/2+2a_\phi)}.
\]
The series converges because $1/2+2a_\phi>1$, and the bound is independent of $P$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[finite-channel normal family]\label{cor:normal}
For every finite source plane $F\subset\cS_a^\circ$, the functions
\[
   M_P^F(z)=(\Phi_P^F)^*(A_P^\circ-z)^{-1}\Phi_P^F
\]
satisfy the uniform bound required in Theorem~\ref{thm:vitali}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Combine self-adjointness of $A_P^\circ$ with Lemma~\ref{lem:sourcebound}:
\[
   \|M_P^F(z)\|\le \|\Phi_P^F\|^2|\Im z|^{-1}\le C_F|\Im z|^{-1}.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[subsequential spectral-measure compactness]\label{lem:helly-cauchy}
Fix a finite source plane $F\subset\cS_a^\circ$.  For every sequence of cutoffs $P_j$ there is a
subsequence $P_{j_\nu}$ and a positive matrix measure $\Sigma^F$ on $\R$, with finite total mass, such
that
\[
   M_{P_{j_\nu}}^F(z)
   \longrightarrow
   \int_\R\frac{d\Sigma^F(t)}{t-z}
\]
locally uniformly on $\C\setminus\R$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $E_P$ be the spectral measure of the self-adjoint operator $A_P^\circ$.  Define the positive
matrix-valued measure
\[
   \Sigma_P^F(B):=(\Phi_P^F)^*E_P(B)\Phi_P^F,\qquad B\subset\R\ \text{Borel}.
\]
The spectral theorem gives
\[
   M_P^F(z)=\int_\R\frac{d\Sigma_P^F(t)}{t-z}.
\]
Corollary~\ref{cor:normal} and Lemma~\ref{lem:sourcebound} give a $P$-independent bound on the total
mass:
\[
   \Sigma_P^F(\R)=(\Phi_P^F)^*\Phi_P^F\le C_F I_F .
\]
For each pair of basis vectors $e_\alpha,e_\beta$ in $F$, the scalar measures
$\langle\Sigma_P^F e_\beta,e_\alpha\rangle$ therefore have uniformly bounded total variation.  By
Banach--Alaoglu, equivalently the Helly selection theorem for finite measures on the locally compact space
$\R$, a diagonal subsequence converges weak-* against $C_0(\R)$ for all finitely many matrix entries.  The
limit is a positive matrix measure $\Sigma^F$ because positivity is preserved under weak-* limits:
for every vector $c$, the scalar measures
$\langle\Sigma_{P_{j_\nu}}^F c,c\rangle$ are positive and converge to
$\langle\Sigma^F c,c\rangle$.

For each $z\in\C\setminus\R$, the function $t\mapsto(t-z)^{-1}$ belongs to $C_0(\R)$, hence the displayed
Cauchy transforms converge pointwise.  On a compact $K\subset\C\setminus\R$, the kernels
$(t-z)^{-1}$ are uniformly bounded and uniformly Lipschitz in $z$ by constants depending only on
$\operatorname{dist}(K,\R)$.  A finite-net argument upgrades pointwise convergence to uniform convergence
on $K$.  Thus convergence is locally uniform on $\C\setminus\R$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[moment criterion for operator-tower convergence]\label{thm:moment-arp}
Fix a point $z_\bullet\in U_-$.  For a finite source plane $F\subset\cS_a^\circ$, the convergence
\[
   M_P^F(z)\longrightarrow \GXi^F(z)
\]
locally uniformly on $U_-$ is equivalent to the convergence of all resolvent moments at $z_\bullet$:
\[
   (\Phi_P^F)^*(A_P^\circ-z_\bullet)^{-k-1}\Phi_P^F
   \longrightarrow
   \frac{1}{k!}\,\partial_z^k\GXi^F(z_\bullet),
   \qquad k=0,1,2,\ldots .
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $M_P^F\to\GXi^F$ locally uniformly on $U_-$, Cauchy's integral formula gives convergence of all
derivatives at $z_\bullet$.  Since
\[
   \partial_z^k(A_P^\circ-z)^{-1}=k!(A_P^\circ-z)^{-k-1},
\]
the displayed moment convergence follows.

Conversely, assume all displayed moments converge.  Corollary~\ref{cor:normal} makes
$\{M_P^F\}_P$ a normal family on $\Omega_-$.  Let $H$ be any subsequential locally uniform limit on
$\Omega_-$.  Cauchy's formula again gives
\[
   \frac{1}{k!}\,\partial_z^kH(z_\bullet)
   =
   \lim_P(\Phi_P^F)^*(A_P^\circ-z_\bullet)^{-k-1}\Phi_P^F
   =
   \frac{1}{k!}\,\partial_z^k\GXi^F(z_\bullet).
\]
The point $z_\bullet$ lies in $U_-$, where $\Xi$ has no zeros because $\Re s>1$ and the Euler product for
$\zeta$ is nonzero there; hence $\GXi^F$ is holomorphic near $z_\bullet$.  The Taylor series of $H$ and
$\GXi^F$ therefore agree in a disk around $z_\bullet$.  By the identity theorem they agree on the
connected component of $U_-$, and then on $\Omega_-$ after removing any apparent poles of the meromorphic
target, because $H$ is holomorphic on all of $\Omega_-$.  Every subsequential limit is the same; normality
then implies the full sequence converges locally uniformly to $\GXi^F$ on $U_-$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[conditional measure-form bridge]\label{thm:measure-bridge}
Assume \textnormal{(i)--(iii)} and that $M_P^F\to\GXi^F$ on $U_-$ for every finite source plane $F$.
Then every subsequential spectral limit of Lemma~\ref{lem:helly-cauchy} has Cauchy transform equal to
$\GXi^F$ on $\Omega_-$, and RH holds.  In particular the convergence hypothesis is at least as strong
as RH; it is a conditional reformulation, not an unconditional input.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix $F$ and a cutoff sequence.  Lemma~\ref{lem:helly-cauchy} gives a subsequence whose transforms converge
locally uniformly on $\C\setminus\R$ to a Cauchy transform
\[
   H^F(z)=\int_\R\frac{d\Sigma^F(t)}{t-z}.
\]
The function $H^F$ is holomorphic on $\Omega_-$.  The tower convergence identifies the same
subsequential limit with $\GXi^F$ on $U_-$.  Hence $H^F=\GXi^F$ on $U_-$ and, by the identity theorem,
on all of $\Omega_-$ after
removing the poles of the meromorphic target.  Lemma~\ref{lem:residue} detects every non-real zero by
some finite channel, so no such pole can exist in $\Omega_-$.  The evenness and real symmetry of $\Xi$
reflect the conclusion to the upper half-plane by Lemma~\ref{lem:xi-basic}(ii)--(iii):
$\Xi(-z)=\Xi(z)$ gives $M_\Xi(-z)=-M_\Xi(z)$ and $\Xi(\bar z)=\overline{\Xi(z)}$ gives
$M_\Xi(\bar z)=\overline{M_\Xi(z)}$ away from poles.  Therefore all zeros of $\Xi$ lie on the real
$z$-axis, which is RH.
\end{proof}


\subsection{Step 6: positive Cauchy transforms have positive Pick kernels}

The following classical fact (see \cite{Akhiezer}) is used.

\begin{lemma}[Cauchy transforms have positive Pick kernels]\label{lem:cauchy-pick}
Let $\Sigma$ be a positive finite matrix measure on $\R$ and
\[
   g(z)=\int_\R\frac{d\Sigma(t)}{t-z}.
\]
Then, for all $z,w\in\C\setminus\R$ with $z\ne\bar w$ \textnormal{(in particular for all $z,w$ in the
same half-plane)}, the integral defining $g$ converges absolutely since
$|t-z|\ge|\Im z|$ and $\Sigma(\R)<\infty$, and
\[
   \frac{g(z)-g(w)^*}{z-\bar w}
   =
   \int_\R
      \frac{d\Sigma(t)}{(t-z)(t-\bar w)}
\]
as a positive matrix kernel.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $t$ is real,
\[
   \frac{1}{t-z}-\frac{1}{t-\bar w}
   =
   \frac{z-\bar w}{(t-z)(t-\bar w)}.
\]
Dividing by $z-\bar w$ gives the displayed identity.  If $z_1,\ldots,z_N$ are
nodes and $c_1,\ldots,c_N$ are vectors in the channel space, then
\[
   \sum_{i,j}
   c_i^*
   \left[
      \int_\R\frac{d\Sigma(t)}{(t-z_i)(t-\bar z_j)}
   \right]
   c_j
   =
   \int_\R
      \left(\sum_i\frac{c_i}{t-\bar z_i}\right)^*
      d\Sigma(t)
      \left(\sum_j\frac{c_j}{t-\bar z_j}\right)
   \ge0.
\]
Thus the block Pick matrices are positive semidefinite.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Step 7: tower-ARP implies ARP-P}

Tower-ARP is a sufficient condition for ARP-P; it is not used elsewhere in the chain.

\begin{definition}[positive tower; tower-ARP]\label{def:ARP}
A \emph{positive tower} for the finite channel $F$ is a sequence $\{\Sigma_P^F\}_P$ of positive
finite matrix measures on $\R$ with $\sup_P\Sigma_P^F(\R)<\infty$, whose Cauchy transforms
\[
   M_P^F(z)=\int_\R\frac{d\Sigma_P^F(t)}{t-z}
\]
converge pointwise on $U_-$ to $g^F(z)$.  The channel data satisfy \emph{tower-ARP} if every finite
source plane $F\subset\cS_a^\circ$ admits a positive tower.  Tower-ARP is a convergence property, not a
per-cutoff identity.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[operator-theoretic towers]\label{rmk:operator-towers}
Any family of self-adjoint operators $A_P$ on Hilbert spaces $\cH_P$ with $P$-uniformly bounded source
maps $\Phi_P^F:F\to\cH_P$ produces the measures of Definition~\ref{def:ARP} through the spectral
compressions $\Sigma_P^F(B)=(\Phi_P^F)^*E_P(B)\Phi_P^F$, for which
$M_P^F(z)=(\Phi_P^F)^*(A_P-z)^{-1}\Phi_P^F$.  The realization layer of Section~\ref{sec:shorting}
records the source bounds such a family would satisfy under its hypotheses (i)--(iii); no such family is
constructed in this paper.
The chain uses nothing beyond Definition~\ref{def:ARP}.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[tower-ARP implies ARP-P]\label{thm:tower-implies-pick}
If the channel data satisfy tower-ARP, Definition~\ref{def:ARP}, then ARP-P holds.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Each $M_P^F$ is, by definition, the Cauchy transform of a positive finite matrix measure.  Hence
Lemma~\ref{lem:cauchy-pick} gives
\[
   \mathsf P[M_P^F;z_1,\ldots,z_N]\succeq0
\]
for every finite node set in $U_-$.  Tower-ARP gives $M_P^F(z_i)\to g^F(z_i)$ at each node.  Since the
cone of positive semidefinite matrices is closed, the limit matrix
$\mathsf P[g^F;z_1,\ldots,z_N]$ is positive semidefinite.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Step 8: ARP-P gives Nevanlinna--Pick interpolants}

Fix a finite channel $F$ and a finite node set $z_1,\ldots,z_N\subset U_-$.  ARP-P says exactly that the
matrix Pick condition for the lower half-plane is satisfied.  The classical matrix-valued
Nevanlinna--Pick theorem therefore supplies a holomorphic matrix function $F_N$ on $\Omega_-$ such that
\[
   F_N(z_j)=g^F(z_j),\qquad j=1,\ldots,N,
\]
and
\[
   \frac{F_N(z)-F_N(w)^*}{z-\bar w}\succeq0.
\]
The hypotheses of the classical theorem are precisely the finite node condition, the target matrices
$g^F(z_j)$, and the positive semidefiniteness of the displayed block Pick matrix; these are exactly
provided by ARP-P, Definition~\ref{def:ARP-P}.

The classical theorem is used in its degenerate form: positive \emph{semi}definiteness of the block
Pick matrix, singular cases included, is necessary and sufficient for solvability in the matrix Pick
class.  The exact statement used, in the half-plane normalization of this paper and with the Cayley
congruence written out, is displayed as Proposition~\ref{prop:np-degenerate} below; its disk core is the
commutant-lifting Schur--Pick theorem \cite{FoiasFrazho}.  ARP-P supplies
exactly this semidefiniteness, so no strict positivity is assumed anywhere in the bridge.

\begin{lemma}[pinned normality of the matrix Pick class]\label{lem:pinned-normality}
Let $\{F_n\}$ be holomorphic $M_m(\C)$-valued functions on $\Omega_-$ with $\Im F_n\preceq0$, and
suppose $F_n(z_1)=W_1$ for all $n$, at some fixed $z_1\in\Omega_-$.  Then a subsequence converges
locally uniformly on $\Omega_-\setminus Z$, for a discrete set $Z$, to a function which extends
holomorphically to all of $\Omega_-$ as a matrix Pick-class function $F$; moreover
$F(z)=\lim_kF_{n_k}(z)$ whenever the right side eventually stabilizes or converges.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Cayley step.}  For $W$ with $\Im W\preceq0$ one computes, for every vector $x$,
\[
   \|(W-iI)x\|^2=\|Wx\|^2+\|x\|^2-2\Im\langle Wx,x\rangle\ge\|x\|^2,
   \qquad
   \|(W+iI)x\|^2\le\|(W-iI)x\|^2 .
\]
Thus $W-iI$ is invertible with $\|(W-iI)^{-1}\|\le1$ and
$S:=(W+iI)(W-iI)^{-1}$ is a contraction, with the exact identities
\[
   S-I=2i(W-iI)^{-1},\qquad W=i(S+I)(S-I)^{-1}.
\]
Thus $S_n(z):=(F_n(z)+iI)(F_n(z)-iI)^{-1}$ is a holomorphic contraction-valued family.

\emph{Montel and pinning.}  The entries of $S_n$ are bounded by $1$, so a diagonal subsequence
converges locally uniformly to a holomorphic contraction-valued $S_\infty$.  At the pinned node,
$S_\infty(z_1)-I=2i(W_1-iI)^{-1}$ is invertible.  Hence
$\varphi:=\det(S_\infty-I)$ is holomorphic and not identically zero, and
$Z:=\{\varphi=0\}$ is discrete in $\Omega_-$.

\emph{Back-transform off $Z$.}  On compacts of $\Omega_-\setminus Z$, $|\varphi|$ is bounded below,
so $(S_{n_k}-I)^{-1}\to(S_\infty-I)^{-1}$ uniformly and
$F_{n_k}\to F:=i(S_\infty+I)(S_\infty-I)^{-1}$ locally uniformly; $F$ is holomorphic with
$\Im F\preceq0$ on $\Omega_-\setminus Z$ as a limit of such.

\emph{Removability across $Z$.}  Fix $\zeta_0\in Z$ and a unit vector $c$, and set
$f:=c^*Fc$ on a punctured disk around $\zeta_0$ inside $\Omega_-\setminus Z$.  Since
$\Im f\le0$, $|f-i|\ge1$, so $h:=(f-i)^{-1}$ is holomorphic and bounded by $1$, hence extends across
$\zeta_0$ by Riemann's theorem.  If $h(\zeta_0)=0$, then $f=i+1/h$ would have a pole at $\zeta_0$;
near a pole the values of $f$ cover all sufficiently large moduli in every direction of the plane, in
particular values with positive imaginary part, contradicting $\Im f\le0$.  Hence
$h(\zeta_0)\ne0$ and $f$ extends holomorphically.  Applying this to
$c\in\{e_j,\,e_k,\,e_j+e_k,\,e_j+ie_k\}$ and polarizing recovers every entry of $F$, which therefore
extends holomorphically across $Z$; the extension is Pick-class by continuity.

\emph{Node values.}  If $F_{n_k}(w)=W$ for all large $k$ at some $w\in\Omega_-$, then when
$w\notin Z$ this passes to the limit directly.  When $w\in Z$, choose a circle around $w$ inside
$\Omega_-\setminus Z$ and use the Cauchy integral:
\[
   F(w)=\frac1{2\pi i}\oint\frac{F(\zeta)}{\zeta-w}\,d\zeta
   =\lim_k\frac1{2\pi i}\oint\frac{F_{n_k}(\zeta)}{\zeta-w}\,d\zeta=W,
\]
since each $F_{n_k}$ is holomorphic at $w$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[degenerate matrix Nevanlinna--Pick theorem, half-plane form]\label{prop:np-degenerate}
Let $z_1,\ldots,z_N\in\Omega_-$ be pairwise distinct and let $W_1,\ldots,W_N\in M_m(\C)$.  There exists
a holomorphic $F:\Omega_-\to M_m(\C)$ with $\Im F\preceq0$ and $F(z_j)=W_j$ for all $j$ if and only if
the block Pick matrix
\[
   \left[\frac{W_i-W_j^*}{z_i-\bar z_j}\right]_{i,j=1}^N
\]
is positive \emph{semi}definite; singular (degenerate) Pick matrices are included.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Reduction to the disk.}  The M\"obius map $\zeta(z)=(z+i)/(z-i)$ takes $\Omega_-$ biholomorphically
onto the unit disk $\mathbb D$, and the node images $\zeta_j:=\zeta(z_j)$ are pairwise distinct.  If the Pick matrix is
positive semidefinite, its diagonal blocks give $(W_j-W_j^*)/(2i\Im z_j)\succeq0$ with $\Im z_j<0$, hence
$\Im W_j\preceq0$; by the Cayley computation in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:pinned-normality},
$W_j-iI$ is then invertible and $S_j:=(W_j+iI)(W_j-iI)^{-1}$ is a contraction, with
$S_j-I=2i(W_j-iI)^{-1}$ invertible.  The algebraic identities
\[
   I-S_iS_j^*
   =
   2i\,(W_i-iI)^{-1}(W_i-W_j^*)\,(W_j^*+iI)^{-1},
   \qquad
   1-\zeta_i\bar\zeta_j
   =
   \frac{2i\,(z_i-\bar z_j)}{(z_i-i)(\bar z_j+i)},
\]
checked by direct expansion (the factors $(W\pm iI)$ and their inverses commute), give the exact block
congruence
\[
   \left[\frac{I-S_iS_j^*}{1-\zeta_i\bar\zeta_j}\right]
   =
   D\left[\frac{W_i-W_j^*}{z_i-\bar z_j}\right]D^*,
